Table 1
Basic Algebra for Calculus, Business, and Economics
1. Q: Why does a calculus course begin with a review of basic algebra?
A: Show answer
Calculus builds on algebraic operations, functions, equations, and graphs. Students use these skills when simplifying expressions, evaluating functions, finding domains, solving equations, and interpreting mathematical models. In business and economics, algebra also expresses relationships among price, quantity, cost, revenue, and profit. If an algebraic step is incorrect, later calculus work can be wrong even when the differentiation or integration method is understood. Chapter 1 therefore reviews number systems, properties of real numbers, exponents, fractions, order of operations, polynomials, factoring, functions, and graphs before introducing calculus techniques in later chapters.
Study instruction:
- List the algebra skills you use when solving a function problem and identify which ones need review before studying limits and derivatives.
Key point:
- Algebra is the working language used throughout calculus and its applications.
- Strong algebra skills reduce avoidable errors in later calculus work.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 1
- Routledge: Calculus for Business and Economics
2. Q: What is a set, and how is set-builder notation used in algebra?
A: Show answer
A set is a well-defined collection of objects. The objects are called elements, and capital letters are commonly used to name sets. A set can be written by listing its elements, such as A = {1, 2, 3}, or by describing a rule that determines membership. In set-builder notation, B = {x such that x satisfies a stated condition} describes all values of x that meet the condition. The vertical bar in conventional set-builder notation is read as “such that.” The empty set, written ∅, contains no elements. Set notation provides a precise language for describing number systems, domains, ranges, and intervals used in calculus.
Study instruction:
- Write one set by listing its elements and another using a rule, then identify whether a given number belongs to each set.
Key point:
- A set is a clearly defined collection of elements.
- Set-builder notation describes membership through a mathematical condition.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1, Basic Algebra
- OpenStax: Real Numbers and Algebra Essentials
3. Q: What are the natural, integer, rational, and real number sets?
A: Show answer
The natural numbers are the counting numbers 1, 2, 3, and so on, although some mathematical texts include zero in this set. The integers include negative whole numbers, zero, and positive whole numbers. Rational numbers are values that can be expressed as a ratio of two integers with a nonzero denominator; their decimal expansions terminate or repeat. The real numbers include both rational and irrational values and are represented by the real number line. The chapter uses N, Z, Q, and R for these sets. Being able to classify numbers helps when deciding which inputs are allowed in a function or whether an algebraic result is meaningful in an application.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples such as −4, 0, 3/5, 2.75, and the square root of 2, noting the convention used for natural numbers.
Key point:
- The number systems expand from counting numbers to integers, rationals, and all real numbers.
- Always check whether a text includes zero in its definition of natural numbers.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Real Numbers: Algebra Essentials
4. Q: How are rational numbers different from irrational numbers?
A: Show answer
A rational number can be written as a fraction m/n, where m and n are integers and n is not zero. Its decimal representation either terminates or repeats a pattern. An irrational number cannot be expressed as a ratio of two integers, and its decimal expansion neither terminates nor repeats. Rational and irrational numbers together make up the real number system. For example, 3/4 and 0.125 are rational, while the square root of 2 is irrational. This distinction matters when describing exact values and when interpreting calculator approximations: a displayed decimal may be a rounded representation of an exact irrational value.
Study instruction:
- Convert several terminating and repeating decimals to fractions, then explain why a non-repeating, non-terminating decimal is not rational.
Key point:
- Rational numbers are ratios of integers with nonzero denominators.
- Irrational numbers are real numbers that cannot be expressed as such ratios.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Real Numbers: Algebra Essentials
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
5. Q: What is the difference between an open interval and a closed interval?
A: Show answer
An interval describes a continuous set of real numbers between two endpoints. A closed interval [a, b] includes both endpoints, while an open interval (a, b) excludes both. A half-open interval such as [a, b) includes a but excludes b, and (a, b] does the opposite. The bracket tells whether the endpoint belongs to the set; the parenthesis tells that it does not. Intervals are useful for expressing function domains and ranges, such as a production quantity restricted to a nonnegative range or a variable constrained between two limits. Infinite endpoints always use parentheses because infinity is not a real number that can be included.
Study instruction:
- Translate four interval notations into inequalities and draw each interval on a number line, marking included and excluded endpoints correctly.
Key point:
- Brackets include endpoints; parentheses exclude them.
- Interval notation expresses continuous restrictions on real-valued variables.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Introduction to Functions
6. Q: What are the commutative and associative properties of real numbers?
A: Show answer
The commutative property says that changing the order of two terms does not change their sum or product: a + b = b + a and ab = ba. The associative property says that changing the grouping of terms does not change a sum or product: (a + b) + c = a + (b + c), and (ab)c = a(bc). These properties apply to addition and multiplication, not generally to subtraction or division. They allow expressions to be rearranged to make calculations easier. For example, grouping 25 and 75 before adding another amount can simplify mental arithmetic, while regrouping factors can make an algebraic expression easier to simplify.
Study instruction:
- Create one numerical and one algebraic example of each property, then identify an operation for which the property does not generally hold.
Key point:
- Commutativity changes order; associativity changes grouping.
- Do not apply these properties indiscriminately to subtraction or division.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Properties of Real Numbers
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
7. Q: What are the additive identity, multiplicative identity, and inverse properties?
A: Show answer
Zero is the additive identity because adding it to a number leaves the number unchanged: x + 0 = x. One is the multiplicative identity because multiplying by it also leaves the number unchanged: x times 1 = x. The additive inverse of x is −x because x + (−x) = 0. For a nonzero x, its multiplicative inverse is 1/x because x times 1/x = 1. These properties explain why subtracting a number is equivalent to adding its negative and why dividing by a nonzero number is equivalent to multiplying by its reciprocal. The nonzero restriction is essential for multiplicative inverses because division by zero is undefined.
Study instruction:
- For a selection of positive, negative, and fractional values, write each additive inverse and multiplicative inverse and verify the result.
Key point:
- Zero and one are the additive and multiplicative identity elements.
- Every real number has an additive inverse, but only nonzero real numbers have multiplicative inverses.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Properties of Real Numbers
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
8. Q: What does the distributive property state, and why is it important?
A: Show answer
The distributive property states that multiplication distributes over addition or subtraction: a(b + c) = ab + ac and a(b − c) = ab − ac. It allows a factor outside parentheses to be applied to every term inside. The reverse operation is factoring, where a common factor is taken out of several terms. Many polynomial simplifications, expansions, and factorizations depend on this property. For example, 7(2x − 3) becomes 14x − 21, while 14x − 21 can be factored as 7(2x − 3). Checking by distributing the factor again is a useful way to verify a factorization.
Study instruction:
- Expand three expressions using distribution and factor the results back to their original form to check the equivalence.
Key point:
- Distribution expands a product over a sum; factoring reverses the process.
- Checking by expanding is a reliable way to verify algebraic transformations.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Greatest Common Factor and Factoring
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
9. Q: What are the main rules of exponents used to simplify algebraic expressions?
A: Show answer
The product rule adds exponents when multiplying powers with the same base: x^m times x^n = x^(m+n). The quotient rule subtracts exponents when dividing powers with the same nonzero base: x^m divided by x^n = x^(m−n). The power rule multiplies exponents when raising a power to another power: (x^m)^n = x^(mn). A power of a product distributes to each factor, and a power of a quotient applies to the numerator and denominator when the expressions are defined. A negative exponent represents a reciprocal, while a zero exponent gives one for a nonzero base. These rules simplify expressions without expanding every repeated factor.
Study instruction:
- Simplify a set of expressions using one exponent rule at a time and state the nonzero or other domain restrictions required by each step.
Key point:
- Exponent laws depend on the bases and the operations being performed.
- Keep domain restrictions visible when dividing or using negative and fractional powers.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Properties of Exponents
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
10. Q: What do negative, zero, and fractional exponents mean?
A: Show answer
A negative exponent indicates a reciprocal: x^(−n) = 1/x^n for x not equal to zero. A zero exponent gives x^0 = 1 for a nonzero base, based on the quotient rule and the identity property. Fractional exponents connect powers with roots: x^(1/n) represents an nth root where that root is defined, and x^(m/n) represents the nth root of x^m under the usual real-number restrictions. The chapter presents a convention assigning 0^0 the value 1, but this should not be treated as a universal rule: in many limit problems 0^0 is an indeterminate form, and different mathematical contexts define it differently. Always follow the convention appropriate to the problem.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite negative and fractional powers as reciprocals or radicals and identify where the real-valued expression is defined.
Key point:
- Negative powers mean reciprocals; fractional powers are related to roots.
- The expression 0^0 is context-dependent and should not be used as a universal exponent rule.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Properties of Exponents
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
11. Q: How are fractions added and subtracted when their denominators are different?
A: Show answer
Fractions must have a common denominator before they can be added or subtracted. A common denominator can be obtained by using the product of the denominators, although the least common denominator may produce simpler arithmetic. Each fraction is rewritten as an equivalent fraction with that denominator, then the numerators are added or subtracted while the common denominator remains unchanged. For example, 4/5 + 8/3 can be rewritten with denominator 15 as 12/15 + 40/15, giving 52/15. The final result can be converted to a mixed number if that format is useful, but an improper fraction is already a valid exact answer.
Study instruction:
- Solve three fraction sums and differences using both a product denominator and the least common denominator, then compare the amount of simplification required.
Key point:
- Add or subtract numerators only after creating a common denominator.
- Equivalent fractions preserve value while allowing a common denominator.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Fractions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
12. Q: How are fractions multiplied and divided?
A: Show answer
To multiply fractions, multiply the numerators together and multiply the denominators together, then simplify if possible. To divide by a fraction, multiply the first fraction by the reciprocal of the second. For example, 2/3 divided by 5/7 becomes 2/3 multiplied by 7/5, which equals 14/15. The divisor must not be zero because division by zero is undefined. Negative signs should be handled consistently, and common factors can be cancelled only when they are factors of the numerator and denominator of the same product. These rules are particularly useful when simplifying rational expressions and formulas in calculus.
Study instruction:
- Convert each fraction division problem into multiplication by the reciprocal and verify the result by multiplying it by the original divisor.
Key point:
- Multiply numerator by numerator and denominator by denominator.
- Dividing by a nonzero fraction means multiplying by its reciprocal.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Fractions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
13. Q: What is the correct order of operations, and how should equal-priority operations be handled?
A: Show answer
The standard order is grouping symbols first, then exponents and roots, then multiplication and division, and finally addition and subtraction. Multiplication and division have equal priority and are evaluated from left to right. Addition and subtraction also have equal priority and are evaluated from left to right. PEMDAS is a memory aid, not a rule that multiplication must always occur before division or addition before subtraction. For example, in 12 divided by 4 multiplied by 3, the division is performed first because it appears first from the left, then the result is multiplied by 3. Grouping symbols, fraction bars, and radicals can change which operations are performed first.
Study instruction:
- Work through a mixed expression line by line, explicitly marking each operation in the order it is performed.
Key point:
- Multiplication and division share priority; addition and subtraction share priority.
- Evaluate equal-priority operations from left to right.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Order of Operations
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
14. Q: What is the difference between a mathematical expression and an equation?
A: Show answer
An expression is a combination of numbers, variables, and operations that represents a quantity but does not state that it equals or relates to another quantity. An equation includes a relation symbol, most commonly an equals sign, and states that two expressions have the specified relationship. An expression such as 2(x + 3) is simplified, while an equation such as 2(x + 3) = 16 is solved for values of x that make the equality true. Inequalities also express relationships and are solved rather than merely simplified. Distinguishing these tasks prevents a common error: treating an expression as though it has a solution when no equation has been stated.
Study instruction:
- Classify a list of algebraic statements as expressions or equations and explain whether each should be simplified or solved.
Key point:
- Expressions represent quantities; equations assert relationships between quantities.
- Simplifying and solving are different algebraic tasks.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Algebraic Expressions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
15. Q: What is a polynomial, and what restrictions apply to its exponents?
A: Show answer
A polynomial is an expression built from constants and variables using addition, subtraction, and multiplication, with variables raised only to nonnegative integer powers. A polynomial may have one term or several terms. Expressions such as 3x^2 − 2x + 7 and 4x^3y − 5xy^2 are polynomials, while 1/x and the square root of x are not polynomials in x because they involve negative or fractional powers. A polynomial expression can be simplified, evaluated, factored, or used to define a polynomial function. Recognizing the form helps determine which algebraic techniques apply and which restrictions may arise from a later quotient or root operation.
Study instruction:
- Classify several expressions as polynomial or non-polynomial and identify the term or exponent that determines the classification.
Key point:
- Polynomial variables have nonnegative integer exponents.
- Negative or fractional variable exponents take an expression outside the polynomial definition.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Polynomial Concepts
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
16. Q: What are terms, coefficients, and like terms in a polynomial?
A: Show answer
Terms are the parts of a polynomial separated by addition or subtraction. A coefficient is the numerical factor multiplying the variable part of a term. Like terms contain exactly the same variables raised to the same powers, even if their coefficients differ. For example, 3x^2y and −5x^2y are like terms, while 3xy and 3x^2y are not. Like terms can be combined by adding or subtracting their coefficients, but unlike terms cannot be combined into one term. Reordering terms can make like terms easier to identify, provided the commutative and associative properties are applied correctly.
Study instruction:
- Group the like terms in a multi-variable polynomial and simplify it by combining only the coefficients of matching variable parts.
Key point:
- Like terms must have identical variable parts and exponents.
- Combine coefficients only after matching the variable structure.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Polynomials and Degree
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
17. Q: What is the degree of a polynomial, and how is it determined?
A: Show answer
For a polynomial in one variable, the degree is the largest exponent of that variable with a nonzero coefficient after the polynomial has been simplified. A constant nonzero polynomial has degree zero. For a polynomial in several variables, the degree of a term is commonly the sum of its variable exponents, and the degree of the polynomial is the greatest term degree. The chapter's examples use one-variable degree to classify linear and quadratic functions and to describe higher-degree polynomial functions. Simplifying like terms first is important because terms may cancel, changing the highest remaining power.
Study instruction:
- Simplify three polynomials before identifying their degrees, including one where the highest-power terms cancel.
Key point:
- The degree is determined by the highest nonzero power after simplification.
- A polynomial's degree helps classify its function and likely graph behavior.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Degree of a Polynomial
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
18. Q: Why does the FOIL method work for squaring a binomial, and what are its limitations?
A: Show answer
FOIL stands for First, Outer, Inner, and Last. It reminds the learner to multiply each term in one binomial by each term in the other when expanding a product of two binomials. For (2x + 3)^2, multiplying (2x + 3)(2x + 3) produces 4x^2 + 6x + 6x + 9, which simplifies to 4x^2 + 12x + 9. FOIL is a memory aid for a two-term by two-term product, not a general rule for multiplying any polynomials. The distributive property works in all cases and is the safer method for products with more terms or higher powers.
Study instruction:
- Expand one squared binomial using FOIL and a three-term polynomial product using repeated distribution; compare why the second requires the general method.
Key point:
- FOIL organizes the four products in a binomial-by-binomial expansion.
- Repeated distribution is the general method for multiplying polynomials.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Factoring and Polynomial Operations
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
19. Q: How does factoring out the greatest common factor simplify a polynomial?
A: Show answer
The greatest common factor is the largest factor shared by every term in a polynomial. Factoring it out rewrites the expression as a product, which can make later simplification or equation solving easier. For example, 4x^2 − 12x has 4x as a common factor, so it becomes 4x(x − 3). This is the reverse of using the distributive property. The chapter recommends checking for a common factor before attempting more complicated factoring methods. After factoring, multiplying the factors back together is a simple way to verify that the original expression has been preserved.
Study instruction:
- Factor the greatest common factor from several expressions, including examples with negative coefficients and multiple variables, then expand to check each result.
Key point:
- Look for a common factor before trying a more complex factoring pattern.
- Factoring should be verified by reversing it with distribution.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Greatest Common Factor
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
20. Q: What is the difference-of-squares identity, and how is it used?
A: Show answer
The difference-of-squares identity states that a^2 − b^2 = (a + b)(a − b). It follows by expanding the product: the two middle terms cancel, leaving a^2 − b^2. This pattern is useful when an expression is the difference of two perfect squares. For example, x^2 − 16 becomes (x + 4)(x − 4). It can also be used to solve equations by factoring and applying the zero-product property. The pattern does not apply to a sum of squares over the real numbers in the same way, so the sign between the squared terms must be checked carefully.
Study instruction:
- Identify the two square terms in several expressions and decide whether the difference-of-squares identity applies before factoring.
Key point:
- A difference of two squares factors into a sum times a difference.
- The identity depends on subtraction between the square terms.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Special Factoring Patterns
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
21. Q: How can a quadratic trinomial be factored when the leading coefficient is one?
A: Show answer
For x^2 + bx + c, look for two numbers whose sum is b and whose product is c. If those numbers are r and s, the trinomial factors as (x + r)(x + s). For example, x^2 + 8x + 15 factors as (x + 3)(x + 5), because 3 + 5 = 8 and 3 times 5 = 15. If the numbers are not obvious, list factor pairs of c and test their sums. The method works only when suitable factors exist in the number system being used; not every quadratic factors neatly over the integers or rationals.
Study instruction:
- Factor several monic quadratics by listing factor pairs and checking both the required sum and product.
Key point:
- The two constants must add to the middle coefficient and multiply to the constant term.
- Some quadratics do not factor over the integers or rationals.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Factoring Polynomials
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
22. Q: How is a quadratic with a leading coefficient other than one factored by grouping?
A: Show answer
For ax^2 + bx + c, multiply a and c, then find two numbers whose product is ac and whose sum is b. Rewrite the middle term as the sum of those two terms, group the four terms into pairs, factor the greatest common factor from each pair, and then factor out the common binomial. For example, 2x^2 − x − 6 can be rewritten as 2x^2 + 3x − 4x − 6, grouped as x(2x + 3) − 2(2x + 3), and factored as (x − 2)(2x + 3). Always expand the result to check it.
Study instruction:
- Factor one quadratic with a leading coefficient greater than one and show the product-sum choice, grouping, common factors, and final verification.
Key point:
- The ac method splits the middle term so common binomial factors can be exposed.
- Factoring by grouping must be checked by multiplication.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Factoring by Grouping
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
23. Q: What is the zero-product property, and why is it needed when solving factored equations?
A: Show answer
The zero-product property states that if a product of factors equals zero, at least one factor must equal zero. Thus, if (x − 2)(3x + 5) = 0, then x − 2 = 0 or 3x + 5 = 0, giving x = 2 or x = −5/3. The property applies to products of more than two factors as well. It is used after an equation has been factored; it cannot be applied to a sum of terms that has not been expressed as a product. The constant factor that is known to be nonzero does not create an additional solution.
Study instruction:
- Solve three factored polynomial equations by setting each variable factor equal to zero and checking each solution in the original equation.
Key point:
- A zero product requires at least one factor to be zero.
- Factor first, then set each factor equal to zero.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Factoring and Solving Quadratics
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
24. Q: When should the quadratic formula be used instead of factoring?
A: Show answer
The quadratic formula solves ax^2 + bx + c = 0 when a is not zero: x = (−b plus or minus the square root of b^2 − 4ac) divided by 2a. It is useful when the quadratic does not factor neatly or when a reliable general method is needed. Factoring can be faster for simple integer roots, but the quadratic formula works for every quadratic equation with real or complex coefficients under the usual algebraic assumptions. The discriminant b^2 − 4ac indicates the type of roots: positive gives two distinct real roots, zero gives one repeated real root, and negative gives two non-real complex conjugate roots.
Study instruction:
- Solve one factorable and one non-factorable quadratic, then verify both solutions by substitution into the original equation.
Key point:
- The quadratic formula is a general method when factoring is inconvenient or impossible over the integers.
- The discriminant indicates the number and type of roots.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Polynomial Factoring and Quadratics
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
25. Q: What is a function, and what makes it different from a general relation?
A: Show answer
A function from a set X to a set Y assigns to every input in X exactly one output in Y. The input is often called the independent variable and the output the dependent variable. A relation that assigns two different outputs to the same input is not a function under this definition. Functions are central to calculus because they model how one quantity changes in response to another. In business and economics, a function might map the number of units produced to total cost, the quantity demanded to price, or revenue to sales volume. The function rule, its domain, and its range together describe the relationship.
Study instruction:
- Examine a table or mapping diagram and determine whether each input has exactly one output; explain why a repeated output is allowed but two outputs for one input are not.
Key point:
- Each permitted input of a function has exactly one output.
- Functions model relationships between quantities and are foundational to calculus.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Introduction to Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
26. Q: How is function notation evaluated at a number or another expression?
A: Show answer
To evaluate f(a), substitute a for every occurrence of x in the rule defining f, then simplify using the order of operations. If f(x) = x^2 − 2, then f(3) = 3^2 − 2 = 7. If the input is an expression such as x + 1, substitute the entire expression and use parentheses: f(x + 1) = (x + 1)^2 − 2. Expanding gives x^2 + 2x − 1. Parentheses are essential because the whole input replaces the variable. The notation f(3) means the function's output at input 3, not multiplication of f by 3.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a function at a positive number, a negative number, a variable, and a compound expression, using parentheses during substitution.
Key point:
- Function evaluation means substituting the entire input into the rule.
- Parentheses prevent sign and exponent errors when the input is an expression.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Function Notation
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
27. Q: How are the sum, difference, product, and quotient of two functions defined?
A: Show answer
If f and g are functions, their sum is (f + g)(x) = f(x) + g(x), their difference is (f − g)(x) = f(x) − g(x), and their product is (fg)(x) = f(x)g(x). Their quotient is (f/g)(x) = f(x)/g(x), provided g(x) is not zero. The domain of each combined function must satisfy the domain restrictions of both original functions, and the quotient adds the restriction that the denominator function cannot equal zero. These operations allow business models to be combined, such as adding two cost components or subtracting cost from revenue to obtain profit.
Study instruction:
- Given two simple functions, form their sum, product, and quotient and state the domain restrictions for each result.
Key point:
- Function operations apply the corresponding arithmetic operation to the outputs.
- The combined function's domain must respect the restrictions of its component functions.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Function Operations
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
28. Q: What is composition of functions, and why does the order matter?
A: Show answer
Composition applies one function to the output of another. If f maps x to z and g maps z to y, then (g composed with f)(x) = g(f(x)). The function f is applied first, and its output becomes the input to g. In general, g composed with f is not equal to f composed with g because changing the order changes the operations being performed. For example, if f(x) = x^2 − 2 and g(x) = 3x + 1, then f(g(x)) and g(f(x)) produce different expressions. Composition is important in calculus, where one function may represent a transformation applied to another relationship.
Study instruction:
- Calculate both f(g(x)) and g(f(x)) for two simple functions and explain which function is applied first in each expression.
Key point:
- In g(f(x)), f is applied before g.
- Function composition is generally not commutative.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Composition of Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
29. Q: What does it mean for a function to be one-to-one or onto?
A: Show answer
A function is one-to-one if different inputs always produce different outputs. Formally, if f(x1) = f(x2), then x1 must equal x2. A function is onto a specified codomain if every element of that codomain is reached by at least one input. These are different properties: one-to-one concerns whether outputs are shared by different inputs, while onto concerns whether every codomain value is attained. A function can be one-to-one without being onto its stated codomain. The distinction matters because an inverse function requires a one-to-one mapping on the domain and corresponding range; onto status depends on how the codomain is defined.
Study instruction:
- Use mapping diagrams to identify a function that is one-to-one, one that is onto, and one that is both or neither.
Key point:
- One-to-one means distinct inputs have distinct outputs.
- Onto means every element of the specified codomain is reached.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Inverse Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
30. Q: How is the inverse of a one-to-one function found and verified?
A: Show answer
Start with y = f(x), solve the equation for x in terms of y, and then interchange the variable names so the inverse is written in the conventional form y = f^−1(x). For f(x) = 3x + 5, solving for x gives x = (y − 5)/3, so f^−1(x) = (x − 5)/3. The inverse reverses the input-output relationship. It can be verified by checking that f(f^−1(x)) = x and f^−1(f(x)) = x on the appropriate domains. The notation f^−1 does not mean 1/f(x); the reciprocal of a function and its inverse are different concepts.
Study instruction:
- Find the inverse of a linear function, then verify both compositions and state any domain or range restrictions.
Key point:
- Find an inverse by solving for the original input and switching variable names.
- An inverse function is not the reciprocal of the original function.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Inverse Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
31. Q: What are the domain and range of a function?
A: Show answer
The domain is the set of permitted input values for which the function is defined. The range is the set of output values the function actually produces from its domain. A function may be described with a broad possible codomain, but its range can be a smaller subset. For example, the function f(x) = x^2 with domain all real numbers has range [0, infinity), because a real square cannot be negative. In business applications, the domain may be restricted by the meaning of the input: a production quantity cannot normally be negative, even if the algebraic formula can be evaluated there.
Study instruction:
- Determine the domain and range of a linear, quadratic, and square-root function using both algebra and a graph.
Key point:
- Domain describes allowed inputs; range describes actual outputs.
- Application constraints can restrict a domain beyond the algebraic formula alone.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Domain and Range
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
32. Q: What restrictions determine the domain of a rational function?
A: Show answer
A rational function is a quotient of two polynomial functions, so its denominator cannot equal zero. To determine the domain, set the denominator equal to zero, solve for the excluded inputs, and remove those values from the real number set. For example, a function with denominator x − 2 is undefined at x = 2, so its domain excludes that value. The graph may have a vertical asymptote at an excluded input, but if a common factor cancels, the graph can instead have a removable hole. The original domain restriction remains even when the algebraic expression simplifies. This distinction is important when interpreting rational functions in calculus.
Study instruction:
- Find the domain of three rational functions by solving their denominator equations and checking whether any excluded value creates a hole or an asymptote.
Key point:
- A rational function is undefined wherever its original denominator is zero.
- Simplifying common factors does not restore an excluded input to the original domain.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Rational Functions and Their Domains
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
33. Q: What is a linear function, and how are its slope and y-intercept interpreted?
A: Show answer
A linear function has the form y = ax + b, where a is the slope and b is the y-intercept. The slope measures the change in output for each one-unit increase in input, while the y-intercept is the output when x = 0. If the slope is positive, the line rises from left to right; if negative, it falls; if zero, it is horizontal. The graph is a straight line. In business models, slope can represent a constant rate such as additional cost per unit or the change in price associated with one additional unit of quantity. The intercept represents the model's starting value at zero input, when that input is meaningful.
Study instruction:
- Interpret the slope and intercept of three linear models using the units of their variables rather than describing them only as numbers.
Key point:
- Slope is the rate of change; the intercept is the output at zero input.
- Units and application context determine what these values mean.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Functions and Graphs
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
34. Q: How can a straight-line graph be drawn using its slope and intercept?
A: Show answer
The y-intercept gives one point on the graph, namely (0, b). The slope gives the vertical change divided by the horizontal change, so it can be used to locate a second point. For example, y = 2x − 3 has y-intercept −3, giving point (0, −3). Choosing x = 2 gives y = 1, so (2, 1) is another point. A straight line through those two points is the graph. A vertical line such as x = 4 is not a function of x because one input x = 4 corresponds to many possible y-values. This distinction matters when interpreting graphs as functions.
Study instruction:
- Graph a linear function using the intercept and slope, then check the line with a third point calculated from the equation.
Key point:
- Two distinct points determine a straight line.
- A vertical line fails the function rule because one input has many outputs.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Functions and Graphs
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
35. Q: What is a polynomial function, and how does its degree relate to its graph?
A: Show answer
A polynomial function in one variable has the form a_n x^n + a_(n−1)x^(n−1) + ... + a_1x + a_0, where the exponents are nonnegative integers and the leading coefficient is nonzero. The degree is the largest exponent. A degree-one polynomial is linear, while a degree-two polynomial is quadratic. Higher-degree polynomials can have more complicated shapes and turning behavior, but their domain is all real numbers because addition and multiplication of real numbers remain defined for every real input. The degree gives useful information about the function's algebraic structure and potential graph behavior, although it does not by itself determine every feature of the graph.
Study instruction:
- Classify several polynomial functions by degree and identify their leading coefficient and constant term.
Key point:
- Polynomial degree is the highest nonzero exponent.
- Polynomial functions are defined for every real input unless additional restrictions are imposed by a larger model.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Polynomial Functions and Degree
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
36. Q: How does vertex form help identify the important features of a quadratic function?
A: Show answer
A quadratic function can be written in vertex form as f(x) = a(x − h)^2 + k. Its vertex is (h, k), and its axis of symmetry is the vertical line x = h. If a is positive, the parabola opens upward and the vertex is a minimum. If a is negative, it opens downward and the vertex is a maximum. Starting from the general form ax^2 + bx + c, the horizontal coordinate of the vertex is h = −b/(2a), and k is found by evaluating f(h). Vertex form makes the horizontal and vertical shifts from the basic square function visible and helps graph the parabola efficiently.
Study instruction:
- Convert one quadratic from general form to vertex form and identify its vertex, axis of symmetry, and opening direction.
Key point:
- The vertex form directly reveals the turning point and axis of symmetry.
- The sign of the leading coefficient determines whether the parabola opens upward or downward.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Quadratic Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
37. Q: What is a rational function, and what does the graph of 1/x illustrate?
A: Show answer
A rational function is the quotient of two polynomial functions with a denominator that is not zero. The basic function y = 1/x is undefined at x = 0, so zero is excluded from its domain. Its graph has two branches, one for positive x and one for negative x, and approaches the axes without crossing them. The vertical line x = 0 is a vertical asymptote, and the horizontal line y = 0 is a horizontal asymptote. This example shows why domain restrictions matter and how the graph can behave near inputs where the formula is undefined. More complicated rational functions can have several excluded inputs, asymptotes, or removable discontinuities.
Study instruction:
- Sketch y = 1/x and label its domain, range, and asymptotes; then compare it with a rational function having a shifted denominator.
Key point:
- Rational functions are undefined where their denominator is zero.
- Asymptotes describe limiting graph behavior near excluded or extreme values.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Rational Functions
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
38. Q: What is a price-demand function, and why does its linear model usually have a negative slope?
A: Show answer
A price-demand function relates the price consumers are willing and able to pay to the quantity demanded. In the chapter's notation, price p is expressed as a function of quantity q. A simple linear approximation has the form p = aq + b with a negative slope, meaning that a greater quantity demanded is associated with a lower price along the demand curve. The intercept gives the model's price when quantity is zero, while the slope describes the change in price for each additional unit. In economics, the law of demand is commonly stated as an inverse relationship between price and quantity demanded, holding other factors constant. The linear model is a simplifying approximation, not a universal law for every market.
Study instruction:
- Interpret a negative-slope demand function in words and identify its price intercept and the economic meaning of its slope.
Key point:
- A simple inverse demand function slopes downward in price-quantity space.
- The linear form is a model whose assumptions should be checked against the application.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Demand, Supply, and Equilibrium
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
39. Q: How can a linear demand function be determined from two price-quantity observations?
A: Show answer
Two distinct points determine a line. If the observations are (q1, p1) and (q2, p2), the slope is (p2 − p1)/(q2 − q1). Substitute one point into p = aq + b to solve for the intercept b. For example, if demand is 30 units at a price of $30 and 10 units at a price of $40, the slope is (40 − 30)/(10 − 30) = −0.5. Substituting q = 10 and p = 40 gives b = 45, so p = −0.5q + 45. Check the model against both observations and make sure the variable order matches the axes and the wording of the question.
Study instruction:
- Construct a demand equation from two observations, show the slope calculation, solve for the intercept, and verify both original points.
Key point:
- Two observations determine a linear demand model when quantity values differ.
- Keep the order of price and quantity consistent when calculating the slope.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Demand and Supply Curves
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
40. Q: What is a supply function, and how does its slope differ from a demand function?
A: Show answer
A supply function describes the relationship between the price producers receive and the quantity they are willing to supply. In the chapter's price-as-a-function-of-quantity notation, a simple linear supply function has a positive slope: higher quantities supplied are associated with higher prices along the supply curve. This contrasts with the negative slope of a simple demand function. The intercept and slope should be interpreted with the variables and units used in the model. In standard economics, the law of supply describes a positive relationship between price and quantity supplied, holding other factors constant. Do not confuse a movement along a supply curve with a shift of the entire supply curve caused by changes in costs, technology, or other conditions.
Study instruction:
- Compare one demand and one supply function on the same axes and explain the meaning of their opposite slopes.
Key point:
- A simple supply curve slopes upward; a simple demand curve slopes downward.
- Changes in price move along a curve, while other market factors can shift the curve itself.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Demand, Supply, and Equilibrium
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
41. Q: What is the market equilibrium point in a simple demand-and-supply model?
A: Show answer
The equilibrium point is where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied. If demand and supply are both expressed as price functions of quantity, set the two expressions equal and solve for the equilibrium quantity. Substitute that quantity into either function to find the equilibrium price. Graphically, it is the intersection of the demand and supply curves. At a price above equilibrium, quantity supplied may exceed quantity demanded, creating a surplus; below equilibrium, quantity demanded may exceed quantity supplied, creating a shortage. The model assumes that the curves represent the same market and time period and that the units are consistent.
Study instruction:
- Solve a pair of linear demand and supply equations algebraically, then verify the intersection on a graph.
Key point:
- Equilibrium occurs when quantity demanded equals quantity supplied.
- The intersection provides both equilibrium quantity and equilibrium price.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Demand, Supply, and Equilibrium
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
42. Q: How is equilibrium found when demand and supply functions are nonlinear?
A: Show answer
The same principle applies: set the demand function equal to the supply function and solve the resulting equation. If the functions are quadratic, this may require factoring or using the quadratic formula. A quadratic equation can produce two mathematical solutions, but not every root is economically feasible. Quantity is generally restricted to nonnegative values, and the price and other variables must satisfy the model's domain. After selecting a feasible quantity, substitute it into the demand or supply equation to find the price. If multiple feasible intersections exist, the model may have more than one equilibrium point and should be interpreted graphically as well as algebraically.
Study instruction:
- Solve a nonlinear equilibrium equation, identify every mathematical root, and justify which roots are economically meaningful using the model's domain.
Key point:
- Nonlinear equilibrium is found by solving demand equals supply.
- Mathematical roots must be checked against economic feasibility and units.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Market Equilibrium
- OpenStax: Quadratic Functions
43. Q: What is a consumption function, and what does its intercept represent?
A: Show answer
A consumption function relates consumption C to revenue or income R. The chapter uses a linear model such as C = 0.2R + 10. The positive slope indicates that consumption increases as revenue increases. The positive intercept represents baseline or minimum consumption when revenue is zero in the simplified model. This is a modeling assumption intended to represent essential consumption that does not disappear entirely when revenue is absent. The function is a simplified relationship and should not be treated as a complete behavioral model for every household or economy. Its domain and range should reflect the context and the units used for revenue and consumption.
Study instruction:
- Interpret the slope and intercept of a consumption function and calculate consumption at two revenue levels, including zero revenue.
Key point:
- The slope describes how consumption changes with revenue.
- The intercept represents modeled baseline consumption at zero revenue.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Economic Relationships and Models
44. Q: What is the difference between fixed costs and variable costs?
A: Show answer
Fixed costs are costs that do not change with the level of output over the relevant range and time period, such as rent for a production space. Variable costs change with output, such as raw materials or packaging used for each unit produced. The distinction is made relative to the output level and time horizon: a cost that is fixed in the short run may change when a firm expands its facilities or renegotiates a lease. The chapter uses fixed rent and per-loaf ingredient costs to construct a simple total cost function. In more complex settings, variable costs need not be perfectly proportional to output, but the distinction remains useful for building a first model.
Study instruction:
- Classify a list of business expenses as fixed or variable for a stated time period and explain any cost whose classification depends on the production range.
Key point:
- Fixed costs do not vary with output over the modeled range.
- Variable costs change with the amount produced.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Costs and Revenue
45. Q: How is a total cost function constructed from fixed and variable costs?
A: Show answer
Total cost is the sum of fixed cost and variable cost: TC = FC + VC. If variable cost is a constant amount v per unit and x units are produced, then VC = vx and the total cost function is C(x) = FC + vx. In the chapter's bread-shop example, rent is $40 and variable cost is $3 per loaf, giving C(x) = 40 + 3x. Producing 32 loaves therefore costs 40 + 3(32) = $136. The intercept represents fixed cost at zero output, while the slope represents the additional variable cost for each unit in this linear model.
Study instruction:
- Construct a total cost function from a fixed expense and a per-unit variable cost, then calculate the cost at a specified output level.
Key point:
- Total cost combines fixed and variable cost components.
- In a linear cost model, the intercept is fixed cost and the slope is variable cost per unit.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Costs and Revenue
46. Q: How is a total revenue function calculated when the selling price is constant?
A: Show answer
Total revenue is the price per unit multiplied by the number of units sold: R(x) = px. If a shop sells each loaf for $6, then selling x loaves produces revenue R(x) = 6x. For 32 loaves, revenue is $192. The model assumes the selling price remains constant over the relevant range and that every unit produced is sold. If price changes with quantity, revenue should instead be written as R(x) = p(x)x, where p(x) is the price function. The distinction is important because a constant-price revenue function is linear, while a quantity-dependent price can create a nonlinear revenue function.
Study instruction:
- Write a revenue function for a constant price and another for a price that depends on quantity; compare their graphs and assumptions.
Key point:
- At a constant unit price, total revenue is price multiplied by units sold.
- If price varies with quantity, the revenue function may be nonlinear.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Total Revenue and Total Cost
47. Q: How is a profit function derived from total revenue and total cost?
A: Show answer
Profit is total revenue minus total cost: P(x) = R(x) − C(x). In the bread-shop example, revenue is 6x and cost is 40 + 3x, so profit is 6x − (40 + 3x) = 3x − 40. A positive value represents a profit, zero represents break-even, and a negative value represents a loss. The parentheses are important because the entire cost function must be subtracted from revenue. Profit depends on the assumptions used for price and cost, so a simple linear profit function should not be treated as a complete description of a business with changing prices, capacity limits, or nonlinear costs.
Study instruction:
- Derive a profit function from given revenue and cost functions, then calculate profit or loss at several output levels.
Key point:
- Profit equals total revenue minus total cost.
- Negative profit represents a loss rather than an invalid mathematical result.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Profit, Revenue, and Cost
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
48. Q: What is the break-even point, and how can it be found algebraically?
A: Show answer
The break-even point occurs when total revenue equals total cost, which is equivalent to profit being zero. It can be found by setting P(x) = 0 or by setting R(x) = C(x), then solving for output x. In the bread-shop example, profit is 3x − 40, so break-even occurs when 3x − 40 = 0, giving x = 40/3, or about 13.33 loaves. The exact algebraic threshold is fractional, but if loaves can only be sold as whole units, the business must examine the results at 13 and 14 loaves rather than simply rounding and claiming that 13 is exactly break-even.
Study instruction:
- Find the break-even quantity from both the profit equation and the equality of revenue and cost, then check the nearest feasible whole-unit outputs.
Key point:
- Break-even means revenue equals cost and profit is zero.
- When output is indivisible, check actual integer quantities around the algebraic threshold.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
- OpenStax: Revenue, Cost, Profit, and Break-Even
49. Q: Why must the domain and units of a business function be checked before interpreting its graph?
A: Show answer
An algebraic formula may be defined for values that are impossible or irrelevant in the business setting. Production quantity cannot normally be negative, and a model may be intended only over a specified range of output. Units also affect interpretation: a quantity expressed in thousands means x = 1.5 represents 1,500 units, not 1.5 individual units. A price function may use dollars per unit, while total cost and revenue use dollars. If the units are mixed or omitted, a correct calculation can be reported incorrectly. The chapter repeatedly restricts economic graphs to feasible quantities and prices, so every model should state its domain, range, units, and assumptions.
Study instruction:
- For a demand, cost, and profit function, write the allowed input interval, output units, and any scale factor such as thousands or millions.
Key point:
- Economic interpretation imposes domain restrictions beyond algebraic validity.
- Units and scale factors must be carried through every calculation and graph.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Domain and Range
- OpenStax: Demand, Supply, and Equilibrium
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1
50. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 1, “Basic Algebra,” of Calculus for Business and Economics?
A: Show answer
Chapter 1 reviews the algebra needed for later calculus: number sets and properties, intervals, exponents, fractions, order of operations, polynomials, factoring, functions, domains, ranges, and graphs. It emphasizes that algebraic manipulation should preserve meaning and that functions provide a language for modeling relationships between business and economic variables. Linear demand and supply functions lead to market equilibrium, while cost, revenue, and profit functions describe a firm's production and financial outcomes. The break-even point is where revenue equals cost. The practical lesson is to simplify carefully, check restrictions, interpret slopes and intercepts in context, and keep units and feasible domains visible. These foundations prepare the learner to study rates of change and derivatives in the next chapter.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter by solving one problem from each area: number properties, exponents, fractions, polynomial factoring, function evaluation, domain and range, graphing, market equilibrium, cost, revenue, and break-even analysis.
Key point:
- Algebraic fluency supports every later topic in calculus and business modeling.
- Correct interpretation requires both mathematical accuracy and attention to context, units, and domain.
Source links:
- Fortney and Smail: Chapter 1, Basic Algebra
- Routledge: Calculus for Business and Economics
- OpenStax: College Algebra Key Concepts
