Table 2
Calculus for Business and Economics — Chapter 2: Rates of Change and the Derivative
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 2?
A: Show answer
The chapter develops the derivative as a tool for measuring how one quantity changes with respect to another. It begins with average rates of change over an interval, then uses limits to define instantaneous rates of change at a single input. The derivative is introduced as a new function whose value gives the instantaneous rate at each point. The chapter then connects that idea to tangent-line slopes and to business measures such as marginal cost, marginal revenue, and marginal profit. These ideas let a business analyst move from describing total amounts to describing how those amounts change as output changes.
Study instruction:
- Draw a concept map linking average rate, limit, derivative, tangent slope, and marginal functions.
- Give one business quantity that could be studied with each concept.
Key point:
- A derivative measures instantaneous rate of change.
- Marginal business quantities are applications of derivatives.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 2
- OpenStax Calculus: Derivatives as Rates of Change
2. Q: What is the average rate of change of y with respect to x?
A: Show answer
The average rate of change compares the change in the dependent variable y with the change in the independent variable x over a specified interval. If the endpoints are (x1,y1) and (x2,y2), the rate is (y2 − y1)/(x2 − x1), provided x2 is not equal to x1. It tells how much y changes on average for each one-unit change in x between those endpoints. The interval matters: a different pair of endpoints can produce a different average rate, especially when the function is nonlinear. The numerator and denominator must be taken in the same endpoint order.
Study instruction:
- Write the average-rate formula and label each change in the numerator and denominator.
- Calculate a rate over an interval in both endpoint orders and explain why the result is unchanged.
Key point:
- Average rate of change is change in output divided by change in input.
- Use a consistent endpoint order in both differences.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Difference Quotients and Secant Slopes
- Fortney, Chapter 2, Section 2.1
3. Q: Why must the units of an average rate of change be stated?
A: Show answer
The units identify what is changing per unit of the independent variable. If y is profit measured in dollars and x is output measured in widgets, the average rate has units of dollars per widget. If revenue is measured in thousands of dollars and output in individual items, the numerical rate must be converted before it is reported in dollars per item. Units help detect mistakes and make the result meaningful to a decision-maker. A bare number such as 220 does not explain whether it means dollars per widget, thousand dollars per widget, or some other rate.
Study instruction:
- Attach units to a rate computed from dollars and units produced.
- Convert a rate expressed in thousands of dollars per item into dollars per item.
Key point:
- A rate carries output units divided by input units.
- Always preserve scale factors such as thousands or millions.
Source links:
- Fortney, Chapter 2, Business Applications
- OpenStax rates of change and units
4. Q: How is the average rate of change calculated for f(x) = 10x^2 from x = 3 to x = 5?
A: Show answer
First calculate the endpoint outputs: f(3) = 10(3^2) = 90 and f(5) = 10(5^2) = 250. The change in output is 250 − 90 = 160, while the change in input is 5 − 3 = 2. Dividing gives an average rate of 160/2 = 80. This means that over the interval from 3 to 5, the output changes by an average of 80 units for each one-unit increase in x. It does not mean the function's instantaneous rate is 80 at every point in the interval.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate the endpoint values before using the rate formula.
- Compare the average rate with the derivative value at x = 3 and x = 5 once the derivative is known.
Key point:
- Evaluate both endpoints before subtracting.
- An interval average is not a constant instantaneous rate.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.1
- OpenStax average and instantaneous rates
5. Q: Why can the average rate of change be negative?
A: Show answer
The average rate is negative when the dependent variable decreases as the independent variable increases over the selected interval. In the chapter's example f(x) = 100x − 20x^2, f(2) = 120 and f(5) = 0, so the average rate from 2 to 5 is (0 − 120)/(5 − 2) = −40. The negative sign describes the direction of change; it is not an arithmetic error. In a business setting, a negative rate might describe declining revenue, falling resale value, or a reduction in profit as output moves through a particular range.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the average rate for a function whose endpoint output falls.
- Explain the business meaning of the negative sign in a sentence with units.
Key point:
- A negative rate means the output fell as the input increased over that interval.
- Interpret the sign in context rather than removing it.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.2
- OpenStax rates of change
6. Q: How is average rate of change used to measure a change in profit?
A: Show answer
The chapter considers P(x) = −2x^2 + 400x − 500 and compares production of 40 and 50 widgets. It calculates P(40) = 12,300 and P(50) = 14,500, so profit rises by 2,200 dollars while output rises by 10 widgets. The average rate is 2,200/10 = 220 dollars per widget. This is the average change in profit per additional widget over that interval. It is not the profit earned on each widget individually, nor does it state the exact profit contribution of the next widget at a particular production level.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the two profit values and calculate the average rate.
- Explain the difference between average profit per widget and average change in profit per additional widget.
Key point:
- The average rate describes profit change over a stated production interval.
- It is not the same as total profit divided by output.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.26
- OpenStax costs and profit
7. Q: What is the geometric meaning of average rate of change?
A: Show answer
The average rate of change between two inputs is the slope of the secant line through the corresponding points on the graph. A secant line intersects the graph at the two selected points. Its slope is calculated using the same rise-over-run formula as any straight line. As the second point moves closer to the first, the secant slope can provide a better estimate of the local rate of change. This geometric interpretation connects the algebraic formula to the graph and prepares the way for defining a tangent line and the derivative.
Study instruction:
- Draw a curve and a secant through two points on it.
- Calculate the secant slope and identify its rise and run.
Key point:
- Average rate of change is the slope of a secant line.
- The secant uses two distinct points on the graph.
Source links:
- OpenStax: Secant Lines and Difference Quotients
8. Q: What is an instantaneous rate of change?
A: Show answer
An instantaneous rate of change describes how quickly a function is changing at one specified input. Unlike an average rate, it is not calculated across a fixed interval. The chapter estimates it by choosing a second input very close to the target, calculating the average rate, and observing how the estimates behave as the second input approaches the target. The exact value is obtained through a limit. This rate is the derivative at that point and is useful when a business asks about the current rate at which cost, revenue, or profit is changing at a particular output level.
Study instruction:
- Contrast a rate over the interval from 2 to 5 with a rate at x = 2.
- Describe how a sequence of closer secant points can estimate the instantaneous rate.
Key point:
- Instantaneous rate is a local rate at one input.
- The derivative provides its exact value when the defining limit exists.
Source links:
- OpenStax definition of the derivative
- Fortney, Section 2.2
9. Q: Why cannot the average-rate formula be used by setting both endpoints equal to the same input?
A: Show answer
If x1 and x2 are both set to a, then the numerator becomes f(a) − f(a) = 0 and the denominator becomes a − a = 0. The quotient is therefore 0/0, which is undefined. The instantaneous rate is not obtained by dividing zero by zero. Instead, one keeps the second input distinct, forms the difference quotient, and then studies its limit as the difference approaches zero. The limit may exist even though the quotient itself is not defined at the point where the increment equals zero.
Study instruction:
- Write the difference quotient with x2 = a + h and identify why h cannot equal zero during the division.
- Explain the difference between an undefined quotient and a limit that exists.
Key point:
- The difference quotient is formed for a nonzero increment.
- The derivative is the limit of that quotient, not its value at zero increment.
Source links:
- OpenStax difference quotient definition
10. Q: How does the chapter estimate the instantaneous rate for f(x) = 10x^2 at x = 2?
A: Show answer
It fixes the first input at 2 and chooses nearby second inputs such as 2.1, 2.01, and 2.0001. The corresponding average rates are 41, 40.1, and 40.001. These estimates approach 40 as the second input approaches 2. The pattern suggests that the instantaneous rate at x = 2 is 40. The numerical method is useful for intuition and for checking a symbolic result, but it is still an estimate until the limit is evaluated. Rounding and calculator precision also affect how many decimal places appear in the table.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate the average rate using x2 = 2.001.
- Explain why the estimate approaches 40 rather than becoming exactly 40 at a nonzero increment.
Key point:
- Closer secant points can provide better numerical estimates of the tangent slope.
- A table suggests the limit but does not replace its calculation.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.3
- OpenStax estimating derivatives
11. Q: What is the difference quotient with increment h?
A: Show answer
The difference quotient is [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h, where h is nonzero. It compares the function value at x + h with the value at x, then divides by the change in input. The quotient is the slope of the secant line through those two graph points. The derivative function is obtained by taking the limit as h approaches zero, provided that limit exists. Keeping h visible during the algebra makes it easier to expand f(x + h), subtract f(x), factor out h, and cancel the common factor before taking the limit.
Study instruction:
- Write the difference quotient for f(x) = x^2 and expand (x + h)^2.
- Identify the factor that must be cancelled before substituting h = 0.
Key point:
- The difference quotient measures a finite change per input increment.
- The derivative is its limit as the increment approaches zero.
Source links:
- OpenStax difference quotient
12. Q: How is the derivative of f(x) = 10x^2 derived from the limit definition?
A: Show answer
Substitute x + h into the function: f(x + h) = 10(x + h)^2 = 10x^2 + 20xh + 10h^2. Subtract f(x) = 10x^2 and divide by h to obtain (20xh + 10h^2)/h. Factor h from the numerator and cancel the nonzero h, leaving 20x + 10h. Now take the limit as h approaches zero to get f'(x) = 20x. The derivative is a new function; evaluating it at a particular x gives the instantaneous rate at that input.
Study instruction:
- Repeat the derivation without skipping the expansion and cancellation steps.
- Evaluate the resulting derivative at x = 2 and x = 3.
Key point:
- Expand, subtract, factor, cancel, and then take the limit.
- For 10x^2, the derivative function is 20x.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.8
- OpenStax derivative definition
13. Q: How can factoring help evaluate a limit that initially produces 0/0?
A: Show answer
Consider f(x) = (x^2 − 9)/(x − 3) as x approaches 3. Direct substitution gives 0/0, so it does not determine the limit. Factor the numerator as (x − 3)(x + 3), then cancel the common factor for x not equal to 3. The simplified expression is x + 3, whose limit as x approaches 3 is 6. The original function remains undefined at x = 3, but its values near 3 approach 6. The cancellation is valid for nearby inputs where x is not 3; it does not redefine the original function at the excluded point.
Study instruction:
- Factor and simplify the expression before evaluating the limit.
- Explain why the limit can exist even though the original function is undefined at the target input.
Key point:
- A 0/0 form signals that direct substitution is inconclusive.
- Algebraic simplification can reveal a limit without changing the original function's domain.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.6
- OpenStax limits and difference quotients
14. Q: Is direct substitution always sufficient for evaluating a limit?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter uses elementary examples where direct substitution works after any necessary algebraic simplification, but that is not a universal rule for all limits. If substitution produces a defined value and the function is continuous at the target, it gives the limit. If it produces 0/0, further analysis is needed; factoring may help in the chapter's examples. Other limits can require methods beyond the introductory techniques. The book explicitly adopts an informal treatment suited to its business applications, so students should not assume that every limit in more advanced calculus can be solved by substitution alone.
Study instruction:
- Classify several substitutions as direct evaluation, 0/0, or division by a nonzero number.
- Identify one reason why the chapter's introductory limit methods are not exhaustive.
Key point:
- Direct substitution works when continuity and the expression permit it.
- An indeterminate form requires additional reasoning.
Source links:
- OpenStax defining the derivative
- Fortney, Section 2.2
15. Q: What is the derivative function, and how is it different from a derivative at one point?
A: Show answer
The derivative function f'(x) gives the instantaneous rate of change of f at every input where the derivative exists. A derivative at one point, such as f'(2), is a single number obtained by evaluating that new function at x = 2. For f(x) = 10x^2, the derivative function is f'(x) = 20x; therefore f'(2) = 40 and f'(3) = 60. Keeping the two meanings separate prevents confusion between a rule that describes all inputs and a numerical rate at a particular input.
Study instruction:
- Write the derivative function for 10x^2 and evaluate it at three inputs.
- Label each result as a function or a number at a point.
Key point:
- f'(x) is a function; f'(a) is its value at a point.
- The derivative function supplies local rates across the domain.
Source links:
- OpenStax derivative at a point
- OpenStax derivative key concepts
16. Q: What do the notations f'(x) and dy/dx mean?
A: Show answer
f'(x), read “f prime of x,” is Newton-style notation for the derivative function. Leibniz notation writes the same derivative as dy/dx when y = f(x). Both notations are widely used and refer to the same instantaneous rate of change. The chapter connects Leibniz notation with the idea of a change in y relative to a change in x, often written as Δy/Δx for finite differences. In derivative notation, dy/dx represents the limiting rate, not an ordinary quotient of two finite changes. Students should recognize both forms and use the one requested in a problem.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a derivative in both prime notation and Leibniz notation.
- Explain the difference between Δy/Δx and dy/dx in this chapter's context.
Key point:
- f'(x) and dy/dx are two notations for the derivative.
- Finite differences and limiting derivatives are related but not identical.
Source links:
- Fortney, Section 2.3
- OpenStax derivative notation
17. Q: Why is the derivative described as an instantaneous rate of change?
A: Show answer
The derivative is defined from average rates over smaller and smaller intervals. As the interval width approaches zero, the secant slope approaches a limiting value, which is the instantaneous rate at the point. This gives a precise meaning to how fast a function is changing at one input. The derivative is not simply the function's value or the amount of change over a finite interval. In business, it can describe the local change in cost, revenue, or profit as output changes at a particular production level.
Study instruction:
- Explain the transition from average rate to instantaneous rate without using the word “instantaneous” in the explanation.
- Give a business example where a local rate is more useful than an interval average.
Key point:
- The derivative is the limit of average rates over shrinking intervals.
- It measures local change rather than total amount.
Source links:
- OpenStax derivatives as rates of change
18. Q: What is the derivative of a constant function?
A: Show answer
A constant function has the same output for every input in its domain. Because the output does not change when the input changes, its average rate of change is zero wherever the denominator is nonzero. The limit of those zero rates is also zero, so if f(x) = c, then f'(x) = 0. The chapter proves this using the derivative definition. The rule is useful when differentiating polynomials because a constant term contributes nothing to the derivative, even though it remains part of the original function.
Study instruction:
- Use the limit definition to differentiate f(x) = 5.
- Explain why the derivative of a constant term disappears from a polynomial derivative.
Key point:
- A constant function has derivative zero.
- Constant terms do not affect the rate of change.
Source links:
- OpenStax constant rule
- Fortney, Example 2.9
19. Q: Why is the derivative of f(x) = x equal to 1?
A: Show answer
Substituting x + h into f gives f(x + h) = x + h. The difference quotient becomes [(x + h) − x]/h = h/h = 1 for h not equal to zero. Taking the limit as h approaches zero leaves 1. The graph of y = x is a straight line with slope 1 everywhere, so the derivative agrees with the line's constant slope. This example is a simple bridge between the algebraic limit definition and the geometric interpretation of a derivative as a tangent slope.
Study instruction:
- Derive the result from the difference quotient rather than memorizing it.
- Compare the result with the slope of the line y = x.
Key point:
- The derivative of the identity function x is 1.
- A straight line has the same slope at every point.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.10
- OpenStax basic differentiation rules
20. Q: What is the derivative of a linear function mx + b?
A: Show answer
The derivative of mx + b is m. The term mx has constant slope m, while the constant b has derivative zero. Thus, differentiating a linear function returns its slope. For example, if f(x) = 5x + 7, then f'(x) = 5. The result does not depend on x because the line's rate of change is constant. In a business model, a linear function's derivative can be interpreted as a fixed change in output per unit change in input, with units determined by the variables.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate three linear functions with positive, negative, and zero slopes.
- State the business units of each slope when x is output and y is dollars.
Key point:
- The derivative of mx + b is m.
- The constant intercept does not change the slope.
Source links:
- OpenStax constant and power rules
- Fortney, Section 2.3
21. Q: What is the power rule for differentiation?
A: Show answer
For a power function f(x) = x^n, the power rule gives f'(x) = n x^(n−1), wherever the function is defined and differentiable. The exponent becomes a coefficient, and the exponent is reduced by one. For example, the derivative of x^3 is 3x^2, and the derivative of x^2 is 2x. The rule avoids repeating the full limit derivation for every power. The chapter develops it from patterns in the derivatives of x, x^2, and x^3, then applies it to more general exponents.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate x^7, x^4, and x^2 using the power rule.
- Check one result by applying the limit definition.
Key point:
- Multiply by the exponent and reduce the exponent by one.
- Apply the rule only where the function is defined and differentiable.
Source links:
- OpenStax power rule
- Fortney, Section 2.3
22. Q: How are negative exponents handled when differentiating?
A: Show answer
Rewrite a reciprocal power as a negative exponent, then apply the power rule. For example, 1/x^4 becomes x^(−4), whose derivative is −4x^(−5), or −4/x^5. This form is easier to differentiate because the power rule applies directly. The domain restriction x not equal to zero remains in force. After differentiating, the result may be rewritten with positive exponents for readability, but the algebraic value must remain unchanged. This method is useful for rational expressions whose denominators contain powers of x.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate 1/x^3 and 5/x^2 after rewriting each with a negative exponent.
- State the input restriction before and after differentiation.
Key point:
- Rewrite reciprocal powers as negative powers before applying the rule.
- The original denominator restriction remains relevant.
Source links:
- OpenStax power rule with negative exponents
23. Q: How does the power rule apply to fractional exponents and roots?
A: Show answer
Rewrite a root as a fractional power, then apply the same exponent rule. For example, √x is x^(1/2), so its derivative is (1/2)x^(−1/2), equivalent to 1/(2√x) for x greater than zero. Likewise, x^(5/3) differentiates to (5/3)x^(2/3). The algebraic rewriting is important because it puts the expression into a form the power rule can use. Domain restrictions must still be considered, especially for even roots and negative powers near zero.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite √x and the cube root of x^5 as powers, then differentiate.
- State where each original function and derivative is defined over the real numbers.
Key point:
- Fractional exponents make root expressions compatible with the power rule.
- Check the real domain before interpreting the derivative.
Source links:
- OpenStax power rule and radicals
- Fortney, Examples 2.15–2.17
24. Q: What is the constant-multiple rule?
A: Show answer
If f(x) = k g(x), where k is a constant, then f'(x) = k g'(x). The constant coefficient can be carried through the differentiation while the remaining function is differentiated. For example, the derivative of 10x^2 is 10 times the derivative of x^2, giving 20x. This rule follows from the limit definition because a constant factor can be taken outside the limit. It makes polynomial differentiation efficient and helps prevent the common error of forgetting to multiply by the coefficient after applying the power rule.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate 7x^3, −4x^5, and 15√x using the constant-multiple rule.
- Identify the coefficient before applying the power rule.
Key point:
- A constant coefficient remains as a multiplier after differentiation.
- Apply the power rule to the function part, not to the coefficient.
Source links:
- OpenStax constant-multiple rule
25. Q: What are the sum and difference rules for derivatives?
A: Show answer
The derivative of a sum is the sum of the derivatives, and the derivative of a difference is the difference of the derivatives. If f(x) = g(x) + h(x), then f'(x) = g'(x) + h'(x); the analogous rule holds for subtraction. These rules extend to any finite sum or difference of differentiable functions. They allow a polynomial to be differentiated term by term after applying the constant-multiple and power rules. The sign of each term must be preserved, especially when the original expression contains subtraction or negative coefficients.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate 4x^3 − 7x^2 + 2x − 9 term by term.
- Check that the constant term contributes zero and that negative signs remain attached to their terms.
Key point:
- Differentiate each term of a sum or difference separately.
- Preserve coefficients and signs throughout the calculation.
Source links:
- OpenStax sum, difference, and constant-multiple rules
26. Q: How do the basic rules combine to differentiate a polynomial?
A: Show answer
Differentiate each term using the power rule, keep its coefficient through the constant-multiple rule, and combine the results using the sum and difference rules. For example, f(x) = 10x^7 − 5x^4 + 3x^3 − 6x^2 + 40x − 20 has derivative 70x^6 − 20x^3 + 9x^2 − 12x + 40. The constant term disappears because its derivative is zero. This method is efficient because each polynomial term can be handled independently; no new limit derivation is needed for the whole polynomial.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate a six-term polynomial and show the rule used on each term.
- Substitute a chosen input into both f and f' to distinguish the function value from the rate.
Key point:
- Polynomial derivatives are found term by term.
- Constant terms vanish, while powers decrease by one.
Source links:
- OpenStax polynomial differentiation
- Fortney, Example 2.22
27. Q: What are higher derivatives?
A: Show answer
The second derivative is the derivative of the first derivative, the third derivative is the derivative of the second, and the process can continue. If f'(x) describes the rate at which f changes, then f''(x) describes how the first derivative changes. The chapter introduces higher derivatives briefly and notes that they will be developed later. In business analysis, higher derivatives can help describe how a marginal quantity itself changes, but students should first be confident with the first derivative and its units before interpreting additional derivatives.
Study instruction:
- Find the first and second derivatives of f(x) = 3x^4 − 2x^2.
- State how the units change when differentiating a second time with respect to the same input.
Key point:
- Higher derivatives are obtained by differentiating repeatedly.
- The second derivative measures the change in the first derivative.
Source links:
- OpenStax derivative key concepts
- Fortney, Section 2.4
28. Q: What is a tangent line to a function at a point?
A: Show answer
A tangent line is the limiting line approached by secant lines as their second point approaches the point of tangency. Its slope is the derivative at that input. The chapter describes it intuitively as a line that “just touches” the curve and gives a close linear approximation near the point. That phrase is only an informal picture: a tangent line can cross a curve at the point, so the defining idea is the limiting slope, not merely touching from one side. The derivative therefore connects the algebraic limit to a geometric line on the graph.
Study instruction:
- Draw several secant lines approaching one point on a curve.
- Explain why the limiting slope is more precise than the phrase “just touches.”
Key point:
- The tangent-line slope is the derivative at the point.
- A tangent is defined by a limiting process, not by a visual slogan.
Source links:
- OpenStax tangent lines and derivatives
29. Q: How does a secant line approach a tangent line?
A: Show answer
A secant line passes through two points on the graph. Hold one point fixed and move the second point closer to it. The secant slope is the average rate of change between the two points. As the second point approaches the fixed point, the slopes may approach a limit, and the corresponding secant lines approach the tangent line. The chapter illustrates this with f(x) = 5x − 0.1x^2 at x = 10. The derivative gives the exact limiting slope, while each finite secant gives an average rate over a nonzero interval.
Study instruction:
- Calculate secant slopes using two different nearby inputs around x = 10.
- Compare the slopes and predict the tangent slope.
Key point:
- Secant slopes are average rates over finite intervals.
- The tangent slope is their limit as the interval shrinks.
Source links:
- OpenStax secant and tangent lines
- Fortney, Section 2.5
30. Q: Why is the tangent line described as a local linear approximation?
A: Show answer
Near the point of tangency, the curve and its tangent line have nearly the same value and slope. This makes the tangent a useful straight-line approximation for small changes in the input. The approximation generally becomes less accurate as the input moves farther from the point, especially when the function curves sharply. In the chapter's graph of f(x) = 5x − 0.1x^2 at x = 10, the tangent line closely approximates nearby function values. The derivative provides the slope needed to build that local approximation.
Study instruction:
- Compare a curve and its tangent line at a chosen point.
- Identify a nearby input where the approximation is reasonable and a distant input where it may fail.
Key point:
- A tangent line approximates a differentiable curve locally.
- The approximation is not automatically reliable far from the point.
Source links:
- OpenStax tangent-line interpretation
31. Q: How does the chapter distinguish average and instantaneous rates in business applications?
A: Show answer
An average rate describes how a business quantity changes across a specified production interval. An instantaneous rate describes the local change at one production level. For profit, the average rate can compare P(40) with P(50), while the derivative P'(x) gives the instantaneous rate at x. The distinction matters because a nonlinear cost, revenue, or profit function can change at different rates across its domain. A manager should use an interval average for a stated range and a marginal value for a local decision near a specific output level.
Study instruction:
- Write one business question that requires an average rate and one that requires a marginal rate.
- Explain why the same number should not be assumed to answer both questions.
Key point:
- Average rates describe intervals; derivatives describe local rates.
- Choose the measure that matches the decision being made.
Source links:
- OpenStax average and instantaneous rates
- Fortney, Section 2.6
32. Q: Why can an average rate over a broad interval be a poor estimate for a small change inside that interval?
A: Show answer
The average rate is the slope of the secant over the whole interval, while a small change near one endpoint depends more closely on the local slope. In the chapter's revenue example R(x) = 500x − 10x^2, the average rate from 10 to 20 is 200 thousand dollars per widget. Using that value to estimate the change from 11 to 12 gives 200 thousand dollars, but the exact change is 270 thousand dollars. The discrepancy occurs because the function is nonlinear and its slope changes with x. A derivative at the relevant point usually provides a more local estimate.
Study instruction:
- Calculate R(12) − R(11) and compare it with the interval average from 10 to 20.
- Explain why a broad-interval average should not be treated as a constant marginal rate.
Key point:
- An interval average may be a rough estimate, not a local rate.
- Nonlinear functions can change substantially within the interval.
Source links:
- Fortney, Examples 2.27–2.29
- OpenStax rates of change
33. Q: What does “marginal” mean in business and economics?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, marginal means the instantaneous rate of change of a total business function. Marginal cost is the derivative of total cost, marginal revenue is the derivative of total revenue, and marginal profit is the derivative of total profit. These derivatives describe how the corresponding total is changing at a particular output level. The word is used because business questions often concern the effect of producing or selling a little more. Marginal values are rates, so their units are typically dollars per additional item rather than total dollars.
Study instruction:
- Match MC, MR, and MP with C'(x), R'(x), and P'(x).
- State the units for each if x counts items and the totals are measured in dollars.
Key point:
- Marginal cost, revenue, and profit are derivatives of total functions.
- Marginal quantities describe local change, not total amounts.
Source links:
- OpenStax marginal functions
- OpenStax firm output and marginal analysis
34. Q: How is marginal cost calculated from a total cost function?
A: Show answer
Marginal cost is the derivative of total cost: MC(x) = C'(x). For the chapter's example C(x) = 350x − 0.75x^2, differentiation gives C'(x) = 350 − 1.5x. At x = 150, marginal cost is 350 − 1.5(150) = 125 dollars per unit. The source solution once calls this “marginal revenue,” but because the differentiated function is C(x), the correct label is marginal cost. Always identify the original total function before assigning the business name to its derivative.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate the cost function and evaluate it at x = 150.
- Explain why the result is marginal cost rather than marginal revenue.
Key point:
- MC(x) = C'(x).
- The business label must match the total function being differentiated.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.30
- OpenStax marginal cost definition
35. Q: How is marginal revenue calculated and interpreted?
A: Show answer
Marginal revenue is the derivative of total revenue: MR(x) = R'(x). The chapter uses R(x) = 5x − 0.0005x^2, where revenue is measured in thousands of dollars. Differentiating gives R'(x) = 5 − 0.001x. At x = 1,000, marginal revenue is 4 thousand dollars per item, or $4,000 per item. The units must be interpreted carefully because the original revenue function is in thousands. A derivative value describes the local rate near that output level, not the total revenue from selling one item.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate the revenue function and evaluate at x = 1,000.
- Convert the result from thousands of dollars to dollars and state the per-item unit.
Key point:
- MR(x) = R'(x).
- Preserve the revenue scale when interpreting the derivative.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.31
- OpenStax marginal revenue definition
36. Q: How is marginal profit calculated?
A: Show answer
Marginal profit is the derivative of the total profit function: MP(x) = P'(x). For P(x) = 4x^2 − 5x + 10, the derivative is P'(x) = 8x − 5. At x = 5, marginal profit is 35 dollars per item. The value estimates how quickly profit is changing at that output level. It does not mean total profit equals $35, nor does it guarantee that the next unit will add exactly $35. For a finite one-unit increase, compare P(x + 1) − P(x) with P'(x).
Study instruction:
- Differentiate the profit function and calculate P'(5).
- Calculate the exact profit change from x = 5 to x = 6 and compare it with the marginal estimate.
Key point:
- MP(x) = P'(x).
- Marginal profit is a local rate, not total profit.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.32
- OpenStax marginal profit definition
37. Q: Why does marginal cost approximate the cost of producing one additional item?
A: Show answer
Marginal cost C'(x) is the instantaneous rate of change in total cost at output x. For a small increase of one item, it approximates C(x + 1) − C(x), the exact additional cost. The approximation is often useful because differentiating a cost function and evaluating it can be easier than calculating two separate totals. It is still an approximation for a finite change, and the accuracy depends on how much the marginal cost changes over that interval. The chapter compares marginal cost with the exact cost of the next widget to illustrate this relationship.
Study instruction:
- Write the exact one-unit cost increase and the marginal-cost approximation at the same output level.
- Explain why they may differ for a nonlinear cost function.
Key point:
- C'(x) approximates C(x + 1) − C(x).
- A derivative is a local rate, while one item is a finite change.
Source links:
- OpenStax marginal cost approximation
38. Q: What does the chapter's 65th-widget example show about exact and marginal cost?
A: Show answer
For C(x) = 100 + 150x − 0.09x^2, the exact cost of the 65th widget is C(65) − C(64) = 138.39 dollars. The marginal cost function is C'(x) = 150 − 0.18x, so C'(64) = 138.48 dollars. The marginal value is close to the exact increase but is not identical. This comparison illustrates why marginal cost is a useful estimate for the next unit and why the exact discrete change should be calculated when precision is required for a specific item.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate C(64), C(65), and C'(64).
- Compute the absolute difference between the exact cost and the marginal estimate.
Key point:
- The exact cost of the next item is C(x + 1) − C(x).
- Marginal cost at x is a close local approximation, not an identity for finite steps.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.34
- OpenStax marginal-cost interpretation
39. Q: What is average revenue, and how does it relate to price?
A: Show answer
Average revenue is total revenue divided by the number of units sold: AR = TR/x for x greater than zero. If total revenue is price times quantity, TR = p(x)x, then AR = p(x). Thus average revenue per unit equals the price-demand function in the chapter's model. The restriction x greater than zero matters because division by zero is undefined. Average revenue is different from marginal revenue: average revenue is revenue per unit across all units sold, while marginal revenue is the derivative of total revenue at a particular quantity.
Study instruction:
- Starting with TR = p(x)x, derive AR = p(x).
- Explain why AR is undefined at x = 0 under the quotient definition.
Key point:
- AR = TR/x and equals price when TR = p(x)x.
- Average revenue and marginal revenue answer different questions.
Source links:
- Fortney, Average Revenue section
- OpenStax revenue concepts
40. Q: What is average cost, and how is it related to fixed and variable costs?
A: Show answer
Average cost is total cost divided by output: AC = TC/x for x greater than zero. Since total cost is fixed cost plus variable cost, AC = FC/x + VC/x. The first term is average fixed cost, AFC, and the second is average variable cost, AVC. Therefore AC = AFC + AVC. This decomposition helps a business understand how the fixed-cost burden per unit changes as output changes and how variable cost contributes to the average. Average cost is undefined at zero output because the formula divides by x.
Study instruction:
- Derive AC = AFC + AVC from TC = FC + VC.
- Calculate average fixed and variable cost for a positive production level.
Key point:
- Average cost equals average fixed cost plus average variable cost.
- Average-cost calculations require positive output.
Source links:
- Fortney, Average Cost section
- OpenStax costs and profit
41. Q: How can total cost be recovered from an average-cost function?
A: Show answer
Since AC = TC/x, multiply both sides by x to obtain TC = x·AC. The chapter uses this relationship to reconstruct a total-cost function from a supplied average-cost expression. Once total cost is written in polynomial form, its constant term can represent fixed cost, while terms that depend on x make up variable cost. The marginal-cost function is then found by differentiating total cost. The order matters: first convert average cost to total cost, then identify fixed and variable components, and finally differentiate to obtain marginal cost.
Study instruction:
- Multiply a sample average-cost function by x to obtain total cost.
- Separate the constant term from output-dependent terms and differentiate the result.
Key point:
- TC = x·AC for positive output.
- Differentiate total cost, not average cost, to obtain marginal cost.
Source links:
- Fortney, Examples 2.36–2.37
- OpenStax marginal cost
42. Q: How can fixed and variable costs be identified from a total-cost function?
A: Show answer
Fixed cost does not depend on the output variable, so it appears as the constant part of the total-cost expression. Variable cost depends on output and contains the terms involving x. In the chapter's reconstruction, TC = x^3 − 5x^2 + 15 + 60x, so FC = 15 and VC = x^3 − 5x^2 + 60x. This decomposition follows the model's assumption about which costs remain when output is zero. In an actual business, cost classification depends on the time horizon and operating assumptions, so the mathematical constant term should be interpreted in context.
Study instruction:
- Separate fixed and variable components in a total-cost polynomial.
- Evaluate the model at zero output to check the fixed-cost component.
Key point:
- Fixed cost is independent of output in the model.
- Variable cost changes with the output level.
Source links:
- Fortney, Example 2.37
- OpenStax explicit and implicit costs
43. Q: Why are units and scale especially important in marginal business calculations?
A: Show answer
A derivative inherits the output-units-per-input-unit structure of the original function. If revenue is measured in thousands of dollars and x counts individual widgets, the derivative is thousands of dollars per widget. If population is measured in hundreds and time in years, the derivative is hundreds of people per year. Reporting only the numerical value can lead to errors by factors of 100, 1,000, or more. The chapter repeatedly emphasizes units in business examples because the numerical result must be translated into a meaningful operational statement.
Study instruction:
- Write the units for a derivative when revenue is in thousands of dollars and output is in widgets.
- Convert one derivative value to dollars per widget and state the result in words.
Key point:
- Derivative units are output units divided by input units.
- Preserve every scale factor when interpreting a result.
Source links:
- Fortney, Business Applications
- OpenStax rates of change and marginal units
44. Q: Why should an average rate not be extrapolated beyond the interval where it was calculated?
A: Show answer
An average rate summarizes a particular interval. If the function is nonlinear, the slope can change outside that interval, so the same average rate may not describe a later range. The chapter's revenue example explicitly notes that an estimate based on the interval from 10 to 20 widgets should not be applied to a change from 19 to 21 without further calculation, because 21 lies outside the interval. Even within the interval, the average is only a rough estimate of a local change. Use the actual function or derivative when a more accurate estimate is required.
Study instruction:
- Identify the interval used to calculate an average rate and mark an input outside it.
- Explain why a nonlinear function may have a different rate in the new region.
Key point:
- Average rates are tied to their stated intervals.
- Do not treat an interval average as a universal constant slope.
Source links:
- Fortney, Examples 2.27–2.29
- OpenStax average versus instantaneous change
45. Q: What algebraic steps are most important when deriving a derivative from the difference quotient?
A: Show answer
Substitute x + h into the function, expand carefully, subtract the original function, divide by h, factor out h, cancel the common factor, and only then take the limit as h approaches zero. Skipping the subtraction step often causes sign errors, while substituting h = 0 before cancellation creates division by zero. The chapter's examples show why algebraic fluency is essential to calculus: the limit becomes manageable after the difference quotient is simplified. Each step should preserve equivalence for nonzero h near zero.
Study instruction:
- Derive the derivative of x^2 using all six steps and annotate each transformation.
- Identify the first invalid step in a worked solution that substitutes h = 0 too early.
Key point:
- Simplify the quotient before taking the limit.
- Never divide by zero or substitute h = 0 before cancellation.
Source links:
- OpenStax difference quotient process
46. Q: What domain caution applies when using the power rule for real exponents?
A: Show answer
The power rule applies to x^n where the function is defined and differentiable, but the real domain depends on the exponent. Negative exponents exclude x = 0. Even roots restrict the real input to nonnegative values, and derivatives of such functions may have additional restrictions at boundary points. The chapter presents the power rule for real n and emphasizes rewriting roots and reciprocals as powers. A correct solution must still respect the original function's domain and avoid evaluating a derivative at a point where it is not defined.
Study instruction:
- State the real domain of x^(−2), √x, and x^(1/3).
- Identify where each derivative can be evaluated over the real numbers.
Key point:
- A differentiation rule does not remove the original domain restrictions.
- Check where the function and its derivative are defined.
Source links:
- OpenStax power rule and domain conditions
- Fortney, Section 2.3
47. Q: What is the difference between total, average, and marginal business quantities?
A: Show answer
A total quantity records the overall amount, such as total cost TC, total revenue TR, or total profit TP. An average quantity divides a total by output, such as average cost AC = TC/x or average revenue AR = TR/x. A marginal quantity is the derivative of the total, such as MC = TC', MR = TR', or MP = TP'. These measures answer different questions: total tells how much overall, average tells how much per unit across all units, and marginal tells the local rate of change at a particular output level. Confusing them can lead to incorrect pricing or production decisions.
Study instruction:
- Create a three-column comparison for total, average, and marginal cost.
- Write one decision question that each measure could answer.
Key point:
- Total, average, and marginal quantities are not interchangeable.
- Each measure has a different formula and business interpretation.
Source links:
- OpenStax marginal functions
- Fortney, Sections 2.6
48. Q: How can marginal cost, marginal revenue, and marginal profit inform a production decision?
A: Show answer
Marginal cost estimates the additional cost associated with a small increase in output, marginal revenue estimates the additional revenue, and marginal profit estimates the additional profit. Since P(x) = R(x) − C(x), differentiating gives P'(x) = R'(x) − C'(x), or MP = MR − MC. If marginal revenue exceeds marginal cost, the local change in profit is positive; if marginal cost exceeds marginal revenue, the local change in profit is negative. This is a useful decision signal, but a complete production decision also needs demand, capacity, constraints, and the range over which the model is valid.
Study instruction:
- Given MR and MC functions, calculate MP and interpret its sign at two output levels.
- Explain why a positive marginal profit does not by itself prove that the business is earning positive total profit.
Key point:
- MP = MR − MC.
- A positive marginal value describes local improvement, not the total profit level.
Source links:
- OpenStax marginal profit relationship
- OpenStax output and profit decisions
49. Q: How should a derivative value be explained in a business report?
A: Show answer
State the quantity being differentiated, the input level, the numerical derivative, and the units. Then translate the rate into plain language, such as “At 150 units of production, total cost is increasing at approximately $125 per additional unit.” If the result is an approximation to a discrete change, say so explicitly. Avoid describing a marginal value as the total cost, total revenue, or exact cost of the next unit unless the model and calculation justify that interpretation. A concise explanation should also state the relevant domain and production range when those restrictions affect the result.
Study instruction:
- Write a two-sentence interpretation of a marginal-cost result with correct units.
- Revise a vague statement that reports only a number and no production level.
Key point:
- A derivative report needs context, input level, units, and interpretation.
- Distinguish a local rate from an exact finite change.
Source links:
- OpenStax interpreting marginal rates
- Fortney, Chapter 2
50. Q: What study sequence best consolidates the chapter's ideas?
A: Show answer
Begin with average rates and practice evaluating functions at two endpoints. Connect the resulting quotient to the slope of a secant line. Then form the difference quotient, simplify it algebraically, and take the limit to obtain the derivative. Learn the basic derivative rules and apply them term by term to polynomials. Finally, interpret the derivative as a tangent slope and as a marginal business quantity, preserving units and distinguishing exact changes from approximations. This sequence follows the chapter's progression from finite changes to local change and then to business decisions.
Study instruction:
- Solve one average-rate problem, one limit-definition derivative, one polynomial derivative, and one marginal-cost interpretation without looking at the worked examples.
- For each answer, state the formula, units, and business meaning where relevant.
Key point:
- The derivative links algebraic limits, geometric slopes, and marginal business measures.
- Understanding the interpretation is as important as calculating the derivative.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 2
- OpenStax Calculus Volume 1, Derivative Key Concepts

Table 3
Exponential and Logarithmic Derivatives in Business and Economics
1. Q: What are exponential functions, and how does their structure distinguish them from polynomial functions?
A: Show answer
An exponential function has the variable in the exponent, commonly written f(x)=b^x, with a positive base b. A coefficient may also multiply the expression, such as c·b^x. In a polynomial, the variable is raised to fixed powers and the coefficients are constants; in an exponential function, the base is fixed and the exponent varies. This distinction matters because exponential change compounds multiplicatively: equal increments in x multiply the function by the same factor. The model is useful for growth, decay, interest, adoption, populations, and other processes whose change is proportional to the current quantity. Its parameters and time units must be interpreted before applying it.
Study instruction:
- Classify several expressions as polynomial, exponential, or mixed, and explain which quantity is the exponent.
Key point:
- The variable in the exponent creates multiplicative change across equal intervals.
- A positive base is required for the real-valued exponential function b^x across real x.
Source links:
-
2. Q: What restrictions apply to the base and coefficient of an exponential function?
A: Show answer
For f(x)=b^x, the base must satisfy b>0 so the function is defined as a real-valued expression for every real x. The base b=1 is permitted but produces the constant function 1, not growth or decay. A prefactor c in c·b^x can be any real constant; it scales the function and may make its values negative or zero if c is negative or zero, even though b^x itself is positive. The coefficient affects the vertical scale and sign, while the base determines the multiplicative behavior and whether the underlying exponential rises, falls, or is constant.
Study instruction:
- Check the domain of b^x and distinguish the base restriction from restrictions on a coefficient.
Key point:
- b must be positive; b=1 gives a constant exponential.
- The coefficient changes scale and sign but not the base-driven pattern.
Source links:
-
3. Q: How do exponential functions behave when the base is greater than one?
A: Show answer
When b>1, b^x is positive for every real x, passes through (0,1), and increases as x increases. Its derivative is ln(b)b^x, which is positive because ln(b)>0 and b^x>0. The graph approaches zero as x tends toward negative infinity and grows without bound as x tends toward positive infinity. For c·b^x, the underlying growth factor remains b, although a negative coefficient reflects the graph vertically and changes the derivative's sign.
Study instruction:
- Sketch b^x for b>1 and annotate the intercept, limiting behavior, and derivative sign.
Key point:
- b>1 produces increasing exponential growth.
- The graph always passes through (0,1) before any coefficient is applied.
Source links:
-
4. Q: What happens when the exponential base is exactly one?
A: Show answer
The function 1^x equals 1 for every real x, so its graph is a horizontal line at y=1. Its derivative is zero. It is neither increasing nor decreasing and does not model exponential growth or decay. This boundary case is useful when interpreting parameter values: a growth model with a multiplicative factor of one represents no change per period, rather than a positive or negative growth rate.
Study instruction:
- Compare 1^x with 1.05^x and 0.95^x over several values of x.
Key point:
- A base of one creates a constant function.
- The derivative is zero, so there is no exponential change.
Source links:
-

A: Show answer
If 0Study instruction:

Key point:
- A base between zero and one creates decreasing exponential behavior.
- Decay is multiplicative retention rather than subtraction of a fixed amount.

6. Q: Why are b^x and (1/b)^x reflections across the y-axis?
A: Show answer
For a positive base b, the reciprocal-base expression satisfies (1/b)^x=b^{-x}. Replacing x with -x in b^x produces a reflection across the y-axis. Thus, b^x and b^{-x} share the value 1 at x=0, but their directions of change are opposite when b>1. The relationship helps compare growth and decay forms and can simplify algebraic transformations or graph interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Use the identity b^{-x}=1/b^x to verify the reflection for two numerical values of x.
Key point:
- Reciprocal bases reverse the exponent's sign.
- The y-axis reflection preserves the intercept at one.
Source links:

7. Q: What is the number e, and why is it central to exponential calculus?
A: Show answer
The number e is a mathematical constant approximately 2.71828, defined through several equivalent constructions, including the limiting expression (1+1/n)^n as n grows without bound. The function e^x is especially important because its derivative is itself: d(e^x)/dx=e^x. This makes it a natural representation of processes whose instantaneous rate is proportional to the current level. It also simplifies continuous compounding, differential equations, logarithmic transformations, and continuously compounded returns.
Study instruction:
- Explain the limiting definition of e and demonstrate why e^x is convenient when differentiating.
Key point:
- e is the base whose exponential function has derivative equal to itself.
- It naturally appears in continuous-rate models.
Source links:
-
8. Q: What is a logarithm, and how does it reverse exponentiation?
A: Show answer
A logarithm answers the question: what exponent is needed to obtain a given value from a specified base? If b^y=x, then y=log_b(x). For example, log_2(16)=4 because 2^4=16. Logarithms are inverse functions of exponentials and convert an unknown exponent into an algebraic quantity. In applications, this allows solving for time, growth rates, or other parameters that appear in an exponent. The base must be positive and not equal to one, and the argument must be positive.
Study instruction:
- Translate three exponential equations into logarithmic form and back, checking each result.
Key point:
- A logarithm identifies the exponent needed to reach a value.
- Logarithms are inverse functions of exponential expressions.
Source links:
-
9. Q: Why must a logarithm's base be positive and different from one?
A: Show answer
A real logarithm log_b(x) requires b>0 and b≠1. A positive base allows real exponential powers to be defined consistently, while base one is unsuitable because 1^y is always one and cannot generate other positive values. The argument x must also be positive because a positive real base raised to a real power produces positive values. These restrictions matter when solving equations: algebraic manipulations may create candidate values that lie outside the logarithm's domain and must be rejected.
Study instruction:
- List the admissible base and argument conditions before solving a logarithmic equation.
Key point:
- The base is positive and not one; the argument is positive.
- Domain checks are part of solving, not an optional final step.
Source links:
-
10. Q: What are the principal logarithm rules, and what conditions should accompany them?
A: Show answer
For positive arguments and a valid base, log_b(1)=0, log_b(b)=1, log_b(b^n)=n, log_b(x^n)=n log_b(x), log_b(xy)=log_b(x)+log_b(y), and log_b(x/y)=log_b(x)-log_b(y). The product and quotient rules transform multiplication and division into addition and subtraction. The power rule brings an exponent outside. These rules should not be used to split a logarithm of a sum, such as log_b(x+y); no corresponding general identity exists. Domain conditions must be maintained when expressions are rearranged.
Study instruction:
- Practice expanding and condensing logarithmic expressions, and identify an invalid split of a logarithm of a sum.
Key point:
- Logarithms convert products into sums and powers into coefficients.
- There is no rule that separates log(x+y) into log(x)+log(y).
Source links:
-
11. Q: What is the natural logarithm, and how is it related to e?
A: Show answer
The natural logarithm is ln(x)=log_e(x). It is the inverse of the exponential function e^x, so ln(e^u)=u and e^{ln(x)}=x for x>0. The natural logarithm is used frequently in calculus, economics, finance, and statistics because its derivative is simple and it converts exponential equations into linear expressions in an exponent. Its domain is x>0. The notation ln should be distinguished from a common logarithm, log base 10, when a problem specifies the base.
Study instruction:
- Use inverse identities to simplify expressions and explain the positive-domain restriction of ln(x).
Key point:
- ln is logarithm base e and reverses e^x.
- The inverse identity e^{ln(x)}=x requires x>0.
Source links:

12. Q: How can logarithms be used to solve an equation with an unknown exponent?
A: Show answer
If an equation has the form a=b^x with positive a and b≠1, take logarithms of both sides: ln(a)=ln(b^x)=x ln(b). Then x=ln(a)/ln(b). The same method works with any valid logarithm base. For example, solving 7=4^x gives x=ln(7)/ln(4), approximately 1.4037. The method isolates the exponent and avoids trial-and-error. Check the resulting value by substituting it into the original equation.
Study instruction:
- Solve an exponential equation by taking logarithms and verify the answer numerically.
Key point:
- Taking logarithms brings an exponent down as a multiplier.
- Divide by ln(b) to isolate the unknown exponent.
Source links:

13. Q: What is the domain and monotonic behavior of logarithmic functions with base greater than one?
A: Show answer
For log_b(x) with b>1, the domain is x>0, the function passes through (1,0), and it increases. Its derivative is 1/(x ln(b)), which is positive for x>0 because ln(b)>0. The graph approaches negative infinity as x approaches zero from the right and grows slowly as x increases. This behavior makes logarithms useful for compressing scales and solving exponential equations, but the domain restriction must be respected in models and calculations.
Study instruction:
- Draw log_b(x) for b>1 and mark its domain, intercept, and limiting behavior.
Key point:
- A logarithm with base greater than one is increasing on x>0.
- The x-intercept is one and the vertical boundary is x=0.

For 00 and decreases as x increases. Its derivative 1/(x ln(b)) is negative because ln(b)<0. The function still passes through (1,0), but its limiting behavior reverses relative to a base greater than one. These functions can be useful when algebraically expressing an inverse of a decreasing exponential, though the choice of base is less common in practical calculations than ln or log base 10.

15. Q: How are exponential and logarithmic graphs related geometrically?
A: Show answer
Because logarithms and exponentials are inverse functions, their graphs are reflections across the line y=x. The exponential graph b^x has a horizontal asymptote at y=0, while log_b(x) has a vertical asymptote at x=0. The point (0,1) on the exponential graph corresponds to (1,0) on the logarithm graph. This reflection helps transfer domain, range, intercept, and monotonicity information from one function to the other.
Study instruction:
- Plot a function and its inverse conceptually, then identify reflected points and asymptotes.
Key point:
- Inverse-function graphs reflect across y=x.
- Exponential and logarithmic domain and range are exchanged.
Source links:

16. Q: What is the derivative of e^x, and what does it mean geometrically?
A: Show answer
The derivative of e^x is e^x. At every x, the slope of the tangent line equals the function's current value, so the slope is positive and grows as the function grows. This property makes e^x the canonical function for a differential equation in which the instantaneous rate of change equals the level itself. Geometrically, the function is increasing and has a tangent slope that is always equal to its y-coordinate.
Study instruction:
- At x=0 and x=1, compare the function value with the tangent slope of e^x.
Key point:
- The derivative of e^x is e^x.
- Its tangent slope is positive and equals the function value.

18. Q: How should the derivative of an exponential function with a coefficient be handled?
A: Show answer
For f(x)=c·b^x, the constant-multiple rule gives f'(x)=c ln(b)b^x. The coefficient c is carried through the derivative, while the base contributes ln(b). If the coefficient is negative, the function is vertically reflected and its derivative changes sign accordingly. For a sum of exponential terms, differentiate each term separately and combine the results. Do not confuse a coefficient in front of the exponential with the base or exponent.

19. Q: What is the derivative of ln(x), and why is its domain important?
A: Show answer
The derivative of ln(x) is 1/x for x>0. The function is defined only for positive x, so the derivative formula must be used within that domain. As x grows, the derivative decreases, meaning ln(x) continues to increase but at a progressively smaller rate. In economic applications, this often supports interpretation of proportional change or marginal effects, although the meaning of the variable and units must be carefully defined.

20. Q: How is the derivative of a logarithm with a base other than e calculated?
A: Show answer
For b>0 and b≠1, d(log_b(x))/dx=1/(x ln(b)). The formula follows from the change-of-base relationship log_b(x)=ln(x)/ln(b), where ln(b) is a constant. The derivative is positive for bases above one and negative for bases between zero and one. When b=e, ln(b)=1 and the expression becomes 1/x. The domain remains x>0.
Study instruction:

- The derivative is 1 divided by x ln(b).
- The base affects the slope through ln(b).

21. Q: How should derivatives of expressions involving both exponential and logarithmic terms be combined?
A: Show answer
Use linearity of differentiation: differentiate each term independently, applying the appropriate exponential or logarithmic derivative and any constant-multiple rule. For example, a term c·b^x contributes c ln(b)b^x, a term d·ln(x) contributes d/x, and a polynomial term follows its power rule. Keep the domain of logarithmic terms in mind and express the derivative in a form that supports interpretation or subsequent evaluation. This chapter's examples demonstrate combinations such as exponential terms, logarithms, and ordinary powers.

22. Q: What is the difference between exponential growth and exponential decay in a model?
A: Show answer
Growth uses a multiplier greater than one per discrete period or a positive continuous rate; decay uses a multiplier between zero and one or a negative continuous rate. Both change proportionally to the current quantity rather than by a fixed additive amount. A discrete model Q(t)=Q_0(1+r)^t uses a per-period rate r, while a continuous model Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt} uses a continuous rate parameter. The rate and time unit must match. Negative rates in the continuous model indicate decay, not a negative quantity.
Study instruction:
- Translate a positive and a negative rate into discrete and continuous model forms.
Key point:
- Growth and decay are multiplicative processes.
- The time unit and rate convention must match.
Source links:
-
23. Q: How is a discrete percentage-growth model constructed?
A: Show answer
If a quantity begins at Q_0 and grows by r per period, with r expressed as a decimal, then Q(t)=Q_0(1+r)^t. The factor 1+r is the multiplier applied each period. A 0.5% growth rate is r=0.005, so the multiplier is 1.005. For decay, r is negative but must remain greater than -1 for a positive multiplier. The model assumes the same percentage rate is applied consistently each period and does not account for changing conditions unless the model is extended.
Study instruction:
- Construct a ten-period model for a starting quantity of 100 growing at 2% per period.
Key point:
- The discrete growth factor is 1+r.
- Convert percentages to decimals before substitution.
Source links:
-
24. Q: How is continuous growth or decay modeled?
A: Show answer
Continuous growth is modeled by Q(t)=Q_0e^{rt}, where r is a continuous rate per time unit and t is measured in matching units. If r is positive, the quantity grows; if negative, it decays. Differentiation gives Q'(t)=rQ(t), showing the instantaneous rate is proportional to the current level. The model is appropriate when a continuous-rate approximation is justified, but it is not automatically more realistic than a discrete model. Parameter estimation and the underlying assumptions matter.
Study instruction:
- Compare a discrete model and continuous model for the same initial quantity and annual rate.
Key point:
- Continuous change uses Q_0e^{rt}.
- The derivative satisfies Q'=rQ.
Source links:

25. Q: How can a derivative be used to estimate a population's instantaneous growth rate?
A: Show answer
For a population modeled by P(t)=P_0(1+r)^t, the derivative is P'(t)=P_0 ln(1+r)(1+r)^t. Evaluating it at a specified time gives the instantaneous change in population per unit of time. For a continuous model P(t)=P_0e^{rt}, the derivative is rP(t). The units are population per time, such as people per year, and should not be confused with the percentage growth rate. A model's estimate may be rounded but should retain its assumptions and time origin.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate a discrete percentage-growth model and interpret the result at a specified year.
Key point:
- The derivative reports instantaneous change in quantity per time.
- A derivative value is not itself a percentage unless normalized by the level.

26. Q: How should the units of an exponential model and its derivative be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The model's output retains the units of the quantity being modeled, while its derivative has those output units divided by the input's units. If a population is measured in millions and time in years, P'(t) is millions of people per year. A value of 0.0039 million per year is approximately 3,900 people per year. Unit conversion and time-origin clarity are essential to avoid interpreting a derivative as a total or a percentage.
Study instruction:
- Write the units of the function and derivative before calculating a population or market-growth rate.
Key point:
- Derivative units are output units per input unit.
- Convert model scale consistently before interpretation.

27. Q: How does a logistic or sigmoid growth model represent saturation?
A: Show answer
The chapter's sigmoid form is N(t)=L/(1+Ce^{-kt}), with positive L, C, and k in the standard increasing case. L is the upper asymptote or carrying-capacity-like ceiling, and the curve starts below L and approaches it as time increases. The denominator changes over time, so early growth is slow, middle growth accelerates, and later growth slows as saturation is approached. The model represents a finite ceiling unlike an unbounded exponential curve. The assumptions and meaning of L should be checked for the application.
Study instruction:
- Identify L, C, and k in a sigmoid model and explain the long-run behavior.
Key point:
- L controls the upper saturation level.
- Sigmoid growth slows as it approaches its ceiling.
Source links:

28. Q: What is the point of inflection or diminishing-returns point in the chapter's sigmoid model?
A: Show answer
For N(t)=L/(1+Ce^{-kt}), the inflection occurs at t=ln(C)/k when C>0 and k>0. At this time, N(t)=L/2 and the growth rate is at its maximum. Before the inflection, the growth rate increases; after it, the growth rate decreases. The point marks a change in curvature rather than the point where growth stops. The population or market continues to increase toward L after this point, but the incremental rate becomes smaller.
Study instruction:
- Derive or verify the inflection time and half-ceiling value for a sigmoid curve.
Key point:
- Inflection time is ln(C)/k for the standard model.
- The level at the inflection is L/2 and the slope is greatest.

29. Q: How can a sigmoid model be used to analyze epidemic or technology-adoption data?
A: Show answer
A sigmoid model can estimate the total ceiling, the timing of rapid growth, and the number of cases or adopters at a specified time. The model's ceiling L is the long-run maximum, while ln(C)/k gives the time of the inflection in the standard form. The chapter illustrates infection counts and market penetration. In real forecasting, data quality, changing behavior, interventions, definitions of cases or adoption, and whether the ceiling is stable all matter. A fitted sigmoid is a descriptive model, not a guarantee that the process will follow its curve.
Study instruction:
- Use a sigmoid model to interpret a ceiling, an inflection date, and a forecast, and list assumptions to test.
Key point:
- The model separates early growth, rapid growth, and saturation phases.
- Forecasts depend on a stable ceiling and model fit.

30. Q: What is the difference between a sigmoid growth curve and unbounded exponential growth?
A: Show answer
Unbounded exponential growth multiplies a quantity at a constant proportional rate and continues without a finite ceiling. A sigmoid model initially may resemble exponential growth, but its growth rate eventually slows and it approaches an upper limit L. The sigmoid's inflection is the point of maximum growth, while exponential growth has a rate that increases indefinitely with the quantity. The distinction is important when a market, epidemic, or population has a finite capacity or potential number of participants.
Study instruction:
- Plot both models conceptually and identify the stage where their predictions diverge.
Key point:
- Exponential growth has no finite ceiling.
- Sigmoid growth incorporates saturation and a declining growth rate after inflection.

31. Q: How should logarithmic growth models in the chapter be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a sigmoid form N(t)=L/(1+Ce^{-kt}) under its logarithmic-growth discussion. The parameters determine the ceiling, starting position, and speed of transition. The expression is not the elementary logarithm function ln(x); it is a logistic-style curve containing an exponential term. Its inflection time is ln(C)/k and its height there is L/2. The distinction in terminology prevents confusing a sigmoid model with a simple logarithmic function such as ln(t).
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the sigmoid expression from a simple logarithmic function and identify each domain.

32. Q: What is compound interest, and how does the periodic-compounding formula work?
A: Show answer
For principal P_0, annual nominal rate r, m compounding periods per year, and t years, the amount is A(t)=P_0(1+r/m)^{mt}. The rate r must be expressed as a decimal and m determines the number of periods per year. The factor 1+r/m is the growth factor for each compounding period, and mt is the total number of periods. This formula assumes the stated rate and compounding frequency apply throughout the investment horizon and that interest is retained in the account.
Study instruction:
- Identify P_0, r, m, and t in a compound-interest problem before calculating.
Key point:
- Periodic compounding uses (1+r/m)^{mt}.
- The nominal rate is divided by the compounding frequency.
Source links:
-
33. Q: Why does the compounding frequency affect the final amount?
A: Show answer
When a nominal annual rate is compounded more frequently, interest is added to the balance sooner and subsequent interest can accrue on that added interest. For a fixed positive nominal rate and time horizon, increasing m generally increases the amount, approaching the continuous-compounding limit. The effect is usually smaller than the effect of changing the rate or duration, but it matters when comparing products. The comparison assumes the same nominal rate, time horizon, fees, and other conditions.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the same principal and rate under annual, quarterly, and monthly compounding and compare the balances.
Key point:
- More frequent compounding increases the amount under a fixed positive nominal rate.
- Compare products using a common horizon and consistent assumptions.
Source links:
-
34. Q: How is continuous compounding derived from periodic compounding?
A: Show answer
The periodic formula is P_0(1+r/m)^{mt}. As m grows without bound, the compounding intervals become increasingly small and the expression approaches P_0e^{rt}. This limit follows from the defining limit for e and represents continuous compounding. It is a mathematical limit, not a claim that a financial institution literally credits interest infinitely many times. Continuous compounding is useful as a model and a comparison benchmark.
Study instruction:
- Explain the limit of (1+r/m)^{mt} as m tends toward infinity.
Key point:
- Periodic compounding converges to P_0e^{rt}.
- Continuous compounding is the limiting mathematical model.
Source links:
-
35. Q: What is simple interest, and how does it differ from compound interest?
A: Show answer
Simple interest accrues only on the original principal, so A(t)=P_0(1+rt) and interest earned is I(t)=P_0rt. Compound interest accrues on the principal and previously credited interest, producing a multiplicative growth formula. For positive rates and time, compound interest generally yields more than simple interest over multiple periods. The choice of formula depends on the contract, not on which result is more favorable. The rate and time units must still match.
Study instruction:
- Compare simple and compound interest for the same principal, rate, and time period.
Key point:
- Simple interest is linear in time.
- Compound interest earns interest on accrued interest.
Source links:
-
36. Q: How can the amount under compound interest be solved for an unknown initial deposit?
A: Show answer
Rearrange A=P_0(1+r/m)^{mt} to isolate P_0: P_0=A/(1+r/m)^{mt}. The future amount, rate, compounding frequency, and horizon must be known. This is a present-value calculation under the specified rate convention. The result is the required initial deposit before considering fees, taxes, inflation, risk, or other cash flows. If there are multiple cash flows, a single deposit formula is not sufficient.
Study instruction:
- Solve for the initial deposit needed to reach a target future amount and verify by substitution.
Key point:
- Present value is the future amount divided by the accumulation factor.
- Additional cash flows or fees require a broader model.
Source links:

37. Q: How can a periodic-compounding equation be solved for an unknown interest rate?
A: Show answer
Starting from A=P_0(1+r/m)^{mt}, divide by P_0, take the power 1/(mt), subtract one, and multiply by m: r=m[(A/P_0)^{1/(mt)}-1]. This produces the nominal annual rate consistent with the stated compounding frequency. It is important to distinguish that rate from an effective annual rate and to use the correct total number of periods. The result is sensitive to the assumptions about timing and cash flows.
Study instruction:
- Rearrange the compound-interest formula to solve for r and label it as a nominal annual rate.
Key point:
- The algebra isolates the per-period factor before converting to the nominal annual rate.
- The compounding frequency is part of the rate definition.

38. Q: How can logarithms solve for the time required to reach a financial target?
A: Show answer
For periodic compounding, A=P_0(1+r/m)^{mt}. After dividing by P_0 and taking logarithms, t=ln(A/P_0)/(m ln(1+r/m)). For continuous compounding, A=P_0e^{rt} gives t=ln(A/P_0)/r. These equations are useful for doubling-time or target-value problems. The target must be reachable under the assumptions, and the rate must be positive for growth to a larger target in finite positive time.
Study instruction:
- Solve a doubling-time problem under periodic and continuous compounding and compare the results.
Key point:
- Logarithms isolate time in an exponential accumulation equation.
- Check that the target and rate make a positive, meaningful time.

39. Q: How should annual rates and monthly rates be converted for a loan calculation?
A: Show answer
For a loan with monthly payments, the periodic rate used in the formula must match the monthly payment interval. A nominal annual rate compounded monthly is often divided by 12 to obtain a monthly rate, while the total number of payments is 12 times the number of years. Contract terms may specify an effective rate or different conventions, so the exact conversion must be checked. Dividing an annual percentage by 12 is not appropriate for every rate convention.
Study instruction:
- Convert a stated annual nominal rate to a monthly rate and identify the contract convention.
Key point:
- The periodic rate must match the payment interval.
- Rate conversion depends on whether the annual rate is nominal or effective.

40. Q: How does the chapter's loan-repayment formula calculate a fixed monthly payment?
A: Show answer
For principal P, monthly rate r, and n monthly payments, the fixed payment is PMT=P·r(1+r)^n/[(1+r)^n-1]. The payment amortizes the balance through a sequence of interest charges and principal reductions. For a term of t years, n=12t. The formula assumes regular payments, a constant monthly rate, and a standard amortizing structure; fees, insurance, balloon payments, irregular dates, or different repayment arrangements may alter the actual schedule.
Study instruction:
- Substitute principal, monthly rate, and total payments into the fixed-payment formula and check units.
Key point:
- The amortizing payment depends on principal, rate, and number of payments.
- A constant-rate, regular-payment assumption underlies the formula.
