41. Q: What is an effective annual interest rate, and why is it useful?
A: Show answer
The effective annual rate expresses the actual annual accumulation from a nominal rate compounded m times in a year. For nominal r, the effective rate is (1+r/m)^m-1. It allows comparison across products with different compounding frequencies by converting them to a common annual accumulation. The comparison assumes the rates and compounding terms are comparable and does not incorporate fees, taxes, risk, liquidity, or other contract features. A quoted nominal rate alone can be misleading when frequencies differ.
Study instruction:
- Calculate effective annual rates for two compounding frequencies and compare them on the same basis.
Key point:
- Effective annual rate incorporates the compounding frequency.
- Compare rates on a common annual basis, then consider fees and risk.

42. Q: How is the effective annual rate calculated for continuous compounding?
A: Show answer
If the nominal rate is continuously compounded at r, the effective annual rate is e^r-1. This is the annual accumulation factor e^r converted to a percentage gain above the initial principal. It is useful for comparing continuous rates with periodically compounded products. The effective rate should be calculated with r in decimal form, and the comparison should include any relevant fees or risk factors outside the mathematical formula.
Study instruction:
- Convert a continuously compounded rate into an effective annual rate and explain the result.
Key point:
- Continuous effective rate is e^r-1.
- The rate r is a decimal in the formula.

43. Q: How can two investment opportunities with different compounding frequencies be compared fairly?
A: Show answer
Convert each quoted rate into an effective annual rate or calculate the accumulated value over the same horizon using identical principal and timing assumptions. A higher nominal rate does not automatically produce a higher return if compounding frequency differs. The chapter's examples illustrate that the comparison should use the amount accrued or the effective annual rate. In practice, also compare fees, taxes, credit risk, withdrawal conditions, liquidity, and whether the rates are fixed or variable.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- Effective rates normalize compounding frequency.

44. Q: What is a continuously compounded return, and how is it calculated from prices?
A: Show answer
For a price P_t at the beginning of a period and P_{t+1} at the end, the continuously compounded return is r_cc=ln(P_{t+1}/P_t). It follows from the continuous accumulation equation when the initial and final prices are treated as a one-period investment. The return is additive across consecutive periods when prices are linked, which is one reason it is used in financial analysis. It is not the same as the simple percentage return, though the two are close for small changes.
Study instruction:
- Calculate a log return from two prices and compare it with the simple percentage return.
Key point:
- Log return is ln(final price divided by initial price).
- It is derived from continuous compounding and differs from simple return.

45. Q: How does a logarithmic return compare with a simple percentage return?
A: Show answer
The simple return is (P_{t+1}-P_t)/P_t, while the log return is ln(P_{t+1}/P_t). For small relative price changes, the two are close because ln(1+x) is approximately x. For larger changes, they differ. Log returns add across periods when the price ratios are multiplied, while simple returns compound multiplicatively. The interpretation and aggregation method should match the analysis, and neither measure by itself accounts for dividends, costs, or risk unless those are included in the price or return definition.
Study instruction:
- Compute both returns for a price change and identify when their approximations diverge.
Key point:
- Log returns approximate simple returns for small changes.
- Log returns add across linked periods; simple returns compound.

A: Show answer
Logarithmic transformations can make relationships easier to model, compress scale, or convert multiplicative relationships into additive ones. The chapter notes their use in econometrics and log-linear models. A log transformation changes interpretation: coefficients may represent proportional or elasticity-like relationships depending on which variables are logged and the model form. The data must be positive for a natural logarithm, and transformed results should be interpreted carefully rather than assumed to be linear in the original units.

- Log transformations change the scale and interpretation of a relationship.

47. Q: How can logarithmic cost functions be used to calculate marginal cost?
A: Show answer
If C(x)=a+b ln(x) for x>0, then C'(x)=b/x. The derivative represents marginal cost in the model's units, or the approximate change in total cost from a small increase in output near x. For example, with C(x)=500+150 ln(x), the marginal cost at x=100 is 150/100=1.5 cost units per additional unit of output. The interpretation depends on how x is scaled and on the model's validity at that output level.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate a logarithmic cost function and evaluate its marginal cost at a specified output.
Key point:
- The derivative of b ln(x) is b/x.
- Marginal cost is local and depends on the variable's units.

48. Q: How is a break-even point determined when cost and revenue functions are given?
A: Show answer
Break-even occurs where total cost equals total revenue, C(x)=R(x). If the equation contains logarithms and linear terms, it may not have a simple closed-form solution. Graphing, numerical methods, or a spreadsheet solver can approximate the intersection. The units and domain must be checked, and the solution should be interpreted as the quantity where profit is zero under the modeled assumptions. Multiple intersections or no intersection may occur depending on the functions.
Study instruction:
- Set cost equal to revenue, identify the valid domain, and verify a numerical solution by substitution.
Key point:
- Break-even equates total cost and total revenue.
- Numerical solutions require domain and approximation checks.

50. Q: How can an exponential demand function be used to interpret price sensitivity?
A: Show answer
If demand is D(p)=A e^{-kp} with positive A and k, the derivative is D'(p)=-kA e^{-kp}=-kD(p). The derivative is negative, indicating that the model predicts demand decreases as price rises. The magnitude depends on the current price and demand level. A derivative measures local change in quantity per unit of price, while elasticity measures relative responsiveness and is a distinct concept. The model should be tested against actual data before it informs pricing.

51. Q: What assumptions and limitations should be checked before applying growth, interest, or demand models?
A: Show answer
Models simplify reality and require parameter and domain assumptions. Growth models may assume a constant rate; interest formulas may omit fees, taxes, withdrawals, or changing rates; sigmoid models may assume a stable ceiling; logarithmic models require positive inputs; and demand functions may omit competition, seasonality, supply constraints, or consumer behavior changes. A calculated result should be presented as conditional on the model rather than as a guaranteed forecast. Validate the model against observed data and consider sensitivity to parameters.
Study instruction:

Use a calculator or spreadsheet to verify numerical substitution, but retain the mathematical structure so the result can be independently reviewed. A plausible-looking number can still arise from a wrong rate convention or exponent.

54. Q: What is the practical difference between a rate, a level, and a derivative in the chapter's applications?
A: Show answer
A level is the modeled quantity at a point in time or output, such as a population or account balance. A rate parameter describes the proportional change built into a model, such as r in Q_0e^{rt}. A derivative is the instantaneous absolute change of the modeled quantity with respect to the independent variable, with units such as people per year or dollars per unit. These concepts are related but not interchangeable: the derivative may depend on the current level, and a rate parameter may be a percentage per time rather than a quantity per time.
Study instruction:
- Explain the level, rate parameter, and derivative using one population model.
Key point:
- Levels, proportional rates, and derivatives are distinct quantities.
- A derivative has units and depends on the model's independent variable.

Table 4
Rules of Differentiation and Business Applications
1. Q: What is the purpose of the differentiation rules introduced in Chapter 4?
A: Show answer
Chapter 4 develops practical rules for differentiating products, quotients, and composite functions, then applies those rules to business and economic models. The rules extend the basic derivative techniques introduced earlier so that more complicated expressions can be differentiated without repeatedly returning to the limit definition. The chapter emphasizes recognizing the structure of a function, selecting the appropriate rule, differentiating each component correctly, and interpreting the result in context. Its business examples use derivatives to analyze marginal changes and to identify candidate quantities or prices that may minimize cost or maximize revenue. The central skill is not memorizing isolated formulas; it is matching a function's structure to a rule and checking that the resulting derivative is meaningful.
Study instruction:
- "Map each chapter section to its main rule and prepare one example showing when that rule is needed. "
Key point:
- Product, quotient, and chain rules extend differentiation to more complex functions.
- Business applications interpret derivatives as rates of change and optimization tools.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
2. Q: What is the product rule, and when should it be used?
A: Show answer
Use the product rule when a function is expressed as the product of two differentiable functions. If F(x) = f(x)g(x), then F'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x). Differentiate the first factor while leaving the second unchanged, then add the first factor multiplied by the derivative of the second. Both terms are necessary. The rule applies whether the factors are polynomials, exponential functions, logarithms, or combinations of these. For example, x²eˣ is a product of x² and eˣ, so its derivative is 2xeˣ + x²eˣ. The product rule is not a rule for multiplying the derivatives; it accounts for the change in each factor while the other factor is present.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the two factors in five expressions and write f, g, f', and g' before applying the formula. "
Key point:
- Differentiate one factor at a time and retain the other factor.
- Add the two resulting terms; do not multiply f' by g' alone.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
3. Q: How should the two factors be identified before applying the product rule?
A: Show answer
Rewrite the expression mentally as f(x) multiplied by g(x), then choose labels that make each derivative straightforward. The labels are interchangeable: selecting u = x² and v = eˣ produces the same result as selecting u = eˣ and v = x². A useful choice is often to assign each visibly distinct factor to a separate function and differentiate them independently. Check that the original expression is exactly u times v before proceeding. Do not treat a sum as a product merely because it contains several terms; for an expression such as x² + eˣ, the sum rule applies instead. When a factor itself contains a sum or composition, differentiate that factor using the relevant additional rule.
Study instruction:
- "For each practice expression, state why it is a product and identify the two functions before differentiating. "
Key point:
- Correctly recognizing the function structure is the first step in choosing a rule.
- The labels u and v are arbitrary, but the factors must reproduce the original function.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
4. Q: Why is the derivative of a product not simply the product of the derivatives?
A: Show answer
The rate of change of a product depends on the change in both factors, not only on the product of their separate rates of change. The product rule captures two contributions: the change in the first factor multiplied by the current second factor, and the current first factor multiplied by the change in the second. For instance, if one factor is increasing while the other is constant, the derivative must still reflect the changing factor. Multiplying f' by g' would instead combine two rates and generally give the wrong result and units. A quick check is to differentiate x times x: the product rule gives 1·x + x·1 = 2x, whereas multiplying the derivatives would give 1, which is not the derivative of x².
Study instruction:
- "Verify the rule by differentiating x·x both as a product and after simplifying it to x². "
Key point:
- A product changes through the change in either factor.
- The product of the derivatives is not the product rule.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
5. Q: How is the derivative of y = x²eˣ found using the product rule?
A: Show answer
Let f(x) = x² and g(x) = eˣ. Their derivatives are f'(x) = 2x and g'(x) = eˣ. Substitution gives y' = 2x·eˣ + x²·eˣ. Since both terms contain eˣ, factor it out to obtain y' = eˣ(x² + 2x). The factorized expression is equivalent to the unsimplified two-term expression and makes the common exponential factor clear. The method illustrates an important habit: differentiate each factor independently first, apply the rule, and simplify only after the derivative has been assembled. The exponential factor is never zero, but no division by it is needed to state the derivative.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the calculation without looking at the solution and explain where each of the two terms comes from. "
Key point:
- The derivative is eˣ(x² + 2x).
- Factoring common terms is optional algebraic simplification, not a separate differentiation rule.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
6. Q: How do you differentiate y = 3x² ln(x)?
A: Show answer
Treat 3x² and ln(x) as the two factors. The derivative of 3x² is 6x, and the derivative of ln(x) is 1/x for x greater than zero. The product rule gives y' = 6x ln(x) + 3x²(1/x), which simplifies to 6x ln(x) + 3x. Factoring 3x gives y' = 3x(2 ln(x) + 1). The logarithm's domain matters: the original real-valued function is defined for x greater than zero, so the derivative is considered on that domain. This example also shows why it is important not to omit the coefficient 3 when differentiating the polynomial factor.
Study instruction:
- "Practice differentiating a polynomial multiplied by a logarithm and state the domain inherited from ln(x). "
Key point:
- Apply the product rule before simplifying the term containing 1/x.
- The real-valued domain is x greater than zero.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
7. Q: What is the derivative of F(x) = eˣ ln(x), and what does the calculation demonstrate?
A: Show answer
Set f(x) = eˣ and g(x) = ln(x). Then f'(x) = eˣ and g'(x) = 1/x. Applying the product rule gives F'(x) = eˣ ln(x) + eˣ/x. Factoring out eˣ produces F'(x) = eˣ(ln(x) + 1/x). The calculation demonstrates that the product rule works even when the factors have different functional forms. It also illustrates that the derivative of a logarithm contributes a reciprocal term, which must remain multiplied by the other original factor. The expression is defined for x greater than zero because ln(x) is part of the original function.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate eˣ ln(x) using both the expanded and factorized forms and check that they are algebraically equivalent. "
Key point:
- F'(x) = eˣ ln(x) + eˣ/x.
- Keep the original undifferentiated factor in each product-rule term.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
8. Q: How is the product rule applied when both factors are polynomials?
A: Show answer
Differentiate each polynomial separately, then substitute the results into f'g + fg'. For F(x) = (x² − 7x)(3x⁴ + 5x³ − 2x²), the derivatives of the factors are 2x − 7 and 12x³ + 15x² − 4x. Therefore F'(x) = (2x − 7)(3x⁴ + 5x³ − 2x²) + (x² − 7x)(12x³ + 15x² − 4x). This factored form is already a correct derivative. If expansion is required, distribute each product carefully and collect like powers; the result is 18x⁵ − 80x⁴ − 148x³ + 42x². Keeping the factored form during the differentiation step reduces avoidable algebra errors.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the two factors first, then expand the final expression independently to verify the polynomial result. "
Key point:
- Product-rule differentiation can be completed before any polynomial expansion.
- Simplification should preserve algebraic equivalence.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
9. Q: What is the main idea behind the limit-based proof of the product rule?
A: Show answer
Start from the derivative definition for f(x)g(x), using the difference quotient [f(x+h)g(x+h) − f(x)g(x)]/h. Add and subtract the intermediate term f(x+h)g(x). This does not change the numerator, but it separates it into two differences: f(x+h)[g(x+h) − g(x)] and g(x)[f(x+h) − f(x)]. After dividing by h, take the limit as h approaches zero. The first contribution approaches f(x)g'(x), and the second approaches g(x)f'(x), using differentiability and continuity of the factors. Adding the contributions gives f'g + fg'. The proof explains why both terms appear, even though routine exercises generally require correct application rather than repeating the proof.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the add-and-subtract step and identify which limit produces each term in the product rule. "
Key point:
- Adding and subtracting the same intermediate expression separates the two sources of change.
- The limit produces f'g + fg'.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
10. Q: When should a product-rule answer be left in factored form rather than fully expanded?
A: Show answer
Leave the answer factored when it is already correct and expansion adds no useful information, especially when the factors are long polynomials or the derivative will be used in a later calculation. For example, (2x + 3)(3x² − 5x + 1) + (x² + 3x + 2)(6x − 5) clearly displays the product-rule structure. Expanding may be appropriate when a problem requests a polynomial in standard form, when coefficients need to be compared, or when a later step requires roots or like terms. Whichever form is chosen, the expression must be algebraically equivalent to the derivative. Simplification is not a substitute for checking the rule itself.
Study instruction:
- "Present one derivative in factored and expanded forms, then state which form is more useful for a specified next step. "
Key point:
- A correct unsimplified derivative is still correct.
- Expand only when it improves interpretation or is required by the task.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
11. Q: What is the quotient rule, and when should it be used?
A: Show answer
Use the quotient rule when a function is the ratio of two differentiable functions and the denominator is nonzero. If F(x) = f(x)/g(x), then F'(x) = [g(x)f'(x) − f(x)g'(x)]/[g(x)]². In words, multiply the denominator by the derivative of the numerator, subtract the numerator multiplied by the derivative of the denominator, and divide by the square of the original denominator. The order of the two numerator terms is important because reversing them changes the sign. The rule is useful for rational functions and expressions such as ln(x)/x² or x/eˣ. The original function's domain must still be respected.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the numerator order and denominator square, then apply the rule to a polynomial ratio and a logarithmic ratio. "
Key point:
- Quotient rule: denominator times numerator derivative minus numerator times denominator derivative, over denominator squared.
- The original denominator cannot be zero.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
12. Q: Why is the denominator squared in the quotient rule, and why does the numerator have a subtraction?
A: Show answer
Both features arise from differentiating a ratio while accounting for changes in the numerator and denominator. The denominator's square appears when the difference quotient is formed and the two nearby denominator values are combined before taking the limit. The subtraction reflects the fact that an increase in the denominator, with the numerator held fixed, tends to reduce the ratio. The numerator's first term captures change in the numerator; the second term captures the effect of change in the denominator. This structure is not optional algebraic decoration. A reliable memory aid is “low d-high minus high d-low, over low squared,” but the formula should still be written explicitly to avoid sign errors.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the meaning of each quotient-rule term and check the sign by differentiating 1/x. "
Key point:
- The denominator is squared in the final formula.
- Reversing the numerator subtraction reverses the derivative's sign.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
13. Q: How do you differentiate y = x/eˣ using the quotient rule?
A: Show answer
Let f(x) = x and g(x) = eˣ. Then f'(x) = 1 and g'(x) = eˣ. The quotient rule gives y' = [eˣ(1) − x(eˣ)]/(eˣ)². Factor eˣ from the numerator to obtain eˣ(1 − x)/(eˣ)², then cancel one factor of eˣ. The result is y' = (1 − x)/eˣ. This example is useful because the quotient rule produces a compact answer after cancellation. It also provides a sign check: the derivative is positive for x less than 1, zero at x = 1, and negative for x greater than 1.
Study instruction:
- "Work through the quotient-rule substitution before cancelling the common exponential factor. "
Key point:
- y' = (1 − x)/eˣ.
- Simplify only after the complete quotient-rule numerator and denominator have been written.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
14. Q: What is the derivative of F(x) = (x² + 1)/(x − 1)?
A: Show answer
Set f(x) = x² + 1 and g(x) = x − 1. Their derivatives are 2x and 1. Applying the quotient rule gives F'(x) = [(x − 1)(2x) − (x² + 1)(1)]/(x − 1)². Expand the numerator: 2x² − 2x − x² − 1 = x² − 2x − 1. Therefore F'(x) = (x² − 2x − 1)/(x − 1)². The original function and derivative are undefined at x = 1, so that value is excluded from the domain. The example illustrates the need to use the original denominator in the quotient-rule formula, not its derivative or a simplified substitute.
Study instruction:
- "Repeat the calculation and mark the excluded value x = 1 before simplifying. "
Key point:
- F'(x) = (x² − 2x − 1)/(x − 1)².
- The denominator restriction x ≠ 1 remains in force.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
15. Q: How is F(x) = ln(x)/x² differentiated?
A: Show answer
Let f(x) = ln(x) and g(x) = x². Then f'(x) = 1/x and g'(x) = 2x. The quotient rule gives F'(x) = [x²(1/x) − ln(x)(2x)]/(x²)². Simplifying the numerator gives x − 2x ln(x), so F'(x) = [x(1 − 2 ln(x))]/x⁴ = (1 − 2 ln(x))/x³. The function is defined for x greater than zero, which is more restrictive than merely requiring the denominator to be nonzero. This example shows how logarithmic differentiation formulas and the quotient rule work together.
Study instruction:
- "Practice simplifying the numerator by factoring x and then cancelling only valid nonzero factors. "
Key point:
- F'(x) = (1 − 2 ln(x))/x³.
- The logarithm restricts the real domain to x greater than zero.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
16. Q: How should a quotient involving a polynomial and an exponential function be differentiated?
A: Show answer
Identify the numerator and denominator as separate functions, differentiate each, and substitute them into the quotient rule. For example, for F(x) = (−7x³ + 5x²)/eˣ, let f = −7x³ + 5x² and g = eˣ. Then f' = −21x² + 10x and g' = eˣ. The numerator becomes eˣ(−21x² + 10x) − (−7x³ + 5x²)eˣ. Dividing by e²ˣ and cancelling one eˣ gives F'(x) = (7x³ − 26x² + 10x)/eˣ. Careful handling of the negative signs is essential when subtracting the second product.
Study instruction:
- "Write the entire quotient-rule numerator with parentheses before distributing the minus sign. "
Key point:
- Parentheses protect the signs in the quotient-rule subtraction.
- Exponential factors may be cancelled only after the full denominator has been formed.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
17. Q: What domain restrictions must be checked when applying the quotient rule?
A: Show answer
The denominator of the original function must be nonzero at every point where the function is evaluated. If the numerator contains ln(x), the logarithm also requires x to be positive in the real-valued setting. If a denominator contains a composite expression, its zero values must be excluded even if later algebraic cancellation appears to remove that factor. A derivative formula is valid only on the domain inherited from the original function, subject to differentiability. For example, cancelling a factor of x from a rational expression does not automatically make the original function defined at x = 0 if the original denominator was zero there. State restrictions before or after differentiation and retain them in the final answer.
Study instruction:
- "For each quotient exercise, determine the original domain before simplifying the derivative. "
Key point:
- Never lose domain restrictions through algebraic cancellation.
- Differentiability is considered only where the original function is defined and the rule's conditions hold.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
18. Q: How can you decide whether a function requires the product rule or the quotient rule?
A: Show answer
Inspect the main operation joining the function's factors. If the function is one factor multiplied by another, the product rule is the natural choice. If it is one function divided by another, the quotient rule applies, provided the denominator is nonzero. Algebraic rewriting may sometimes provide an easier route: x/eˣ can be written as xe⁻ˣ and differentiated with the product rule plus the chain rule. However, a transformation must preserve the domain and be algebraically valid. The best method is the one that is easiest to apply accurately, not the one that appears most sophisticated. Regardless of method, the final derivative should agree with an independent check where feasible.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a mixed set of expressions by their outermost operation and solve two using alternative valid methods. "
Key point:
- Products and quotients have different derivative formulas.
- Algebraic rewriting can simplify a task, but domain and equivalence must be preserved.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
19. Q: What is the chain rule, and what kind of function does it differentiate?
A: Show answer
The chain rule differentiates a composite function, meaning that one function is applied to the output of another. If F(x) = f(g(x)), then F'(x) = f'(g(x))·g'(x), provided the required differentiability conditions hold. The derivative of the outer function is evaluated at the inner function, and then multiplied by the derivative of the inner function. This final factor accounts for how quickly the input to the outer function is changing with x. The chain rule is essential for expressions such as e³ˣ, (6x² + 4x)³, square roots of polynomials, and functions nested through several intermediate variables.
Study instruction:
- "For five composite expressions, identify the inner function, outer function, and derivative of each before substituting. "
Key point:
- Differentiate the outer function while retaining the inner expression, then multiply by the inner derivative.
- The chain rule handles composition, not merely multiplication.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
20. Q: How can the inner and outer functions be identified in a composite expression?
A: Show answer
Work from the outside operation inward. In y = (6x² + 4x)³, the outer function raises an input to the third power, while the inner function forms 6x² + 4x. Let u = 6x² + 4x and y = u³. Then dy/du = 3u² and du/dx = 12x + 4. Multiplying and replacing u gives dy/dx = 3(6x² + 4x)²(12x + 4). The inner function is the complete expression being supplied to the outer operation; it is not necessarily a single term. Clear identification prevents the common error of differentiating only the visible exponent and forgetting the derivative of the inside.
Study instruction:
- "Draw brackets around the complete inner expression in each exercise and label the outside operation separately. "
Key point:
- The inner function is the input to the outer function.
- The derivative of the inner function must multiply the outer derivative.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
21. Q: What does the chain rule look like in Leibniz notation?
A: Show answer
If y depends on an intermediate variable u and u depends on x, the chain rule is written as dy/dx = (dy/du)(du/dx). The notation emphasizes the dependency path: a change in x changes u, and the change in u then changes y. The factors are derivatives with respect to different variables, not ordinary fractions to be cancelled without regard to meaning. The notation is particularly useful when a function is composed of three or more stages, such as demand depending on advertising expenditure that itself depends on time. In that case, multiply the derivative at each link in the dependency chain.
Study instruction:
- "Translate one composite function from f(g(x)) notation into a labelled sequence of intermediate variables and Leibniz derivatives. "
Key point:
- Leibniz notation makes the chain of variable dependencies explicit.
- Each derivative must be evaluated with respect to its stated variable.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
22. Q: How do you differentiate y = e³ˣ using the chain rule?
A: Show answer
Let u = 3x, so y = eᵘ. The derivative of eᵘ with respect to u is eᵘ, and du/dx = 3. The chain rule gives dy/dx = eᵘ·3. Replacing u with 3x produces y' = 3e³ˣ. The factor 3 is essential because the exponent changes three times as fast as x. A frequent mistake is to write only e³ˣ, which differentiates the outer exponential form but omits the rate of change of its exponent. The same method works for e raised to any differentiable inner function: retain the exponential expression and multiply by the derivative of the exponent.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate e to the power of 5x and e to the power of x², showing the inner derivative in each case. "
Key point:
- The derivative of eᵘ is eᵘ, multiplied by du/dx.
- y' = 3e³ˣ.

- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
23. Q: How do you differentiate a polynomial raised to a power, such as f(x) = (6x² + 4x)³?
A: Show answer
Treat the complete polynomial as the inner function u = 6x² + 4x and the cube as the outer function. The outer derivative is 3u², while the inner derivative is 12x + 4. Multiplying gives f'(x) = 3(6x² + 4x)²(12x + 4). There is no need to expand the polynomial square or multiply all terms unless the problem specifically requires an expanded result. This is an example of combining the power rule with the chain rule. The same structure applies to any differentiable inner function raised to a constant power n: multiply n times the inner function to power n − 1 by the inner derivative.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the generalized power-and-chain pattern to a fourth power and a negative power, retaining the inner expression. "
Key point:
- For [g(x)]ⁿ, the derivative is n[g(x)]ⁿ⁻¹g'(x).
- Do not omit the derivative of the inner polynomial.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
24. Q: What is the derivative of f(x) = √(x³ − 9)?
A: Show answer
Rewrite the square root as (x³ − 9)^(1/2). Let u = x³ − 9 and apply the power rule to the outer function: the derivative with respect to u is (1/2)u^(−1/2). The inner derivative is 3x². Multiplying gives f'(x) = (1/2)(x³ − 9)^(−1/2)(3x²), or 3x²/[2√(x³ − 9)]. For real values, the original square root requires x³ − 9 to be nonnegative. At points where the radicand is zero, the displayed derivative formula has a zero denominator and is not defined. Domain and differentiability therefore need to be checked in addition to the algebra.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a radical to fractional-exponent form, differentiate the outer power, and then apply the inner derivative. "
Key point:
- f'(x) = 3x²/[2√(x³ − 9)] where the derivative exists.
- A real square root requires a nonnegative radicand, and the derivative may have a stricter domain.

25. Q: How do you differentiate an exponential function with a base other than e, such as 3^(x² + x)?
A: Show answer
The derivative of a^u, for positive constant base a, is a^u ln(a) multiplied by du/dx. Here u = x² + x, so du/dx = 2x + 1. Therefore the derivative is 3^(x² + x) ln(3)(2x + 1). The logarithmic factor ln(3) is required because the base is 3 rather than e. The chain-rule factor 2x + 1 is also required because the exponent is not simply x. This example combines the derivative rule for an exponential function with a composite exponent. It is important to distinguish the constant base from the variable exponent.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate 5^(2x) and 2^(x³), identifying both the base-related logarithm and the inner derivative. "
Key point:
- d(a^u)/dx = a^u ln(a)u'.
- Both ln(a) and the derivative of the exponent must be included.

26. Q: How is the chain rule applied to a composition of three functions?
A: Show answer
Identify the three dependency stages and multiply the derivative at each stage. In the chapter's business example, demand D depends on an intermediate expression u, u depends on marketing expenditure M, and M depends on time t. The chain is dD/dt = (dD/du)(du/dM)(dM/dt). The example defines D as the square root of 500 + 20M(t), with M(t) = 100t². Thus dD/du = 1/(2√u), du/dM = 20, and dM/dt = 200t. Substituting t = 3 gives 20·600/[2√(500 + 20·900)] ≈ 44.11 units of demand per month.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the dependency chain D ← u ← M ← t and calculate each derivative separately before multiplying. "
Key point:
- A three-stage composition requires multiplying all three linked derivatives.
- The result is approximately 44.11 demand units per month at month 3.

- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
27. Q: Can the chain rule be combined with the product rule or quotient rule?
A: Show answer
Yes. A function may contain both a product or quotient and composite factors. First identify the outer structure and apply the corresponding product or quotient rule. When differentiating an individual factor that is itself a composite function, apply the chain rule to that factor. For example, differentiating x²e^(3x) requires the product rule for x² multiplied by e^(3x), and the chain rule when differentiating e^(3x). The result is 2xe^(3x) + 3x²e^(3x). The order of work can be organized in different ways, but every layer of the function must be differentiated. This layered approach prevents the mistaken belief that only one rule may be used in a problem.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate one product containing a composite exponential factor and annotate where each rule is applied. "
Key point:
- Differentiation rules can be combined when a function has nested structure.
- Apply the chain rule inside the product or quotient calculation wherever required.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
28. Q: What does a derivative represent in a business or economic model?
A: Show answer
A derivative represents the instantaneous rate at which an output changes with respect to an input. If cost C depends on quantity x, C'(x) is marginal cost; if revenue R depends on quantity x, R'(x) is marginal revenue; and if profit P = R − C, then P'(x) = R'(x) − C'(x) is marginal profit. The derivative is a local rate at a specified operating point, not automatically the exact change over a whole additional unit. For a small change, it approximates the change in the dependent variable per unit change in the independent variable. Its units must be stated correctly, such as dollars per item, revenue units per dollar of price, or demand units per month.
Study instruction:
- "For each business function, state the dependent variable, independent variable, derivative meaning, and units. "
Key point:
- A derivative is an instantaneous rate of change at a point.
- Interpret the derivative using the units and meaning of the model variables.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Derivatives as Rates of Change
29. Q: How are derivatives used to find a cost-minimizing or revenue-maximizing quantity?
A: Show answer
The chapter introduces a standard candidate-finding procedure: differentiate the cost or revenue function, set the derivative equal to zero, and solve for the decision variable. A solution to C'(x) = 0 is a stationary candidate for cost minimization; a solution to R'(x) = 0 is a stationary candidate for revenue maximization or minimization. The derivative equation identifies where the instantaneous slope is zero, but it does not by itself establish the type of extremum. The analyst must check the sign of the derivative around the candidate, use a suitable second-derivative test, and consider endpoints and feasible constraints. If quantity must be an integer, compare feasible neighboring values as well.
Study instruction:
- "Solve a cost or revenue optimization problem and add a separate step that verifies the candidate is feasible and is the required extremum. "
Key point:
- Setting the first derivative to zero finds stationary candidates.
- Classification, feasibility, and boundary checks are still necessary.

- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Derivatives as Rates of Change
30. Q: How does the chapter's bulk-purchasing cost model produce an optimal quantity of approximately 316 items?
A: Show answer
The model is C(x) = 50,000/x + 0.5x, where x is the number of items purchased. Differentiating gives C'(x) = −50,000/x² + 0.5. Set the derivative to zero: −50,000/x² + 0.5 = 0, so x² = 100,000 and the positive solution is x ≈ 316.227. Since the decision variable is a number of items, a practical candidate is 316 items. The chapter presents this as the quantity that minimizes the modeled cost. A careful decision-maker should also verify that the stationary point is a minimum and compare nearby feasible integers if the quantity must be whole. The negative mathematical root is not feasible for a purchase quantity.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the derivative and solve the stationary equation, then compare total cost at 316 and adjacent feasible quantities. "
Key point:
- The positive stationary quantity is approximately 316.227 items.
- Operational quantity constraints require a feasible integer decision.

31. Q: How is the cost-minimizing quantity found for C(x) = 500,000/x + 8x?
A: Show answer
Differentiate each term: C'(x) = −500,000/x² + 8. Set C'(x) equal to zero and solve: 8 = 500,000/x², so x² = 62,500 and x = ±250. Because x represents a purchase quantity, the negative solution is not meaningful. The positive stationary candidate is 250 items. This example shows how a reciprocal cost component can fall as quantity increases while a linear component rises. The derivative identifies the point at which their combined marginal effects balance. As with any optimization result, the model's domain and feasible purchasing rules must be checked, and the candidate should be verified as a minimum rather than accepted solely because the first derivative is zero.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the negative root is excluded and verify the positive candidate using the derivative's sign on either side. "
Key point:
- The positive stationary candidate is 250 items.
- A business decision must use the feasible root and verify the extremum.

32. Q: How is a stationary cost quantity found when the cost function is a quotient, such as C(x) = (2x² + 300)/(x + 5)?
A: Show answer
Apply the quotient rule with numerator f(x) = 2x² + 300 and denominator g(x) = x + 5. Then f' = 4x and g' = 1, so C'(x) = [(x + 5)(4x) − (2x² + 300)]/(x + 5)². Simplifying the numerator gives 2x² + 20x − 300. Setting it to zero produces x² + 10x − 150 = 0, with roots approximately 8.23 and −18.23. Only the positive root is meaningful for a quantity. The chapter rounds the business decision to 8 units. For a discrete decision, compare total costs at feasible neighboring integer quantities and respect any minimum or maximum order restrictions.
Study instruction:
- "Re-derive the quotient-rule numerator and compare costs at 8 and 9 units before finalizing a whole-unit order. "
Key point:
- The positive continuous stationary candidate is approximately 8.23.
- The feasible integer choice should be checked against neighboring quantities and business constraints.

33. Q: Why does setting a first derivative equal to zero not automatically prove that a cost or revenue point is a minimum or maximum?
A: Show answer
A zero first derivative identifies a stationary point, where the tangent slope is horizontal, but the point may be a local minimum, a local maximum, or neither. For example, a function can flatten and continue increasing, so a zero derivative alone is insufficient. To classify a candidate, examine the sign of the first derivative immediately before and after it: a change from negative to positive indicates a local minimum, while positive to negative indicates a local maximum. Alternatively, use the second derivative test when its conditions are satisfied. Also check endpoints and feasible boundaries because an absolute optimum may occur there. The chapter explicitly notes that verification of the minimum is deferred to later material, so students should not confuse candidate generation with proof of optimality.
Study instruction:
- "Construct one function with a minimum and one stationary point that is not an extremum, then compare their derivative signs. "
Key point:
- C'(x) = 0 or R'(x) = 0 identifies candidates, not a guaranteed optimum.
- Use a classification test and inspect the feasible domain.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Derivatives as Rates of Change
34. Q: How is the rate of change of demand with respect to price found for D(p) = 100e^(−0.05p)?
A: Show answer
Differentiate the exponential function using the chain rule. The constant 100 remains as a multiplier. The derivative of e^(−0.05p) is e^(−0.05p) multiplied by the derivative of its exponent, −0.05. Therefore D'(p) = 100(−0.05)e^(−0.05p) = −5e^(−0.05p). The derivative is negative for every real price in the model's mathematical domain because the exponential term is positive. It indicates that predicted demand falls as price increases. The magnitude of the derivative depends on the price level, so the same price increase need not produce the same absolute change in demand at every point. The model's economic use also requires a sensible price domain.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the demand function and explain the sign and units of D'(p) at a selected price. "
Key point:
- D'(p) = −5e^(−0.05p).
- The negative derivative indicates falling demand as price rises within the model.

35. Q: How does the chapter find the price that makes revenue stationary when demand is D(p) = 100e^(−0.05p)?
A: Show answer
Revenue equals price multiplied by quantity demanded, so R(p) = pD(p) = 100p e^(−0.05p). This is a product of p and an exponential function, so apply the product rule and the chain rule. The derivative is R'(p) = 100e^(−0.05p) + 100p(−0.05)e^(−0.05p), which factors to 100e^(−0.05p)(1 − 0.05p). Since the exponential factor is never zero, set 1 − 0.05p = 0 and obtain p = 20. This is a stationary revenue price. The derivative changes from positive to negative around 20, so within the model's relevant price range it is a revenue maximum.
Study instruction:
- "Build revenue from price times demand, differentiate using the product rule, and explain why the exponential factor can be removed when solving. "
Key point:
- The stationary price is p = 20.
- The sign change from positive to negative identifies a local revenue maximum.

36. Q: How is the rate of change of profit calculated when R(q) = 200q − q² and C(q) = 500 + 50q at q = 50?
A: Show answer
Profit is revenue minus cost: P(q) = R(q) − C(q) = (200q − q²) − (500 + 50q) = 150q − q² − 500. Differentiate to obtain P'(q) = 150 − 2q. At q = 50, P'(50) = 150 − 100 = 50. The marginal profit at that output is therefore 50 currency units per additional unit of quantity, under the model's units. It suggests that a small increase around q = 50 is associated with an approximate 50-unit increase in profit per additional unit. The derivative is a local approximation; the exact profit change from producing one more item is obtained by evaluating P(51) − P(50).
Study instruction:
- "Calculate P'(50) and separately calculate P(51) − P(50) to distinguish marginal approximation from exact discrete change. "
Key point:
- P'(q) = 150 − 2q and P'(50) = 50.
- Marginal profit approximates, but is not always identical to, the exact next-unit change.

37. Q: How is marginal profit found when revenue contains an exponential function and cost contains q ln(q)?
A: Show answer
The chapter's model has R(q) = 200e^(0.05q) and C(q) = 500 + 50q ln(q). Profit is P(q) = R(q) − C(q). Differentiate the revenue term using the chain rule: its derivative is 10e^(0.05q). Differentiate q ln(q) using the product rule: the derivative is ln(q) + 1. Therefore P'(q) = 10e^(0.05q) − 50 ln(q) − 50. At q = 100, substitution gives approximately 1,203.87 in profit units per additional quantity unit. This value is model-dependent and should be interpreted with the units of revenue, cost, and q. The calculation combines product and chain rules in one business application.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the revenue and cost components separately, then combine them as P' = R' − C'. "
Key point:
- The derivative of q ln(q) is ln(q) + 1.

38. Q: How does the revenue function R(x) = 500x e^(−0.0001x) produce a stationary quantity of 10,000 units?
A: Show answer
Apply the product rule to the product of x and e^(−0.0001x), retaining the constant 500. The derivative is R'(x) = 500e^(−0.0001x) + 500x(−0.0001)e^(−0.0001x). Factor the common term to obtain R'(x) = 500e^(−0.0001x)(1 − 0.0001x). The exponential factor is strictly positive, so the stationary equation reduces to 1 − 0.0001x = 0, giving x = 10,000. The derivative is positive below this value and negative above it, so the function increases and then decreases. Within the model's feasible sales range, 10,000 units is therefore the revenue-maximizing candidate.

- "Derive R'(x), solve its zero, and use the sign of the derivative on both sides to confirm the maximum. "

39. Q: How can a business model measure the rate at which demand changes over time when demand depends on marketing expenditure?
A: Show answer
When demand depends on marketing expenditure M and M depends on time t, demand is a composite function. In the chapter's example, D = √(500 + 20M) and M(t) = 100t². Apply the chain rule through the intermediate variables: dD/dt = [1/(2√(500 + 20M))]·20·200t. At t = 3, M = 900, so dD/dt = 12,000/[2√18,500] ≈ 44.11 demand units per month. This rate combines how demand responds to marketing expenditure with how marketing expenditure changes over time. It is not simply the direct derivative of demand with respect to time unless the intermediate dependency is included.

40. Q: How should a continuous optimum be converted into a practical whole-unit business decision?
A: Show answer
First solve the continuous optimization problem to obtain the stationary candidate. Then apply the actual feasible decision constraints. If the variable represents units that cannot be fractional, the final choice must be an integer. Compare the objective function at the nearest feasible integers around the continuous solution, and also inspect any relevant minimum order, capacity, budget, or contract limits. Rounding to the nearest integer is a useful initial step but is not a proof that the rounded value is best. For example, a continuous minimum near 8.23 suggests checking 8 and 9 units, while a quantity near 316.23 suggests checking 316 and 317. Select the feasible quantity with the better objective value.
Study instruction:
- "For a non-integer optimum, calculate the objective at both adjacent feasible integers and document the constraint applied. "
Key point:
- Continuous calculus identifies a candidate; operational decisions must respect discrete constraints.
- Compare nearby feasible choices rather than relying on rounding alone.

41. Q: What common mistakes should be checked when differentiating products, quotients, and composite functions?
A: Show answer
Common product-rule mistakes include multiplying the derivatives instead of using f'g + fg', omitting one term, or differentiating both factors in the same term. Quotient-rule mistakes include reversing the numerator subtraction, forgetting to square the denominator, or losing parentheses around a negative numerator. Chain-rule mistakes include differentiating only the outer function and omitting the inner derivative, or failing to identify the complete inner expression. Algebraic errors can also occur when expanding, factoring, or cancelling. A final check should confirm that the correct structural rule was selected, every factor was differentiated with respect to the right variable, signs and exponents are correct, and domain restrictions remain valid.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal error checklist and use it to review a mixed set of derivative exercises before checking the answers. "
Key point:
- Most errors arise from missing terms, incorrect signs, or omitted chain factors.
- Verify structure, algebra, and domain separately.

- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Differentiation Rules
- OpenStax: The Chain Rule
42. Q: How should the result of a derivative calculation be checked before it is used in a business decision?
A: Show answer
Check the mathematical steps and the meaning of the result. Confirm that the original function was transcribed correctly, identify the outer operation, select the correct rule, and differentiate each component with the correct variable. Simplify carefully and verify the domain. Where possible, compare the derivative with an alternative algebraic method, a numerical approximation, or a graph of the function. For an economic interpretation, confirm the units and sign: a negative demand derivative, for example, indicates decreasing demand with increasing price in the specified model. For optimization, check the derivative sign around the candidate, the feasible interval, boundaries, and integer constraints. Finally, test whether the conclusion is plausible under the model assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Apply a structured verification checklist to one product, one quotient, and one business optimization problem. "
Key point:
- Mathematical correctness and contextual interpretation are separate checks.
- Do not make an operational decision from an unchecked stationary value.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Derivatives as Rates of Change
43. Q: What is the difference between a marginal rate and the exact change from one additional unit?
A: Show answer
A marginal rate is the derivative evaluated at a specified point, representing the instantaneous change per unit change in the independent variable. The exact change from increasing a discrete quantity from x to x + 1 is f(x + 1) − f(x). For a sufficiently smooth function and a small change, the derivative provides a local approximation to that difference, but the two values need not be identical. The chapter's profit example interprets P'(50) = 50 as an approximate increase in profit associated with selling the next item. A careful analyst can compute the exact discrete difference as a separate check, particularly when the quantity is large, the function is curved, or the decision has material financial consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the derivative at x with the exact one-unit difference for a quadratic profit function. "
Key point:
- The derivative is an instantaneous marginal rate.
- A one-unit discrete change is calculated by subtracting function values.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Rules of Differentiation
- OpenStax: Derivatives as Rates of Change

The exercises in Section 4.5 require learners to select and combine the rules rather than follow a single worked pattern. The early problem groups cover polynomial, rational, exponential, logarithmic, and composite expressions. Later questions require first and second derivatives and apply rates of change to profit, efficiency, chemical reaction, concentration, revenue, demand, and purchasing-cost models. These problems test whether the learner can translate a verbal situation into a function, identify the dependent and independent variables, differentiate accurately, evaluate at a stated point, and interpret the result. The most effective practice is to show the rule selection and intermediate algebra, not merely write a final answer. Check units, domains, and feasibility in applied questions.
Study instruction:
- "Complete a representative exercise from each problem group and annotate the rule, substitution, simplification, and interpretation. "
