Applications of Optimization: Derivatives, Concavity, and Business Decisions
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 5, Applications of Optimization?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses derivatives to study function behavior and to guide decisions about quantities that can be adjusted. It develops stationary points, local and absolute extrema, higher-order derivatives, concavity, inflection points, and business applications such as cost, revenue, profit, price-demand relationships, and diminishing returns. The core method is to model an objective, differentiate, interpret derivative signs, identify candidates, and check the domain and constraints before drawing a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter as a model-to-decision sequence: define the objective, differentiate, classify, compare, and interpret."
Key point:
- Derivatives turn function behavior into systematic analysis.
- Optimization requires mathematical tests plus domain and context checks.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
2. Q: How does a function's increasing or decreasing behavior relate to its graph?
A: Show answer
A function is increasing over an interval when larger inputs correspond to larger function values; its graph rises from left to right. It is decreasing when larger inputs correspond to smaller values; its graph falls from left to right. These descriptions apply over intervals, not necessarily over the entire domain. A function may rise, fall, and change direction at points where its derivative is zero or undefined. Determining these intervals helps locate turning points and understand whether an objective is improving or deteriorating as the input changes.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a function and identify its increasing and decreasing intervals using both the graph and derivative information."
Key point:
- Monotonic behavior is interval-specific.
- Direction of change is a clue to possible extrema.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
3. Q: What information does the first derivative provide?
A: Show answer
The first derivative f'(x) measures instantaneous rate of change and represents tangent slope where the derivative exists. A positive derivative indicates local rise, a negative derivative indicates local fall, and a zero derivative indicates a horizontal tangent. Derivative signs across an interval can establish increasing or decreasing behavior. A zero derivative alone does not establish a maximum or minimum; the sign pattern nearby or another valid test must be examined.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret positive, negative, and zero derivative values in terms of tangent slope and function behavior."
Key point:
- The derivative describes local change.
- A zero slope is a candidate condition, not a classification.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
4. Q: What is a stationary point?
A: Show answer
A stationary point occurs at a differentiable point where f'(x)=0 and the tangent is horizontal. Stationary points may be local maxima, local minima, or neither, such as a stationary inflection point. Solve the derivative equation to obtain the x-coordinate, then substitute that value into the original function to obtain the y-coordinate. Both coordinates matter when reporting the point on the graph, and the nature of the point must be determined separately.
Study instruction:
- "Solve f'=0, calculate f(x) at each solution, and classify each candidate."
Key point:
- Stationary points have zero derivative.
- Classification and coordinates require additional work.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
5. Q: Why are stationary points only candidates for extrema?
A: Show answer
A horizontal tangent can occur without a change in direction. For example, f(x)=x^3 has f'(0)=0 but continues increasing through x=0; the point is not a maximum or minimum. A function may also have a horizontal tangent at a genuine extremum, but the derivative equation does not distinguish the cases. The function's behavior on nearby intervals, the second derivative where appropriate, or another analysis is needed to determine the nature of a candidate.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a cubic stationary inflection with a quadratic maximum or minimum."
Key point:
- Stationarity does not guarantee a turning point.
- Classify candidates before using them in a decision.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
6. Q: What is the difference between local and absolute extrema?
A: Show answer
A local maximum is the largest value within a sufficiently small neighborhood; a local minimum is the smallest in such a neighborhood. An absolute maximum or minimum is the largest or smallest value across the entire specified domain. A function may have several local extrema but only one absolute extremum of a given type, or multiple points sharing the absolute value. The distinction matters when a decision must be optimal across all feasible choices rather than merely better than nearby alternatives.
Study instruction:
- "Mark local extrema on a graph and then compare their values with all other values in the stated domain."
Key point:
- Local extrema compare nearby points.
- Absolute extrema compare the full domain.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
7. Q: How does the first derivative test classify a stationary point?
A: Show answer
The first derivative test examines f'(x) immediately to the left and right of a candidate. A change from positive to negative means the function rises and then falls, giving a local maximum. A change from negative to positive means the function falls and then rises, giving a local minimum. If the derivative does not change sign, the candidate is not a local extremum under this test, although it may be a stationary point with another feature.
Study instruction:
- "Build a sign chart around every stationary candidate and record the sign transition."
Key point:
- Positive-to-negative indicates a local maximum.
- Negative-to-positive indicates a local minimum.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
8. Q: How should test intervals be selected for derivative signs?
A: Show answer
Find all zeros of f' and any points where the derivative is undefined but the function exists. These boundaries divide the domain into intervals. Choose one test value in each interval and evaluate the sign of f'; the sign remains constant within an interval when no derivative zero or discontinuity separates it. The resulting chart shows where the function rises, falls, and changes direction. Test values should be chosen for convenience, not because they are special values of the original function.
Study instruction:
- "Create a derivative sign chart with all candidate boundaries and one convenient test value per interval."
Key point:
- Candidates partition the domain into intervals.
- Sign charts establish the direction of the function.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
9. Q: Why should derivative signs be examined on both sides of a candidate?
A: Show answer
The derivative at a single point describes tangent slope, but an extremum is defined by nearby function values. The left and right derivative signs reveal whether the function approaches the candidate by rising or falling and whether it reverses direction afterward. This prevents misclassifying a horizontal tangent where the function continues in the same direction. It also establishes intervals of increase and decrease, not merely the label attached to one point.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the signs of f' on adjacent intervals and explain the resulting shape."
Key point:
- Extrema concern nearby values, not just the tangent.
- The sign transition is decisive evidence.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
10. Q: Why must endpoints be considered when finding absolute extrema on a closed interval?
A: Show answer
On a closed interval [a,b], an absolute maximum or minimum may occur at an interior stationary point, a point where the derivative is undefined but the function is defined, or either endpoint. The derivative equation identifies interior stationary candidates but is not the entire procedure. Evaluate the original function at all candidates and at a and b, then compare the values. This complete comparison is the standard method for absolute extrema on a closed, bounded interval when the function is continuous.
Study instruction:
- "For a continuous function on [a,b], list interior candidates and both endpoints, evaluate f, and compare."
Key point:
- Endpoints can be absolute extrema even without f'=0.
- A complete candidate list is essential.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
11. Q: Why can absolute extrema fail to exist on an open or unbounded domain?
A: Show answer
An open interval may exclude a value that the function approaches but never attains, and an unbounded interval may allow growth without limit. A continuous function on an open interval is not guaranteed to attain its largest or smallest value. For example, f(x)=x on (0,1) approaches 1 and 0 but attains neither. Optimization must distinguish a supremum or infimum from an actual maximum or minimum and examine limits, domain restrictions, and feasibility.
Study instruction:
- "Use a function on an open interval to distinguish attained extrema from limiting values."
Key point:
- Domain boundaries may be excluded or infinite.
- Approaching an optimal value is not the same as attaining it.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
12. Q: What is the second derivative and how is it calculated?
A: Show answer
The second derivative f''(x) is the derivative of f'(x). It measures how the first derivative, and therefore the slope of the original function, changes with x. If f' describes instantaneous change in the original quantity, f'' describes the change in that rate. Differentiate f' again. For example, if f(x)=2x^3−18x^2+48x−27, then f'(x)=6x^2−36x+48 and f''(x)=12x−36.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a function twice and label the original function, first derivative, and second derivative."
Key point:
- The second derivative measures change in the first derivative.
- It is central to concavity and the second-derivative test.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
13. Q: How does the second derivative test classify a stationary point?
A: Show answer
At a stationary point c where f'(c)=0 and f'' exists, f''(c)>0 indicates a local minimum because the graph is locally concave up. If f''(c)<0, the graph is locally concave down and the point is a local maximum. This test is convenient because it can classify a candidate without a full sign chart. The conditions must be satisfied at the candidate; the test does not automatically classify nonstationary points or points where the second derivative is unavailable.
Study instruction:
- "At each stationary candidate calculate f'' and apply its sign only after confirming f'(c)=0."
Key point:
- Positive second derivative indicates a local minimum.
- Negative second derivative indicates a local maximum.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
14. Q: What does an inconclusive second derivative test mean?
A: Show answer
If f''(c)=0 at a stationary point, the second derivative test does not determine whether the point is a maximum, minimum, or neither. The result is inconclusive rather than evidence that the point has no extremum. A first-derivative sign chart, higher-order derivative analysis, or direct comparison of nearby function values may resolve the classification. The same caution applies if the second derivative does not exist; use a method that remains valid for the function and domain.
Study instruction:
- "Practice a case where f'=0 and f''=0, then classify it using derivative signs or nearby values."
Key point:
- Zero second derivative is not a classification.
- Use an alternative test when necessary.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
15. Q: What does concavity describe?
A: Show answer
Concavity describes how a graph bends as x changes. A concave-up graph bends like a cup, with slopes generally increasing; a concave-down graph bends like a frown, with slopes generally decreasing. Concavity is different from whether the function is increasing or decreasing: a graph can rise while concave down or fall while concave up. The second derivative provides an algebraic way to identify curvature when it exists, allowing analysis without relying solely on a plotted graph.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch the four combinations of increasing or decreasing behavior and concavity."
Key point:
- Concavity concerns change in slope, not direction.
- Rising or falling and curvature are separate properties.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
16. Q: How does the sign of the second derivative determine concavity?
A: Show answer
Where f''(x)>0 throughout an interval, f' is increasing and the function is concave up. Where f''(x)<0 throughout an interval, f' is decreasing and the function is concave down. To establish concavity, find zeros and undefined points of f'', partition the domain, and test the sign on each interval. The sign at one isolated point does not establish concavity over an entire interval, so interval analysis is required.
Study instruction:
- "Find f'', partition the domain using its zeros, and construct a concavity sign chart."
Key point:
- Positive second derivative means concave up.
- Negative second derivative means concave down.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
17. Q: What is a point of inflection?
A: Show answer
A point of inflection is where a function changes concavity, from up to down or down to up. Its x-coordinate is a candidate when f'' is zero or undefined, but a genuine change in concavity must be established. The y-coordinate is obtained by evaluating the original function. Inflection points describe a change in curvature and are not necessarily local maxima or minima because the function may continue increasing or decreasing through the point.
Study instruction:
- "Find a candidate from f'' and confirm the signs on its left and right."
Key point:
- Inflection requires a change in concavity.
- The function need not change from rising to falling.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
18. Q: Why is solving f''=0 not enough to prove an inflection point?
A: Show answer
The second derivative may equal zero without changing sign. For example, f(x)=x^4 has f''(x)=12x^2, which is zero at x=0 but remains nonnegative on both sides; the graph does not change concavity there. A candidate must be checked using signs of f'' on neighboring intervals or another curvature analysis. The same principle applies when f'' is undefined: the point may be an inflection, but only a demonstrated curvature change establishes it.
Study instruction:
- "Compare f(x)=x^3 and f(x)=x^4 at zero and inspect the second-derivative signs."
Key point:
- A zero second derivative is only a candidate.
- Sign change, not the equation alone, establishes inflection.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
19. Q: Can a function have an inflection point where its first derivative is not zero?
A: Show answer
Yes. Inflection concerns a change in concavity, while stationarity concerns a horizontal tangent. These conditions are independent. A function may pass through an inflection point with a nonzero tangent slope and continue rising, while its curvature changes from concave down to concave up. A stationary inflection occurs when both conditions coincide, but that is only one possible type. Classifying points by the correct property avoids confusing curvature changes with turning points.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze an inflection with nonzero slope and compare it with a stationary inflection."
Key point:
- Inflection and stationarity are different properties.
- Nonzero slope does not prevent a curvature change.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
20. Q: How can first- and second-derivative analysis be combined?
A: Show answer
The first derivative identifies stationary candidates and interval direction; the second derivative identifies concavity and may classify stationary points or inflection candidates. Begin by defining the domain, calculating f', solving f'=0, and testing signs. Then calculate f'', identify concavity intervals, and check candidate inflection points. Finally, evaluate endpoints and compare values when absolute extrema are required. The combined analysis provides a more complete description than either derivative alone.
Study instruction:
- "Complete a function-analysis worksheet with domain, f', sign chart, f'', concavity, inflection, and extrema."
Key point:
- The first derivative analyzes direction.
- The second derivative analyzes curvature.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
21. Q: How does the cubic f(x)=2x^3−18x^2+48x−27 illustrate local extrema?
A: Show answer
Its derivative is f'(x)=6x^2−36x+48=6(x−2)(x−4). The derivative is positive before 2, negative between 2 and 4, and positive after 4. Therefore, it has a local maximum at x=2 and a local minimum at x=4. The original function gives f(2)=13 and f(4)=5. The second derivative f''(x)=12x−36 is negative at 2 and positive at 4, confirming the classifications.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the factorization, sign chart, function values, and second-derivative confirmation."
Key point:
- Positive-negative-positive produces a maximum then a minimum.
- Substitute candidates into the original function for coordinates.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
22. Q: How should polynomial derivative equations be solved when locating stationary points?
A: Show answer
Differentiate the polynomial, set the derivative equal to zero, and solve. Factoring is useful when recognizable factors exist; otherwise, algebraic or numerical methods may be needed. Each real solution within the domain becomes a stationary candidate. Record candidates accurately, avoid dropping repeated roots, and classify them using derivative signs or the second derivative. Evaluate the original function at relevant x-values if coordinates or extremum values are required.
Study instruction:
- "Work through a polynomial whose derivative factors into linear terms and verify all real candidates."
Key point:
- Solving f'=0 identifies candidates.
- Factoring, domain restrictions, and classification must be handled carefully.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
23. Q: Why does the domain matter when analyzing extrema?
A: Show answer
A candidate is relevant only if it lies in the function's domain and the feasible domain of the problem. Business models may restrict production to nonnegative quantities, advertising budgets to a range, or staffing to whole numbers. A stationary point outside those restrictions cannot be selected. The domain also determines which endpoints must be checked and whether the problem asks for a continuous optimum or an implementable integer decision. Apply constraints before interpreting the result.
Study instruction:
- "Annotate the domain and eliminate stationary candidates outside the feasible range."
Key point:
- Mathematical candidates must be feasible.
- Domain constraints can change the optimum.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
24. Q: What is a systematic procedure for solving an optimization problem?
A: Show answer
Define the decision variable and feasible domain, then express the objective as a function. Differentiate, solve for stationary candidates, and classify them using derivative behavior. Include feasible endpoints and points where the function is defined but differentiability fails. Evaluate the objective at all relevant candidates, compare values, and translate the result into the units and decision language of the problem. Finally, check whether assumptions make the recommendation realistic.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the procedure to a simple revenue, profit, or production problem and document each step."
Key point:
- Optimization moves from model to candidates to comparison.
- A mathematical result must be translated into a feasible decision.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
25. Q: What assumptions should be checked before trusting an optimization model?
A: Show answer
Check whether the objective represents the real outcome, coefficients and data are credible, and the feasible domain matches actual constraints. Consider whether quantities can be treated as continuous and whether the model ignores capacity, time, quality, uncertainty, or market changes. A mathematically correct optimum can mislead if the function is poorly specified or constraints are omitted. Optimization is conditional on the model, not a guarantee of real-world success.
Study instruction:
- "List assumptions behind a model and identify which require evidence or sensitivity testing."
Key point:
- The optimum is only as credible as the model.
- Mathematical precision does not remove modeling uncertainty.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
26. Q: How are revenue, cost, and profit related?
A: Show answer
Revenue R(x) represents sales income at quantity x, total cost C(x) represents costs associated with that quantity, and profit P(x)=R(x)−C(x). Revenue may depend on price and quantity, while cost may contain fixed and variable components. These functions must use consistent units and assumptions. A business decision should not maximize revenue automatically when costs matter; profit or another objective may be the appropriate function to optimize.
Study instruction:
- "Write revenue, cost, and profit functions for a simple business scenario and check units."
Key point:
- Profit is revenue less total cost.
- The objective function should reflect the actual decision goal.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
27. Q: What is marginal cost and how is it estimated?
A: Show answer
Marginal cost is the additional cost associated with producing one more unit. For differentiable C(x), C'(x) approximates the change in cost for a small increase at x. It is a rate, with units of currency per unit of output. The estimate should not be confused with the exact discrete difference C(x+1)−C(x). The derivative is useful for smooth models and small increments, but the finite difference may be needed for an exact next-unit calculation.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a cost function, substitute the current production level, and interpret the rate."
Key point:
- Marginal cost is the derivative of total cost.
- It approximates, but need not equal, the exact next-unit cost.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
28. Q: Why can marginal cost differ from the exact next-unit cost?
A: Show answer
The derivative measures an instantaneous rate at x, whereas the exact next-unit cost is C(x+1)−C(x). When the cost curve changes slope over that interval, the derivative at x may not equal the finite difference. For a smooth function and a small increment, the derivative often provides a useful approximation. The difference may matter when the function is strongly curved or the increment is large, so exact differences are useful for discrete production decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate both C'(x) and C(x+1)−C(x) for a nonlinear cost function."
Key point:
- Derivative estimates and finite differences are related but not identical.
- Curvature and increment size affect approximation accuracy.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
29. Q: What are marginal revenue and marginal profit?
A: Show answer
Marginal revenue R'(x) measures the rate at which revenue changes as quantity changes, while marginal profit P'(x) measures the rate at which profit changes. Because P=R−C, P'=R'−C'. These rates approximate additional revenue or profit for a small increase in quantity. Their signs help determine whether increasing output improves the objective, but the feasible domain and the full objective still matter when selecting a final quantity.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate revenue, cost, and profit and confirm that P'=R'−C'."
Key point:
- Marginal profit is marginal revenue minus marginal cost.
- Its sign indicates the local direction of profit change.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
30. Q: What is a price-demand function?
A: Show answer
A price-demand function describes the price that can be charged as a function of quantity demanded or offered for sale in a modeled market. In simplified examples, price often decreases as quantity increases. The function provides a relationship between quantity and price; it is not automatically a complete model of consumer behavior, competition, or market dynamics. Its domain must be restricted to economically meaningful quantities and prices, and the relationship should be treated as an assumed or estimated model.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret a price-demand function at several quantities and identify a meaningful range."
Key point:
- Price-demand functions link price and quantity.
- A demand model is an assumption that should be checked.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
31. Q: How is a revenue function derived from a price-demand function?
A: Show answer
Revenue is price multiplied by quantity. If price is p(x), then R(x)=p(x)x. For example, p(x)=50−0.01x gives R(x)=50x−0.01x^2. The resulting function expresses sales income in terms of quantity alone. It should be defined over a range where the demand relationship and quantity assumptions remain valid; negative prices or quantities beyond market limits may signal that the model is being extrapolated too far.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a price-demand function into revenue by multiplying price by quantity and simplifying."
Key point:
- Revenue equals price times quantity.
- Revenue must be expressed in one decision variable for optimization.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
32. Q: How can a quadratic revenue function be maximized?
A: Show answer
For R(x)=8x−x^2, R'(x)=8−2x, so the stationary candidate is x=4. R''(x)=−2 confirms a local maximum, and R(4)=16. The price or other variables must be evaluated from their corresponding model. For a quadratic with negative leading coefficient, the vertex is the maximum, but the candidate must lie within the feasible quantity domain.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a quadratic revenue function, solve R'=0, confirm R''<0, and calculate the value."
Key point:
- A negative second derivative confirms a local revenue maximum.
- Feasibility must be checked before adopting the vertex.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
33. Q: How should a business maximize profit rather than revenue?
A: Show answer
Profit is P(x)=R(x)−C(x), so optimize that difference, not revenue alone. The stationary condition is P'=R'−C'=0, meaning marginal revenue equals marginal cost at an interior candidate. A maximum must then be confirmed by derivative behavior or comparison with feasible candidates. A quantity that maximizes revenue may produce lower profit if costs rise substantially, so revenue optimization and profit optimization are distinct decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Build a profit function, solve P'=0, and compare with the revenue-maximizing quantity."
Key point:
- Profit optimization balances revenue and cost.
- Cost can shift the profit optimum away from the revenue optimum.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
34. Q: Why is marginal revenue equal to marginal cost at an interior stationary profit candidate?
A: Show answer
Since P'=R'−C', setting P'=0 gives R'=C'. At that quantity, the modeled incremental revenue and incremental cost rates balance, so the profit derivative is zero. This condition identifies a candidate, not necessarily a maximum: profit may be minimized or the test may be inconclusive. The second derivative, sign pattern, and feasible boundaries must still be considered. The equality depends on the differentiable model and an interior candidate.
Study instruction:
- "Derive the equality from P=R−C and explain why a second test is needed."
Key point:
- The equality follows from the derivative of profit.
- A zero marginal-profit rate does not prove maximum profit.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
35. Q: How do fixed and variable costs affect profit optimization?
A: Show answer
Fixed costs do not vary with quantity over the modeled range, while variable costs change with production. Total cost may be written C(x)=FC+VC(x). If FC is constant, its derivative is zero, so it does not change marginal cost or the interior stationary condition. However, fixed costs still affect total profit and whether operating is worthwhile. Quantity optimization and the decision to enter, exit, or continue are related but separate analyses.
Study instruction:
- "Separate fixed and variable costs and compare their effects on marginal cost and total profit."
Key point:
- Constant fixed costs affect profit levels but not marginal cost.
- Operating decisions may require additional criteria.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
36. Q: How should a monopolist use a price-demand function to optimize revenue or profit?
A: Show answer
A monopolist model may specify price as a function of quantity, so revenue is R(x)=p(x)x. To maximize revenue, differentiate R and solve for candidates. To maximize profit, subtract cost first and differentiate P=R−C. Evaluate candidates and feasible endpoints, then derive the price from demand at the selected quantity. The model simplifies market behavior and should not be treated as universal, since regulation, competitors, uncertainty, and strategy may matter.
Study instruction:
- "Derive revenue from a demand function and separately calculate revenue and profit candidates when costs are supplied."
Key point:
- Price is determined from demand at the chosen quantity.
- Revenue and profit optimization require different objectives.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
37. Q: What is diminishing marginal returns?
A: Show answer
Diminishing marginal returns occur when marginal output or response begins to decline as an input increases. Output may still be increasing; the decline concerns the rate of additional output, not necessarily total output. In a differentiable model, diminishing returns can be identified when the output slope begins to decrease, corresponding to a transition from concave up to concave down. The point of transition is often modeled as an inflection point, subject to a verified concavity change and domain constraints.
Study instruction:
- "Plot an output function and distinguish increasing output from declining marginal output."
Key point:
- Diminishing returns mean marginal gains are declining.
- Total output need not be falling.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
38. Q: Why is the point of diminishing returns often an inflection point?
A: Show answer
Marginal return is the first derivative. When marginal returns stop increasing and begin decreasing, f' changes from rising to falling, so f'' changes from positive to negative. That corresponds to a transition from concave up to concave down. The inflection point is a useful marker in models with that shape, but it must be checked because an inflection may occur in another direction or there may be multiple curvature changes.
Study instruction:
- "Use f' as marginal output and f'' as its change, then confirm the curvature transition."
Key point:
- Diminishing marginal returns concern the declining slope.
- A concavity change can locate the transition.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
39. Q: How does the advertising model N(x)=−0.25x^3+6x^2+8x illustrate diminishing returns?
A: Show answer
The chapter models sales N(x) against advertising spending x, measured in thousands of dollars, over 0≤x≤16. N''(x)=−1.5x+12. Setting it to zero gives x=8; the sign changes from positive before 8 to negative after 8. The modeled point of diminishing returns occurs at $8,000 of weekly advertising. The sales response is still increasing at that point, but the rate of increase begins to decline.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the advertising function twice, solve N''=0, and check signs around x=8."
Key point:
- The model's diminishing-return candidate is $8,000.
- The change is in the rate of sales response, not necessarily in sales.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
40. Q: How can staffing models identify diminishing returns?
A: Show answer
For a staffing function, output is modeled against the number of workers. Calculate the second derivative, solve for candidates where it equals zero, and test concavity on either side within the allowed range. The candidate must be interpreted in feasible units; workers are ordinarily integers, so a continuous model may need rounding or comparison of nearby whole-number choices. The result can guide a staffing review but should be combined with capacity, service quality, workload, and cost considerations.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a staffing function's second derivative and compare the continuous candidate with feasible integer levels."
Key point:
- The second derivative describes how marginal staffing output changes.
- Continuous candidates may need operational adjustment.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
41. Q: How does the bakery example in the chapter locate diminishing returns?
A: Show answer
The chapter models service as N(x)=−0.0075x^4+0.12x^3 for 0≤x≤12. Its second derivative is N''(x)=−0.09x^2+0.72x. The candidate roots are x=0 and x=8; in the staffing context, x=8 is the relevant interior point where concavity changes from up to down. The owner can use this as a signal that marginal service gains begin to decline, then evaluate nearby staffing choices and operational constraints.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate N'' and its roots, check signs around x=8, and interpret in staff and service units."
Key point:
- The example identifies eight staff as the relevant candidate.
- It is a model-based guide, not an automatic staffing rule.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5

42. Q: What common mistakes occur when using derivatives for optimization?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include solving f'=0 but failing to classify candidates, confusing local with absolute extrema, ignoring endpoints, using the second derivative test when f''=0, assuming every f''=0 point is an inflection, and overlooking the domain. Business errors include maximizing revenue when profit is the goal, confusing marginal rates with exact next-unit differences, mixing units, and treating a continuous result as implementable. A structured candidate list and sign chart reduce these errors.
Study instruction:
- "Review a solution and check for incomplete candidate lists, wrong objectives, missing tests, and unit errors."
Key point:
- Optimization errors often arise from incomplete checks.
- Domain, objective, units, and test conditions must be verified.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
-
43. Q: How should optimization results be checked for sensitivity and practical limitations?
A: Show answer
Examine how the result changes if assumptions, coefficients, costs, demand, or constraints vary. A stationary point that moves substantially under small changes may be fragile, while a broad optimum may be more robust. Compare the mathematical result with actual capacity, integer quantities, budget limits, quality, risk, and strategic objectives. The derivative method provides a model-based answer; sensitivity and operational checks help determine whether that answer should guide real decisions or be refined.
Study instruction:
- "Perturb a model coefficient or constraint and compare the resulting optimum with the original candidate."
Key point:
- Sensitivity analysis tests stability.
- Practical constraints may alter implementation.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5

44. Q: How should the chapter's practice problems be approached systematically?
A: Show answer
For stationary-point problems, differentiate, solve f'=0, and classify each candidate. For inflection problems, differentiate twice and confirm a concavity change. For absolute extrema, respect the given domain and compare all feasible candidates and endpoints. For business problems, define the objective, derive revenue or profit from supplied relationships, calculate marginal rates when requested, and distinguish estimates from exact differences. For diminishing-return problems, use f'' and interpret the candidate in the input's units.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one exercise from each problem group and record the model, derivatives, candidates, tests, and final interpretation."
Key point:
- Each problem type has a consistent workflow.
- Interpretation and domain checks are as important as algebra.
Source links:
- Fortney, Calculus for Business and Economics, Chapter 5
