Table 6
Antiderivatives, Definite Integrals, and Business Applications
1. Q: What is an antiderivative, and how is it related to integration?
A: Show answer
An antiderivative of a function f(x) is a function F(x) whose derivative is f(x), so F'(x) = f(x). Finding an antiderivative reverses differentiation: differentiation measures a rate of change, while integration can recover a function from that rate. For example, because the derivative of 3x is 3, one antiderivative of the constant function 3 is 3x. Integration is the process of finding such antiderivatives when the task is an indefinite integral. In business applications, this reversal is useful when a marginal or instantaneous rate is known but the corresponding total function is required. The result is generally a family of functions until an initial value or other condition identifies one member.
Study instruction:
- Explain the relationship F'(x) = f(x) and practice checking proposed antiderivatives by differentiating them.
Key point:
- Integration reverses differentiation by recovering a function from its derivative.
- An antiderivative is not necessarily unique until additional information is supplied.
Source links:
-
2. Q: Why does an indefinite integral include an arbitrary constant?
A: Show answer
Differentiation removes additive constants. For example, 40x, 40x + 10, and 40x − 20 all have derivative 40. Therefore, knowing only that F'(x) = 40 does not reveal which original function was intended. The complete family is F(x) = 40x + C, where C can be any constant. The arbitrary constant is not an optional decoration: omitting it would incorrectly imply that only one antiderivative exists. The same principle applies to every indefinite integral. A separate condition, such as a known value of the original function at a particular input, is needed to determine C when a unique function is required.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate several functions that differ only by an additive constant and explain why they produce the same derivative.
Key point:
- Additive constants disappear during differentiation.
- The constant of integration represents the family of possible antiderivatives.
Source links:
-
3. Q: What do the symbols in the indefinite-integral notation mean?
A: Show answer
In the expression ∫ f(x) dx, the integral sign indicates integration, f(x) is the integrand, and dx identifies x as the variable of integration. The result is an antiderivative plus an arbitrary constant: ∫ f(x) dx = F(x) + C, where F'(x) = f(x). The notation is connected to the idea of summation, but an indefinite integral is specifically a family of antiderivatives rather than a numerical area. The variable matters because integration is performed with respect to the variable shown after d. If a function depends on t and the expression ends in dt, the antiderivative is taken with respect to t.
Study instruction:
- Label the integral sign, integrand, variable of integration, antiderivative, and constant in several examples.
Key point:
- The integrand is the function being integrated.
- The differential identifies the variable with respect to which integration occurs.
Source links:
-
4. Q: How is the indefinite integral of a constant function calculated?
A: Show answer
If k is a constant, then ∫ k dx = kx + C. The reason is that the derivative of kx + C is k. For example, ∫ 7 dx = 7x + C and ∫ −4 dx = −4x + C. This rule is useful when a rate of change is constant. If a business has a constant marginal cost of 2 dollars per unit, integrating that rate gives a variable-cost increase of 2x over x units, before any fixed-cost amount is added. Always retain the constant of integration for an indefinite integral, even when the integrand itself is a constant.
Study instruction:
- Solve at least three constant-function integrals and verify each answer by differentiation.
Key point:
- The antiderivative of k is kx + C.
- The constant of integration remains necessary even for simple integrands.
Source links:
-
5. Q: When can the power rule be used to integrate x raised to a power?
A: Show answer
For a real exponent n other than −1, the power rule is ∫ x^n dx = x^(n+1)/(n+1) + C. Increase the exponent by one, then divide by the new exponent. For example, ∫ x^3 dx = x^4/4 + C, while ∫ x^(−5) dx = x^(−4)/(−4) + C. The restriction n ≠ −1 is essential because the denominator n + 1 would otherwise be zero. The rule applies to positive, zero, and negative real exponents within their valid domains, but the exceptional reciprocal function must be handled separately.
Study instruction:
- Practise the power rule with positive, zero, and negative exponents, and explicitly check whether n equals −1 before applying it.
Key point:
- Add one to the exponent and divide by the new exponent.
- The exponent −1 is excluded and requires a logarithmic rule.
Source links:
-
6. Q: Why is the integral of 1/x different from the ordinary power rule?
A: Show answer
The function 1/x is x^(−1), so applying the ordinary power rule would require division by −1 + 1, which is zero. The correct antiderivative is ∫ (1/x) dx = ln(abs(x)) + C on any interval that does not cross zero. The absolute-value notation accounts for both positive and negative x values, while the logarithm's real-valued domain excludes zero. The antiderivative is valid separately on intervals to the left or right of zero; a single interval crossing zero is not a valid domain for the integrand. This exception is especially important when integrating rational expressions or expressions containing a function divided by its derivative-related factor.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the reciprocal exception and verify the derivative of ln(abs(x)) on an interval that does not contain zero.
Key point:
- The exponent −1 does not use the ordinary power rule.
- The antiderivative is logarithmic and its domain must exclude zero.
Source links:
-
7. Q: How do the constant-multiple and sum-and-difference rules simplify integration?
A: Show answer
A constant can be factored outside an integral: ∫ kf(x) dx = k∫ f(x) dx. A sum or difference can be integrated term by term: ∫ [f(x) ± g(x)] dx = ∫ f(x) dx ± ∫ g(x) dx. These rules allow a polynomial or a linear combination of functions to be separated into manageable pieces. For example, ∫ (4x^2 + 2x) dx = 4(x^3/3) + 2(x^2/2) + C = (4/3)x^3 + x^2 + C. The arbitrary constants from the separate terms combine into one arbitrary constant, so the final expression needs only one C.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a multi-term polynomial integral as a sum of simpler integrals before applying the relevant rules.
Key point:
- Constants may be factored out and sums may be integrated term by term.
- One final constant of integration is sufficient for the combined expression.
Source links:
-
8. Q: How are exponential functions integrated?
A: Show answer
For a constant base b greater than zero and not equal to one, ∫ b^x dx = b^x/ln(b) + C. For the natural exponential, ∫ e^x dx = e^x + C because the derivative of e^x is itself. A constant multiplier can be handled using the constant-multiple rule; for example, ∫ 3e^x dx = 3e^x + C. The base matters: the denominator ln(b) is required for a general exponential base. These rules are useful in growth and decay models, including business situations in which a quantity changes at a rate related to its current level.
Study instruction:
- Compare the antiderivatives of e^x and b^x and verify the general-base result by differentiating it.
Key point:
- The antiderivative of e^x is e^x + C.
- A general base b requires division by ln(b).
Source links:
-
9. Q: How is the natural logarithm integrated, and what is the corresponding rule for a general logarithm base?
A: Show answer
Integration by parts gives ∫ ln(x) dx = x ln(x) − x + C for x greater than zero. For a logarithm with base b, the corresponding result is ∫ log_b(x) dx = x log_b(x) − x/ln(b) + C, for b greater than zero and not equal to one and x greater than zero. The formula can be checked by differentiating the proposed antiderivative. This check is important because the coefficient of the second term must cancel the derivative contribution created by the product x log_b(x). A reliable approach is to verify formulas rather than rely on visual similarity between logarithm rules.
Study instruction:
- Differentiate x ln(x) − x and x log_b(x) − x/ln(b) to confirm that the original logarithmic integrands are recovered.
Key point:
- ∫ ln(x) dx = x ln(x) − x + C.
- The general-base formula must contain the factor 1/ln(b) in its second term.
Source links:
-
10. Q: Which chain-rule patterns can be integrated directly in this chapter?
A: Show answer
Two useful patterns are ∫ f'(x)e^(f(x)) dx = e^(f(x)) + C and ∫ f'(x)/f(x) dx = ln(abs(f(x))) + C, where the relevant functions are defined and the denominator is nonzero. In each case, the integrand contains the derivative of an inner function. The first pattern works because differentiating e^(f(x)) produces e^(f(x))f'(x). The second works because differentiating ln(abs(f(x))) produces f'(x)/f(x) on an interval where f(x) does not equal zero. Recognizing the inner function and its derivative prevents unnecessary algebra and identifies a direct antiderivative.
Study instruction:
- In each candidate integral, identify the inner function and check whether its derivative is also present as a factor.
Key point:
- A matching inner derivative signals a direct chain-rule integration pattern.
- The logarithmic pattern requires a nonzero denominator on the interval considered.
Source links:
-
11. Q: How can an antiderivative be checked for correctness?
A: Show answer
Differentiate the proposed antiderivative and confirm that the result equals the original integrand throughout the relevant domain. For example, differentiating x^4/4 + C gives x^3, so it is an antiderivative of x^3. For a logarithmic result, use the chain rule and check the domain as well as the algebra. A correct-looking expression may still contain a coefficient error, a missing exponent adjustment, or an omitted inner derivative. Verification is particularly useful after integrating long polynomials, exponential expressions, or business functions. It checks the mathematical operation without requiring a separate integration method.
Study instruction:
- After completing every practice integral, differentiate the answer and compare the derivative term by term with the original integrand.
Key point:
- Differentiation is the direct test of an indefinite integral.
- Domain restrictions must be checked in addition to algebraic equality.
Source links:
-
12. Q: How does an initial condition determine the constant of integration?
A: Show answer
Integration produces a family of functions containing C. An initial condition supplies a known value of the original function and allows C to be solved. Suppose R'(x) = −0.025x + 2000 and R(0) = 0. Integration gives R(x) = −0.0125x^2 + 2000x + C. Substituting x = 0 and R(0) = 0 gives C = 0. If the known condition were different, the constant could be different. The condition must describe the total function, not merely the marginal rate. In business applications, a zero-output revenue condition may be reasonable, while a cost function may have a nonzero value at zero output because fixed costs remain.
Study instruction:
- Integrate a given marginal function, substitute the supplied point, and solve explicitly for C before writing the final function.
Key point:
- An initial value selects one member of the antiderivative family.
- Revenue and cost functions may have different values at zero activity.
Source links:
-
13. Q: What is the geometric meaning of a definite integral when the function is nonnegative?
A: Show answer
For a nonnegative function f(x) on [a,b], the definite integral ∫ from a to b of f(x) dx represents the area between the graph, the x-axis, and the vertical boundaries x = a and x = b. For a simple line such as f(x) = 3x on [0,20], the region is a triangle with base 20 and height 60, so its area is 600. The integral generalizes this area calculation to curves that cannot be handled easily with elementary geometric formulas. The geometric interpretation is useful, but the definite integral is more general than ordinary area because it also records sign when the function lies below the axis.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a nonnegative function over a stated interval and identify the region represented by its definite integral.
Key point:
- For a nonnegative function, the definite integral equals the area under the curve.
- Integration extends area calculation to curved regions.
Source links:
-
14. Q: How does a Riemann sum approximate the area under a curve?
A: Show answer
A Riemann sum divides an interval [a,b] into n subintervals and approximates the region with rectangles. If each subinterval has width Δx = (b − a)/n and the selected sample point in subinterval i is x_i, the approximation is the sum of f(x_i)Δx over all subintervals. Each rectangle contributes its height, f(x_i), multiplied by its width. The estimate depends on the number of rectangles and the sample points selected. The chapter uses midpoint sample points for its examples. Riemann sums provide the construction behind the definite integral and explain why integration can represent accumulated quantities as well as geometric regions.
Study instruction:
- Draw an interval divided into equal pieces, mark each midpoint, and calculate the corresponding rectangle-height sum.
Key point:
- A Riemann sum adds rectangle areas to estimate an integral.
- The estimate depends on both subinterval width and sample-point choice.
Source links:
-
15. Q: Why does increasing the number of rectangles generally improve a midpoint Riemann-sum estimate?
A: Show answer
Increasing n makes each subinterval narrower because Δx = (b − a)/n. Narrower rectangles usually follow the curve more closely, reducing the mismatch between the actual curve and the rectangular approximation. In the chapter's example f(x) = 3x on [0,20], two midpoint rectangles happen to give the exact area because the graph is a straight line and the midpoint heights balance the triangular region. For a curved function, a finite midpoint sum will generally remain an approximation, but increasing n improves its accuracy under the usual integrability conditions. The improvement is a convergence principle, not a guarantee that every individual finite estimate is exact.
Study instruction:
- Calculate midpoint estimates for the same function using two different values of n and compare each estimate with the exact integral when available.
Key point:
- More, narrower rectangles generally provide a closer approximation.
- A linear example can be exact even with relatively few rectangles.
Source links:
-
16. Q: What happens to a Riemann sum as the number of subintervals approaches infinity?
A: Show answer
As n increases without bound, the subinterval width Δx approaches zero. Under the conditions that make the function integrable, the Riemann sums converge to the definite integral. Symbolically, the definite integral is the limit of the sums of f(x_i)Δx as n approaches infinity. This limit transforms a finite approximation into the exact accumulated value represented by the integral. The chapter introduces the definition using positive functions and later uses signed values when the graph lies below the x-axis. The limiting process is the mathematical foundation of definite integration, not simply a numerical trick for obtaining a better estimate.
Study instruction:
- Explain why Δx tends to zero as n grows and describe how the finite sum becomes a definite integral in the limit.
Key point:
- The definite integral is the limit of suitable Riemann sums.
- The limit captures exact accumulation as rectangle widths shrink.
Source links:
-
17. Q: Why is a definite integral a signed area rather than always a positive geometric area?
A: Show answer
The definite integral adds positive contributions where f(x) is above the x-axis and negative contributions where f(x) is below it. Consequently, a region below the x-axis produces a negative integral even though its ordinary geometric area is positive. This sign convention is useful in calculus because it preserves orientation and makes properties such as reversing integration limits work consistently. If the question asks for the actual geometric area, the sign must be handled separately. A student should therefore distinguish the numerical value of the signed integral from the nonnegative size of a physical or geometric region.
Study instruction:
- For a graph with positive and negative portions, mark which parts contribute positively and which contribute negatively to the definite integral.
Key point:
- Definite integrals measure signed accumulation.
- Geometric area is nonnegative and may require an absolute-value or interval-splitting calculation.
Source links:
-
18. Q: How should the area be calculated when a curve lies below the x-axis?
A: Show answer
If f(x) is negative throughout an interval, its definite integral over that interval is negative. The ordinary geometric area is the magnitude of that signed result. If the function changes sign inside the interval, split the interval at its x-intercepts, evaluate each signed part, and add the magnitudes of the pieces. Simply taking the absolute value of the total integral is not sufficient when positive and negative regions cancel, because cancellation can make the signed total smaller than the total geometric area. The chapter's reflected-curve example illustrates a negative integral of −21.0625 whose corresponding geometric area is 21.0625.
Study instruction:
- Find the x-intercepts of a function, divide the interval at those points, and calculate geometric area by adding the magnitudes of each signed integral.
Key point:
- A region below the x-axis contributes a negative integral.
- For total geometric area, handle each sign region separately.
Source links:
-
19. Q: What is the effect of reversing the upper and lower limits of a definite integral?
A: Show answer
Reversing the limits changes the sign of the integral: ∫ from b to a of f(x) dx = −∫ from a to b of f(x) dx. The Fundamental Theorem still applies in the same order, using F(upper limit) minus F(lower limit). For example, if ∫ from 1 to 2 of 4x^3 dx equals 15, then ∫ from 2 to 1 of 4x^3 dx equals −15. The geometric region has not changed; only the orientation of integration has changed. This property is a useful check when evaluating definite integrals and when interpreting signed accumulation.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate one integral in both directions and verify that the two answers have equal magnitudes and opposite signs.
Key point:
- Reversing limits reverses the sign.
- The magnitude of the signed integral is unchanged when only the orientation is reversed.
Source links:
-
20. Q: What is the value of a definite integral when its upper and lower limits are equal?
A: Show answer
The integral from a to a of any integrable function is zero. There is no interval over which accumulation occurs, so the signed contribution is zero. The chapter demonstrates this with an integral whose two endpoints are both 1. A related property is that positive and negative contributions can cancel over a wider interval, producing a zero integral even though the graph encloses nonzero geometric area. These two situations should not be confused: equal endpoints create a zero integral because the interval has zero width, whereas cancellation occurs because signed contributions offset one another.
Study instruction:
- Compare an integral with equal limits to an integral over a nonzero interval whose positive and negative regions cancel.
Key point:
- Equal integration limits always give zero.
- A zero signed integral does not always mean that geometric area is zero.
Source links:
-
21. Q: What does the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus state?
A: Show answer
If f is continuous on the closed interval [a,b] and F is an antiderivative of f on that interval, then the definite integral of f from a to b equals F(b) − F(a). The theorem connects the two perspectives developed in the chapter: an indefinite integral finds antiderivatives, while a definite integral represents signed accumulation. Instead of calculating an infinite limiting sum directly, a student can find an antiderivative and evaluate it at the endpoints. Continuity is part of the stated condition in the chapter's formulation. The theorem is the central computational tool for exact definite integrals in the examples and business applications.
Study instruction:
- State the theorem in your own words and identify the continuity condition, antiderivative, lower limit, and upper limit.
Key point:
- A definite integral can be evaluated using an antiderivative at the endpoints.
- The calculation is upper value minus lower value.
Source links:
-
22. Q: How is a definite integral evaluated using the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus?
A: Show answer
First find an antiderivative F(x) of the integrand. Then substitute the upper limit b and lower limit a and calculate F(b) − F(a). For example, the integral of −x^2 + 4x + 4 from 1 to 4 is evaluated using F(x) = −x^3/3 + 2x^2 + 4x, giving F(4) − F(1) = 21. The arbitrary constant is normally omitted because it appears in both endpoint evaluations and cancels. Careful substitution, parentheses, and order are important, particularly when the lower endpoint is negative or the antiderivative contains several terms.
Study instruction:
- Work through a polynomial definite integral and show the upper-endpoint substitution and lower-endpoint subtraction on separate lines.
Key point:
- Find F first, then calculate F(b) − F(a).
- The arbitrary constant cancels and need not be written in the definite-integral calculation.
Source links:
-
23. Q: How is the area between two curves calculated when one curve stays above the other?
A: Show answer
If f(x) is the upper curve and g(x) is the lower curve throughout [a,b], the area between them is ∫ from a to b of [f(x) − g(x)] dx. The vertical distance between the curves at each x is the upper function minus the lower function. Integrating that distance accumulates the area across the interval. The same result can be understood as the area under the upper curve less the area under the lower curve. The functions do not both need to be above the x-axis; the essential condition is that their vertical order is known throughout the interval.
Study instruction:
- Sketch both curves, label the upper and lower functions, and form the difference before integrating.
Key point:
- Area between curves is the integral of upper minus lower.
- The vertical ordering of the curves matters more than their position relative to the x-axis.
Source links:
-
24. Q: How can the intersection points of two curves determine the limits of integration?
A: Show answer
When the area is enclosed by two curves and no interval is supplied, the intersection points often provide the horizontal boundaries. Set the functions equal, solve f(x) = g(x), and use the relevant solutions as the x-limits. For example, x^2 − 6x + 9 = x − 1 leads to (x − 5)(x − 2) = 0, so the curves intersect at x = 2 and x = 5. The functions must then be compared within that interval to identify which is upper. Solving the intersections is only the first step; the correct integrand still depends on the curves' relative positions between the limits.
Study instruction:
- Solve an intersection equation, mark the roots on a number line, and test a point between consecutive roots to identify the upper curve.
Key point:
- Intersections often establish the integration boundaries.
- A test point determines which function is upper within the interval.
Source links:
-
25. Q: What should be done if two curves cross within the interval over which area is required?
A: Show answer
If the curves exchange vertical positions, one single expression for upper minus lower may become negative on part of the interval. Split the calculation at each crossing point, identify the upper curve on each subinterval, and integrate the appropriate difference separately. Add the resulting nonnegative areas. Equivalently, the geometric area is the integral of the absolute vertical difference, provided the function is handled piecewise at its zeros. This avoids cancellation between regions and prevents a negative result from being mistaken for an area. A graph or sign table is often the clearest way to determine where the order changes.
Study instruction:
- Find all crossing points, divide the interval into subintervals, and write the upper-minus-lower expression separately for each part.
Key point:
- A change in curve order requires splitting the integral.
- Geometric area adds the pieces rather than allowing them to cancel.
Source links:
-
26. Q: Why must units be considered when interpreting an integral in a business application?
A: Show answer
Integration accumulates a rate over the variable's unit. If marginal cost is measured in dollars per item and the integration variable is items, the resulting integral is measured in dollars. If a rate is measured in dollars per hour and is integrated over hours, the result is also dollars. Dimensional reasoning helps determine whether a formula is meaningful and can expose a mismatch between a variable and its interpretation. For example, price per item multiplied by a sales rate in items per day produces dollars per day; integrating that revenue rate over days produces total dollars. The units should be stated alongside the mathematical result.
Study instruction:
- Write the units of the integrand and integration variable before calculating a business integral, then state the units of the result.
Key point:
- Integration multiplies a rate by the unit of the variable over which it accumulates.
- Dimensional checks help detect incorrect business formulas.
Source links:
-
27. Q: How does integrating marginal cost determine the cost of producing a range of additional units?
A: Show answer
Marginal cost is the derivative of total cost, C'(x). Integrating marginal cost from a to b gives C(b) − C(a), the increase in total cost as output rises from a to b. It does not automatically give the full cost of producing b units because the initial cost C(a) may be nonzero. For example, integrating a constant marginal cost of 2 dollars per unit from 10 to 15 gives 10 dollars of additional cost over that production range. The result is an incremental cost, not necessarily the entire cost function value at 15. This distinction is essential when fixed costs or prior production costs exist.
Study instruction:
- For a stated production range, identify whether the question asks for incremental cost or total cost and select the correct integral or cost-function evaluation.
Key point:
- ∫ from a to b of C'(x) dx = C(b) − C(a).
- Integrating marginal cost gives a change in cost, not automatically total cost.
Source links:
-
28. Q: How is the cost of producing a particular numbered unit represented by an integral?
A: Show answer
In a continuous-output model, the cost attributed to the 25th unit can be approximated by integrating marginal cost from output 24 to output 25. More generally, the incremental cost associated with moving from n − 1 to n units is the integral of C'(x) over that interval. This is different from integrating from zero to n, which gives the variable-cost increase for the first n units. The chapter's example uses the interval from 24 to 25 to calculate the cost of the 25th item. The method treats output as continuous for modelling purposes, even though physical items are discrete.
Study instruction:
- Translate phrases such as first n units, units a through b, and the nth unit into their corresponding integration limits before calculating.
Key point:
- The nth unit corresponds to the interval from n − 1 to n in this continuous model.
- Integration limits must match the exact production range described.
Source links:
-
29. Q: What is the difference between variable cost obtained by integrating marginal cost and total cost including fixed costs?
A: Show answer
Integrating C'(x) from zero to x gives C(x) − C(0), which is the change in cost from zero output. If C(0) is a fixed cost, that amount must be added to obtain total cost. For example, if fixed costs are 20,000 dollars, a variable-cost function of 0.3x^3 + 0.25x^2 becomes total cost C(x) = 0.3x^3 + 0.25x^2 + 20,000. Confusing these quantities understates total cost by the fixed-cost amount. The correct initial condition should come from the problem statement or applicable business information, not an unsupported assumption that cost at zero output is always zero.
Study instruction:
- Identify C(0) in a cost problem and show separately how it changes the integrated variable-cost expression into total cost.
Key point:
- Marginal cost integration determines the change in cost.
- Fixed costs are captured by the initial value C(0).
Source links:
-
30. Q: How can a total cost function be derived from a marginal cost function and fixed costs?
A: Show answer
Integrate the marginal cost function to obtain a general cost function with C, then use the fixed-cost condition C(0) to determine the constant. For example, if C'(x) = 0.9x^2 + 0.5x and fixed costs are 20,000 dollars, integration gives C(x) = 0.3x^3 + 0.25x^2 + C. Since C(0) = 20,000, the constant is 20,000. The final function is C(x) = 0.3x^3 + 0.25x^2 + 20,000. Substituting a production quantity then gives the modelled total cost at that output, subject to the assumptions and units of the original model.
Study instruction:
- Solve a marginal-cost problem by integrating first, applying the fixed-cost condition second, and evaluating total cost only after the constant is known.
Key point:
- Integrate marginal cost before applying the fixed-cost condition.
- The initial cost determines the constant in the total cost function.
Source links:
-
31. Q: How is a total revenue function recovered from marginal revenue?
A: Show answer
Marginal revenue is the derivative of total revenue, MR = R'(x). Integrating MR gives a general revenue function R(x) = ∫ MR dx. If the business has zero revenue when it sells zero units, the condition R(0) = 0 determines the integration constant. For example, if MR = 40 − 0.02x, then R(x) = 40x − 0.01x^2 + C; using R(0) = 0 gives C = 0. The resulting total revenue function describes revenue at each quantity under the model. If the initial revenue is not zero, the supplied condition must be used instead of assuming it.
Study instruction:
- Integrate a marginal-revenue function and use the stated revenue at zero sales or another initial quantity to determine the constant.
Key point:
- Total revenue is an antiderivative of marginal revenue.
- An initial revenue condition is needed to determine the constant.
Source links:
-
32. Q: How are total revenue, average revenue, and marginal revenue related?
A: Show answer
Total revenue R(x) is the amount received from selling x units. Marginal revenue R'(x) measures the additional revenue associated with a small increase in sales, while average revenue R(x)/x is revenue per unit for x greater than zero. When revenue equals price multiplied by quantity, average revenue corresponds to the price-demand function. These measures answer different questions: total revenue is an aggregate amount, marginal revenue is a rate of change with respect to quantity, and average revenue is a per-unit average. A business should not substitute one measure for another when evaluating pricing, sales expansion, or the revenue effect of an additional unit.
Study instruction:
- Given a total-revenue function, calculate its derivative and its average-revenue expression, then explain the distinct meaning and units of each.
Key point:
- Marginal revenue is the derivative of total revenue.
- Average revenue is total revenue divided by quantity and corresponds to unit price in the chapter's model.
Source links:
-
33. Q: How can a business identify the quantity that maximizes total revenue using marginal revenue?
A: Show answer
A candidate interior maximum of total revenue occurs where marginal revenue R'(x) equals zero. Solve MR = 0 to identify the stationary quantity, then check that the point is a maximum rather than a minimum or another stationary point. This can be done by examining the sign of MR on either side, using the second derivative when appropriate, and considering feasible production or market boundaries. For example, if MR = 4 − 0.5x, setting MR to zero gives x = 8; the corresponding total-revenue function is R(x) = 4x − 0.25x^2, which has a downward-opening quadratic shape and reaches its maximum at 8. The result is conditional on the model's feasible range.
Study instruction:
- Solve MR = 0, inspect the sign or second derivative, and compare the candidate with any quantity constraints or endpoints.
Key point:
- MR = 0 identifies a candidate stationary revenue level.
- A maximum must be confirmed using curvature, sign changes, and feasible boundaries.
Source links:
-
34. Q: How can revenue accumulated over time be calculated when price and sales rate vary?
A: Show answer
If P(t) is the price per unit and q(t) is the sales rate in units per unit of time, the instantaneous revenue rate is P(t)q(t). Total revenue over [t1,t2] is the integral of that rate over time. The units confirm the interpretation: dollars per unit multiplied by units per hour gives dollars per hour, and integrating over hours gives dollars. Care is needed if a supplied Q(t) represents cumulative units sold rather than a sales rate. In that case, the rate is Q'(t), so the revenue-rate expression would use P(t)Q'(t). The variable must be interpreted consistently before applying the formula.
Study instruction:
- Check whether a quantity function represents a cumulative total or a rate, and write the revenue-rate units before integrating over time.
Key point:
- Total revenue is the integral of price multiplied by sales rate.
- A cumulative quantity function must be differentiated before it is used as a rate.
Source links:
-
35. Q: How is a profit function derived when marginal cost and marginal revenue are known?
A: Show answer
Profit is total revenue minus total cost: P(x) = R(x) − C(x). If marginal revenue and marginal cost are supplied, integrate each separately to recover R(x) and C(x), applying the relevant initial conditions. Then subtract total cost from total revenue. The constants may differ because the revenue and cost functions can have different values at zero output. In particular, fixed costs can make C(0) positive even when R(0) is zero, so profit at zero production may be negative. It is therefore unsafe to assume that profit equals zero at zero output unless the problem supplies that condition or the business model justifies it.
Study instruction:
- Derive R(x) and C(x) separately, apply their own initial conditions, and only then calculate P(x) = R(x) − C(x).
Key point:
- Profit is total revenue less total cost.
- Fixed costs and distinct integration constants affect profit at zero output.
Source links:
-
36. Q: What is consumer surplus in the inverse-demand model?
A: Show answer
If inverse demand is p = D(x), the market price is p0, and the quantity demanded at that price is x0, consumer surplus is the area under the demand curve and above the market-price line from zero to x0. It can be calculated as CS = ∫ from 0 to x0 of D(x) dx − p0x0, or equivalently as ∫ from 0 to x0 of [D(x) − p0] dx. The demand curve represents consumers' willingness to pay for successive units, while the market price is what they actually pay. The difference measures the aggregate surplus in the model, not necessarily a cash refund or a saving received equally by every buyer.
Study instruction:
- Draw the inverse-demand curve and market-price line, shade the region above price and below demand, and write the corresponding integral.
Key point:
- Consumer surplus is willingness-to-pay area less actual expenditure.
- The inverse-demand formula integrates from zero to the equilibrium or specified quantity.
Source links:
-
37. Q: How is consumer surplus calculated when the demand function gives price as a function of quantity?
A: Show answer
First determine the market quantity x0 by using the market price p0 in the inverse-demand function p = D(x). Then calculate the area between the demand curve and the price line. For example, if p = 50 − 2x and p0 = 30, solving 30 = 50 − 2x gives x0 = 10. Consumer surplus is ∫ from 0 to 10 of [(50 − 2x) − 30] dx, which equals 100. The calculation requires both the quantity consistent with the market price and the difference between willingness to pay and the price actually paid. Substituting the wrong quantity changes the region and the result.
Study instruction:
- Solve the demand equation for x0 before integrating and check that the demand price at x0 equals the stated market price.
Key point:
- Determine the market quantity before calculating consumer surplus.
- Integrate the demand-price difference over the relevant quantity range.
Source links:
-
38. Q: How is consumer surplus expressed when quantity demanded is written as a function of price?
A: Show answer
If demand is expressed as x = D(p), the surplus can be represented horizontally rather than vertically. The market price p0 is the lower price boundary, and m0 is the highest willingness-to-pay price where demand falls to zero. The area is expressed as the integral of D(p) with respect to price from p0 to m0. This formulation measures the horizontal quantity at each price level over the relevant price range. It is an alternative representation of the same surplus region, provided the inverse relationship and limits are defined correctly. The variable of integration and the interpretation of the demand function must be kept consistent.
Study instruction:
- Sketch the demand curve with price on the vertical axis and quantity on the horizontal axis, then identify the price limits and the quantity function used in the integral.
Key point:
- Consumer surplus can be represented using either inverse demand or quantity demand.
- The integration variable determines the relevant orientation and limits.
Source links:
-
39. Q: What is producer surplus in the inverse-supply model?
A: Show answer
If inverse supply is p = S(x), the market price is p0, and the quantity supplied is x0, producer surplus is the area below the market-price line and above the supply curve from zero to x0. It is calculated as PS = p0x0 − ∫ from 0 to x0 of S(x) dx, or equivalently ∫ from 0 to x0 of [p0 − S(x)] dx. The supply curve represents the minimum price associated with supplying each successive unit in the model, while the market price is received for those units. Producer surplus is an economic surplus measure and should not automatically be equated with accounting profit, because it does not by itself deduct all fixed or other business costs.
Study instruction:
- Draw the inverse-supply curve and market-price line, shade the region between them, and express the vertical difference as an integral.
Key point:
- Producer surplus is market-revenue area less the area under supply.
- Producer surplus is not the same as total accounting profit.
Source links:
-
40. Q: How is producer surplus calculated using a linear supply function?
A: Show answer
Suppose supply is p = 2x and x0 = 10. The market price is p0 = S(10) = 20. The market-revenue rectangle is p0x0 = 200, while the area under the supply curve is ∫ from 0 to 10 of 2x dx = 100. Producer surplus is therefore 200 − 100 = 100. The same result follows by integrating p0 − S(x) over the interval. The calculation illustrates why both the market price and the quantity supplied are needed. If either is incorrect, the rectangle and the area under supply will not describe the actual surplus region in the model.
Study instruction:
- Calculate p0 from the supply function, then evaluate the market-revenue rectangle and subtract the integral under supply.
Key point:
- Producer surplus is the gap between market receipts and the supply-curve area.
- Verify the market price and quantity before calculating the surplus.
Source links:
-
41. Q: How can market equilibrium be found from demand and supply functions?
A: Show answer
Equilibrium occurs where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied at the same price. If both functions express price as a function of quantity, set inverse demand equal to inverse supply and solve for x; substitute the equilibrium quantity into either function to obtain p0. If both functions express quantity as a function of price, set demand equal to supply and solve for price first, then calculate the quantity. The variable orientation matters. Once the equilibrium price and quantity are known, they can be used as the limits and reference values for consumer- and producer-surplus calculations. A solution should also be checked against the economically feasible domain.
Study instruction:
- Identify whether demand and supply are written as price functions or quantity functions, solve the matching equations, and verify both quantities agree at equilibrium.
Key point:
- Equilibrium equates demand and supply at a common price and quantity.
- The algebraic method depends on how the functions are expressed.
Source links:
-
42. Q: How are consumer surplus and producer surplus different?
A: Show answer
Consumer surplus measures the difference between consumers' willingness to pay and the market price they pay. Producer surplus measures the difference between the market price received and the minimum prices represented by the supply curve. Both are areas derived from demand and supply relationships, but they lie on opposite sides of the market-price line. Consumer surplus is generally above the price line and below demand; producer surplus is below the price line and above supply. Neither measure should be confused with total revenue, total cost, or accounting profit. They are economic measures of gains relative to the market price under the assumptions of the demand-and-supply model.
Study instruction:
- Draw demand, supply, and the market-price line on one graph and label the consumer- and producer-surplus regions separately.
Key point:
- Consumer surplus reflects buyer willingness to pay above price.
- Producer surplus reflects seller willingness to accept below price.
Source links:
-
43. Q: How is a midpoint Riemann sum calculated for a specific interval?
A: Show answer
Divide the interval into n equal pieces, calculate Δx = (b − a)/n, find the midpoint of each subinterval, evaluate the function at those midpoints, and add the rectangle areas f(x_i)Δx. For example, for f(x) = 3x on [0,20] with n = 2, the width is 10 and the midpoints are 5 and 15. The estimate is 3(5)(10) + 3(15)(10) = 600. The example is exact because the function is linear, but midpoint sums for curved functions generally approximate rather than exactly equal the integral. Clear tabulation of midpoints and heights reduces arithmetic errors.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a table with subinterval, midpoint, function value, and rectangle contribution before adding the Riemann sum.
Key point:
- Midpoint estimates use the function value at each subinterval's center.
- Each rectangle contribution is height multiplied by Δx.
Source links:
-
44. Q: Why can different sample-point choices produce different Riemann-sum estimates?
A: Show answer
A rectangle's height depends on the point chosen within its subinterval. Midpoint, left-endpoint, and right-endpoint choices can therefore produce different sums for the same function and partition. The chapter uses midpoints to keep its calculations consistent, but the general definition permits other sample points. For an increasing function, left-endpoint rectangles often lie below the curve and right-endpoint rectangles often lie above it; the reverse tendency occurs for a decreasing function. These are useful intuition checks, not substitutes for examining the function's behavior. As the partition becomes finer, suitable sums converge to the same definite integral when the function is integrable.
Study instruction:
- Calculate left-, right-, and midpoint sums for a simple increasing function and explain why their estimates differ.
Key point:
- Sample-point choice affects a finite approximation.
- Appropriate Riemann sums converge to the same integral as the partition is refined.
Source links:
-
45. Q: How can an integral represent the total change in a business quantity?
A: Show answer
In many business applications, the integrand is a rate of change such as marginal cost, marginal revenue, or another quantity measured per unit of activity. Integrating that rate over an interval gives the total change in the underlying quantity over the interval. For example, integrating marginal cost from output a to output b gives the increase in total cost between those production levels. This is an application of the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus: the accumulated rate equals the difference between the underlying function's endpoint values. The interpretation depends on the units and on the integrand genuinely representing the relevant rate.
Study instruction:
- For each business rate in a problem, identify the underlying total function and state what change the definite integral measures.
Key point:
- Integrating a rate accumulates the corresponding change.
- The limits specify the starting and ending activity levels or times.
Source links:
-
46. Q: How can a marginal-cost integral distinguish the cost of the first units from the cost of later units?
A: Show answer
The integration limits define which production range is being measured. Integrating from zero to 20 gives the variable-cost increase for the first 20 units. Integrating from 50 to 100 gives the additional cost of moving from 50 units to 100 units, corresponding to the 51st through 100th units in the chapter's interpretation. Integrating from 24 to 25 estimates the incremental cost of the 25th unit. The same marginal-cost function can therefore produce different answers because the intervals represent different questions. The total cost at a final output level may additionally include fixed costs or an initial cost balance.
Study instruction:
- Translate each verbal production range into lower and upper output limits before substituting the marginal-cost function.
Key point:
- Integration limits determine the production range whose incremental cost is measured.
- Total cost may require adding an initial fixed-cost amount.
Source links:
-
47. Q: How should the constant of integration be handled when cost, revenue, and profit functions are derived separately?
A: Show answer
Each integrated function has its own constant until its initial condition is applied. Cost and revenue should not automatically share the same constant because they represent different economic quantities. For example, revenue may be zero at zero sales while cost remains positive because the business incurs fixed costs. Profit is then revenue less cost, so its constant reflects the difference between the initial revenue and initial cost. If a problem provides fixed costs, a zero-revenue condition, or another starting value, apply each condition to the function it describes. This avoids the common error of forcing profit to zero at zero output despite the existence of fixed costs.
Study instruction:
- Write separate general functions for cost and revenue, label their constants, apply each initial condition, and then derive profit.
Key point:
- Different total functions can have different integration constants.
- Profit at zero output may be negative when fixed costs exist.
Source links:
-
48. Q: What common mistakes should be avoided when applying integration rules?
A: Show answer
Frequent mistakes include forgetting the constant of integration, applying the power rule when the exponent is −1, increasing an exponent without dividing by the new exponent, omitting the logarithm-base adjustment, and failing to check the domain of a logarithmic expression. Other errors include mishandling negative signs, integrating a sum incorrectly, confusing the variable of integration, and failing to verify the answer by differentiation. In definite integrals, students may reverse endpoint order, subtract the lower value incorrectly, or confuse signed area with geometric area. A disciplined process of identifying the rule, checking its conditions, and differentiating the result prevents many of these errors.
Study instruction:
- Build a personal error checklist and use it to review every indefinite and definite integral before accepting the result.
Key point:
- Rule conditions and constants matter as much as the mechanical calculation.
- Differentiation and endpoint checks catch many integration errors.
Source links:
-
49. Q: What errors commonly occur when calculating consumer or producer surplus?
A: Show answer
Common errors include using the wrong market quantity, substituting the wrong market price, confusing inverse demand with quantity demand, reversing the vertical difference, and integrating the wrong function. Consumer surplus requires demand minus market price in the inverse-demand formulation; producer surplus requires market price minus supply. A student may also calculate the area under demand or supply but forget to subtract the price rectangle, or may treat the surplus as total profit. The best control is to sketch the graph, label the market equilibrium, shade the required region, and derive the integral from the geometry before performing the algebra.
Study instruction:
- For a surplus problem, draw and label the graph first, write the relevant area difference, and check whether the resulting surplus should be nonnegative.
Key point:
- Consumer surplus uses demand above price; producer surplus uses price above supply.
- A diagram helps prevent reversed differences and incorrect limits.
Source links:
-
50. Q: How can a business interpret a calculated integral without overstating what the model proves?
A: Show answer
A mathematical result is conditional on the supplied functions, units, interval, and assumptions. An integral of marginal cost estimates a cost change only if the marginal-cost function adequately represents the relevant production range. A revenue integral depends on whether the quantity function is a sales rate or cumulative total. Surplus calculations depend on the demand and supply relationships and the market price and quantity used. The result does not by itself establish that the model reflects actual market behavior, that all costs are included, or that a business decision is optimal under every constraint. Decision-makers should verify inputs, feasible ranges, units, and assumptions before relying on the numerical result.
Study instruction:
- For each business-integral result, list the function source, units, interval, assumptions, and limitations before explaining its managerial meaning.
Key point:
- Integration is mathematically precise only relative to the supplied model.
- Business conclusions require validation of data, assumptions, and constraints.
Source links:

51. Q: How can a marginal-revenue model be used to calculate revenue at a stated sales quantity?
A: Show answer
Suppose MR = 40 − 0.02x and revenue is zero when x = 0. Integrating gives R(x) = 40x − 0.01x^2 + C. Applying R(0) = 0 gives C = 0. At x = 1,500, R(1,500) = 40(1,500) − 0.01(1,500)^2 = 60,000 − 22,500 = 37,500. The calculation demonstrates the full sequence: integrate marginal revenue, use the initial condition, and substitute the requested quantity. The result is revenue in the units implied by the model, and the quantity should remain within the range for which the marginal-revenue function is appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the integration and initial-condition steps, then evaluate the function at the specified quantity with units shown.
Key point:
- The marginal-revenue function must be integrated before total revenue is evaluated.
- The initial condition determines the constant and affects the result.
Source links:
-
52. Q: How can a cost model with fixed costs be used to calculate total production cost?
A: Show answer
Suppose marginal cost is C'(q) = 0.6q^2 + 2q and fixed costs are 2,000. Integrating gives C(q) = 0.2q^3 + q^2 + C. Since C(0) = 2,000, the total-cost function is C(q) = 0.2q^3 + q^2 + 2,000. At q = 25, the variable portion is 0.2(25^3) + 25^2 = 3,125 + 625 = 3,750, so total cost is 5,750. This example shows why fixed costs must be included after integrating marginal cost and why the total-cost function, rather than only the integral from zero, is used to answer a total-cost question.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the integrated variable cost separately from fixed costs and verify the final total by substituting the stated production quantity.
Key point:
- Fixed costs enter through the initial condition C(0).
- Total cost equals variable cost plus the fixed-cost amount in this model.
Source links:
-
53. Q: How can consumer surplus be calculated from a nonlinear demand function?
A: Show answer
Suppose inverse demand is p = 30 − x − 0.5x^2 and the specified market quantity is x0 = 4. First calculate p0 = D(4) = 30 − 4 − 0.5(16) = 18. Consumer surplus is the integral of demand minus price from zero to four: ∫ from 0 to 4 of [30 − x − 0.5x^2 − 18] dx. This is 48 − 8 − 32/3 = 88/3, or approximately 29.33 monetary units. The example illustrates that a nonlinear demand curve does not change the underlying area logic; it changes only the antiderivative and arithmetic required to evaluate the region.
Study instruction:
- Find the market price from the demand function before setting up the consumer-surplus integral, then check the area using a graph.
Key point:
- Consumer surplus is the area between inverse demand and the market price.
- Nonlinear demand requires integrating the full function difference.
Source links:
-
54. Q: How can producer surplus be calculated from a supply function with a nonzero intercept?
A: Show answer
Suppose inverse supply is p = 5 + 0.5x and the supplied quantity is x0 = 8. The market price is p0 = 5 + 0.5(8) = 9. The market-revenue rectangle is 9 × 8 = 72. The area under supply is ∫ from 0 to 8 of (5 + 0.5x) dx = 40 + 16 = 56. Producer surplus is 72 − 56 = 16. The nonzero intercept matters because the supply curve begins above zero price; omitting the constant term would overstate the surplus. The calculation should always use the complete supply function and the price consistent with the specified quantity.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate the supply function at the market quantity and integrate every term, including any constant intercept.
Key point:
- A supply intercept contributes to the area under the supply curve.
- Producer surplus is market receipts less the integrated supply amount.
Source links:
-
55. Q: What is a reliable step-by-step method for solving integration problems in business and economics?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying what the function represents: a marginal rate, demand price, supply price, or rate over time. Confirm the independent variable, units, domain, and interval. Select the appropriate integration rule or identify the area represented by the definite integral. For an indefinite integral, include C and use any initial condition to determine it. For a definite integral, use an antiderivative and evaluate upper minus lower. For geometric areas or surplus, sketch the curves, identify the relevant region, and split intervals if the curves change order or the integrand changes sign. Finally, check the result by differentiation, endpoint substitution, units, and a reasonableness review against the original business question.
Study instruction:
- Apply this sequence to one marginal-cost problem, one revenue problem, and one surplus problem, recording the model assumptions and final units.
Key point:
- Interpret the function and interval before choosing an integration method.
- Verify the algebra, units, assumptions, and economic meaning before using the result.

Table 7
Integration Techniques and Business Applications
1. Q: What are the main integration techniques developed in Chapter 7, and how do they extend the basic integration rules?
A: Show answer
Chapter 7 develops three techniques for integrals that cannot be handled conveniently by applying a basic antiderivative rule directly: integration by substitution, integration by parts, and integration using partial fractions. Substitution reverses the chain rule by replacing a useful inner expression and its differential with a new variable. Integration by parts rearranges the product rule so that a difficult product integral can be expressed using a more manageable integral. Partial fractions decompose a rational function into simpler fractions whose antiderivatives are known. The chapter then applies these methods to cumulative sales, subscriber growth, resource depletion, depreciation, revenue, interest, and income probabilities. The methods are complementary: a business model may require more than one technique in sequence.
Study instruction:
- "Make a one-page comparison of the three methods, identifying the algebraic pattern that signals each method and the information needed to complete it."
Key point:
- Substitution reverses composition, parts reorganizes products, and partial fractions decomposes rational functions.
- Business applications usually integrate a rate to obtain a cumulative quantity.
Source links:
-
2. Q: What is integration by substitution, and when is it useful?
A: Show answer
Integration by substitution is a method for simplifying an integral containing a composite function. The method replaces an inner expression, commonly written as u = u(x), with a new variable u. Its differential du = u'(x) dx is used to replace the corresponding part of the original integrand. The goal is to convert the entire integral into an expression involving u and du only, integrate that simpler expression, and then substitute the original expression for u. It is especially useful when the integrand contains a function and, up to a constant factor, the derivative of its inner expression. It is not enough to identify a plausible inner expression: the differential must also be accounted for completely.
Study instruction:
- "For several composite integrands, identify a possible inner function and check whether its derivative is present in the remaining factors."
Key point:
- A successful substitution must transform both the integrand and the variable of integration.
- The transformed integral should contain no leftover x terms.
Source links:
-
3. Q: Why is integration by substitution described as the reverse of the chain rule?
A: Show answer
The chain rule differentiates a composite function by differentiating the outer function and multiplying by the derivative of the inner function. If a derivative has the form f'(u(x))u'(x), integration can reverse that process by setting u = u(x) and du = u'(x) dx. The integral then becomes the antiderivative of f'(u) with respect to u. For example, differentiating (2/3)(x+5)^(3/2) produces the square root of x+5. Reversing the derivative therefore requires recognizing x+5 as the inner expression and replacing dx with du because du = dx. The method is broader than memorizing special exponential or logarithmic patterns; it applies whenever the chain-rule structure can be recovered.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a composite expression first, then reverse the resulting derivative using a substitution and compare the result with the original function."
Key point:
- The chain rule supplies the structural pattern that substitution reverses.
- The derivative of the inner expression must be represented in the differential.
Source links:
-
4. Q: How should the inner function u be selected in an integration-by-substitution problem?
A: Show answer
Choose u as the expression whose derivative is present, or can be made present by multiplying or dividing by a constant. It is often the inner part of a composite expression, such as x^3+7 inside a square root, 2x-4 inside a power, or -2x in an exponential. The best choice makes du simple and reduces the integral to a familiar power, exponential, or logarithmic form. A useful test is to differentiate the proposed u and compare u'(x) dx with the other factors in the original integrand. If the remaining expression cannot be rewritten entirely in terms of u, the substitution is incomplete and another technique or an algebraic rearrangement may be needed.
Study instruction:
- "For each candidate u, write du explicitly before changing the integral and reject choices that leave unrelated x terms behind."
Key point:
- Choose u to simplify the whole integral, not merely to rename a complicated expression.
- The derivative of u is the test of whether the choice is useful.
Source links:
-
5. Q: Why must the differential dx be converted when an integral is rewritten using u?
A: Show answer
The differential identifies the variable with respect to which integration is performed. If the integrand is rewritten as a function of u while the integral still ends in dx, the expression mixes variables and the substitution has not been completed. From u = u(x), differentiate to obtain du = u'(x) dx, then solve for the differential needed in the integral. For example, if u = x^3+7, then du = 3x^2 dx and x^2 dx = du/3. Replacing x^2 dx with du/3 changes both the integrand and the variable of integration. Every remaining factor must also be expressible in terms of u.
Study instruction:
- "Practice writing the equation for du and solving it for the exact differential factor appearing in the original integral."
Key point:
- Changing the inner expression without changing dx leaves an invalid or incomplete substitution.
- The complete transformed integral uses u and du only.
Source links:
-
6. Q: How does substitution solve the integral of the square root of x+5?
A: Show answer
For the integral of sqrt(x+5) dx, set u = x+5. Differentiating gives du = dx, so the integral becomes the integral of u^(1/2) du. Apply the power rule to obtain u^(3/2)/(3/2), or (2/3)u^(3/2), and substitute x+5 back for u. The result is (2/3)(x+5)^(3/2)+C. This example is useful because the inner expression has derivative 1, so the differential conversion is immediate. It also illustrates the complete sequence: identify the inner function, differentiate it, rewrite the integral, integrate, and return to the original variable.
Study instruction:
- "Rework the example without looking at the solution, then differentiate your antiderivative to confirm that it returns sqrt(x+5)."
Key point:
- The power rule is applied to the new variable after substitution.
- Always verify an indefinite integral by differentiation.
Source links:
-
7. Q: How is substitution applied to an integral containing x squared and the square root of x cubed plus 7?
A: Show answer
For the integral of x^2 sqrt(x^3+7) dx, choose u = x^3+7 because its derivative is 3x^2. Thus du = 3x^2 dx and x^2 dx = du/3. The integral becomes one-third of the integral of u^(1/2) du. Applying the power rule gives (1/3)(2/3)u^(3/2), or (2/9)(x^3+7)^(3/2)+C. The important feature is the matching factor x^2 dx. The derivative of the inner expression is not identical to that factor, but it differs only by the constant 3, which can be handled outside the integral.
Study instruction:
- "Solve the integral step by step and explain why the factor of one-third appears when converting x^2 dx to du."
Key point:
- A constant multiple of the inner derivative is sufficient for substitution.
- Keep the constant factor throughout the calculation.
Source links:
-
8. Q: How does substitution work for an expression such as (2x-4) cubed?
A: Show answer
For the integral of (2x-4)^3 dx, let u = 2x-4. Then du = 2 dx, so dx = du/2. The integral becomes one-half the integral of u^3 du, which equals u^4/8+C. Substituting back gives (2x-4)^4/8+C. This example demonstrates that the derivative of the inner expression may be a constant other than 1. The constant must be included when converting dx. Omitting the factor of one-half would produce an antiderivative whose derivative is twice the required integrand.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the proposed answer and compare coefficients to see why the factor created by du = 2 dx is essential."
Key point:
- Solve the differential equation for dx before integrating.
- A missing constant multiplier is a common source of substitution errors.
Source links:
-
9. Q: How is an exponential integral such as the integral of e to the power of negative 2x evaluated?
A: Show answer
Set u = -2x. Then du = -2 dx, so dx = -du/2. The integral of e^(-2x) dx becomes negative one-half the integral of e^u du, which is -(1/2)e^u+C. Substituting back gives -(1/2)e^(-2x)+C. The negative coefficient is not optional: it comes directly from the derivative of the exponent. This is a general pattern for integrating an exponential of a linear expression. If the exponent is ax, the antiderivative of e^(ax) is e^(ax)/a+C, provided a is nonzero.
Study instruction:
- "Work two examples with positive and negative coefficients in the exponent and check each by differentiation."
Key point:
- The coefficient in the exponent appears as a divisor in the antiderivative.
- Preserve the sign produced by the differential substitution.
Source links:
-
10. Q: How are definite integrals handled after making a substitution?
A: Show answer
A definite integral can be evaluated by finding an antiderivative and applying the original limits, or by converting the limits to the new variable and integrating entirely in u. When changing variables, convert both endpoints using the substitution. For example, if u = -2x and x runs from 0 to 1, the corresponding u-limits are 0 and -2. The chapter's exponential example evaluates the antiderivative at the original x-limits and subtracts the lower value from the upper value. Unlike an indefinite integral, a definite integral does not require an arbitrary constant of integration because the constant cancels when the endpoint values are subtracted.
Study instruction:
- "Solve one definite integral using the original limits and again using converted u-limits; confirm that both methods agree."
Key point:
- If the variable changes, the limits must be handled consistently.
- Definite integrals do not retain a free constant of integration.
Source links:
-
11. Q: Why is a constant of integration required for an indefinite integral but not for a definite integral?
A: Show answer
An indefinite integral represents a family of antiderivatives. Since the derivative of a constant is zero, infinitely many functions differ only by an additive constant and have the same derivative. The notation +C records that family. A definite integral instead produces a numerical accumulation over an interval by evaluating an antiderivative at the upper endpoint and subtracting its value at the lower endpoint. If the antiderivative contains C, the subtraction is (F(b)+C)-(F(a)+C), so C cancels. The distinction matters when a business model requires a cumulative function: an initial condition is then used to determine the particular constant for that model.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an indefinite antiderivative with a definite integral and demonstrate algebraically how the constant cancels in endpoint subtraction."
Key point:
- Use +C for indefinite integrals.
- Use endpoint evaluation for definite integrals and initial conditions for cumulative models.
Source links:
-
12. Q: How does substitution produce a logarithm when the numerator is the derivative of the denominator?
A: Show answer
Consider the integral of (2x+4)/(x^2+4x) dx. Let u = x^2+4x, so du = (2x+4) dx. The integral becomes the integral of 1/u du, whose antiderivative is ln(abs(u))+C. Substituting back gives ln(abs(x^2+4x))+C. The logarithm uses the absolute value because the real antiderivative of 1/u is defined on intervals where u is nonzero and can be positive or negative. The denominator's zeros must be excluded from the domain of the antiderivative. This pattern is common when a rational integrand contains a denominator and its derivative in the numerator.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the denominator and differentiate it; then determine whether the numerator is an exact or constant multiple of that derivative."
Key point:
- The integral of 1/u is ln(abs(u))+C on intervals that do not cross u=0.
- The numerator-denominator derivative relationship is the key recognition test.
Source links:
-
13. Q: Why can a seemingly suitable substitution fail when the numerator does not match the derivative of the inner expression?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's contrasting example, the integrand has numerator 2x+6 and denominator x^2+4x. The natural choice u = x^2+4x gives du = (2x+4) dx, not (2x+6) dx. After expressing the denominator as u, the numerator still contains an x-dependent difference, so the integral cannot be converted into an expression involving u and du alone by that substitution. This does not prove that the original integral has no antiderivative or cannot be evaluated by another method. It shows only that the proposed substitution, used directly, is insufficient. A valid method must account for every factor.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the numerator with the derivative of the denominator and write the unmatched term explicitly before deciding whether substitution is complete."
Key point:
- A plausible inner function does not guarantee a workable substitution.
- Failure of one substitution is a signal to reconsider the method, not to force the algebra.
Source links:
-
14. Q: How can an antiderivative obtained by substitution be checked reliably?
A: Show answer
Differentiate the proposed antiderivative with respect to the original variable and simplify the result. The derivative must equal the original integrand on the relevant domain. This check is particularly effective for substitution because it tests whether the chain-rule factor and any constant multiplier were preserved. For a definite integral, an independent check can include evaluating the antiderivative at both endpoints and confirming that the order is upper minus lower. For business applications, also check that the units of the result are consistent with the quantity being accumulated. A mathematically correct expression can still be misinterpreted if its units or initial condition are wrong.
Study instruction:
- "Build a verification habit: differentiate, simplify, check the domain, and confirm units before using the result in a business model."
Key point:
- Differentiation is the principal check for an indefinite integral.
- Domain, endpoint, and unit checks protect the interpretation of the result.
Source links:
-
15. Q: What is integration by parts, and how is its formula derived from the product rule?
A: Show answer
Integration by parts is a technique for integrating a product when direct integration or substitution is inconvenient. Begin with the product rule: the derivative of fg is f'g+fg'. Integrate both sides with respect to x. The integral of the derivative of fg is fg, up to a constant, so rearranging gives the formula integral of f g' dx = fg - integral of f'g dx. The method transfers the derivative from one factor to the other while replacing the other factor with an antiderivative. It is useful when one factor becomes simpler after differentiation and the other has an accessible antiderivative. The choice of factors determines whether the transformed integral is easier.
Study instruction:
- "Derive the integration-by-parts formula from the product rule without memorizing it, then identify which factor is differentiated and which is integrated."
Key point:
- Integration by parts is the integral form of the product rule.
- Its success depends on choosing factors that simplify the remaining integral.
Source links:
-
16. Q: What is the definite integration-by-parts formula, and what does the boundary term represent?
A: Show answer
For limits a to b, integration by parts is integral from a to b of f g' dx = [fg] from a to b minus integral from a to b of f'g dx. The bracketed term means evaluate fg at b and subtract its value at a. It is the boundary contribution created by the product rule. The remaining integral accounts for the effect of differentiating f and integrating g'. Omitting the boundary term changes the result. The definite formula is especially useful in applications where a cumulative quantity is measured over a fixed period, such as depreciation or production during the first several years.
Study instruction:
- "Solve a definite integration-by-parts problem and write the boundary evaluation separately before subtracting the remaining integral."
Key point:
- The definite formula includes both the endpoint term and the residual integral.
- Evaluate the upper endpoint first and subtract the lower endpoint.
Source links:
-
17. Q: How should the two factors be selected when applying integration by parts?
A: Show answer
Select f so that its derivative f' is easy to calculate and preferably simpler than f. Select g' so that its antiderivative g is also easy to find. The chapter emphasizes both requirements: it is not enough for one factor to simplify if the other factor becomes harder to integrate. For example, in the integral of x e^x, choosing f=x and g'=e^x works because f'=1 and g=e^x. The resulting integral is simply the integral of e^x. A poor choice may create a more complicated residual integral and defeat the purpose of the method. Factor selection is therefore a judgment step, not a mechanical rearrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Before applying the formula, write f, f', g', and g in a four-part table and assess whether the new integral is simpler than the original."
Key point:
- Differentiate the factor that becomes simpler.
- Integrate the factor whose antiderivative is manageable.
Source links:
-
18. Q: How does integration by parts evaluate the integral of the natural logarithm of x?
A: Show answer
Treat ln(x) as the factor f and 1 as g'. Then f' = 1/x and g = x. Applying the formula gives integral ln(x) dx = x ln(x) - integral x(1/x) dx, which simplifies to x ln(x)-x+C. The example illustrates an important choice: a logarithm can be treated as a factor even when it is not visibly multiplied by another function, because it can be written as ln(x) times 1. Differentiating the logarithm produces a simpler rational expression, while integrating 1 is immediate. The result can be verified by applying the product rule to x ln(x)-x.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the derivation and differentiate the final expression, showing how the two constant terms cancel."
Key point:
- A single-function integral can be treated as a product with 1.
- Integration by parts is effective when the logarithm becomes simpler after differentiation.
Source links:
-
19. Q: How is the integral of x squared times ln(x) evaluated using integration by parts?
A: Show answer
Choose f = ln(x) and g' = x^2. Then f' = 1/x and g = x^3/3. The formula gives x^3 ln(x)/3 minus the integral of (1/x)(x^3/3), which is one-third the integral of x^2. Integrating the remaining power produces x^3/9, so the antiderivative is x^3 ln(x)/3 - x^3/9 + C. The choice works because the logarithm becomes 1/x, while the polynomial factor has an easy antiderivative. Differentiating the result confirms that the terms created by the product rule simplify to x^2 ln(x).
Study instruction:
- "Work through the factor selection and verify the final expression by differentiating each term separately."
Key point:
- The logarithm is usually a useful factor to differentiate in this type of example.
- Keep the one-third factor from the antiderivative of x^2 throughout the calculation.
Source links:
-
20. Q: How does integration by parts solve the integral of x times e to the power x?
A: Show answer
Choose f=x and g'=e^x. Then f'=1 and g=e^x. Substitution into the formula gives integral x e^x dx = x e^x - integral e^x dx, which equals x e^x-e^x+C. The derivative of x e^x contains two terms because of the product rule; subtracting e^x removes the extra term and leaves x e^x. This example is a model for products of a polynomial and an exponential function. The polynomial becomes simpler when differentiated, while the exponential remains unchanged when integrated.
Study instruction:
- "Solve the integral and then differentiate x e^x-e^x to show explicitly why the unwanted e^x terms cancel."
Key point:
- A factor such as x may be selected because its derivative is simpler.
- The residual integral should be easier than the original product.
Source links:
-
21. Q: Why does the integral of x squared times e to the power x require integration by parts more than once?
A: Show answer
Let f=x^2 and g'=e^x. Then f'=2x and g=e^x, so the first application produces x^2e^x minus twice the integral of x e^x. The remaining integral is the one from the preceding example and also requires integration by parts. Substituting its result gives x^2e^x-2xe^x+2e^x+C. The method works because repeated differentiation reduces the polynomial degree until a constant remains. This pattern is common for a polynomial multiplied by an exponential function. Each application should reduce the complexity of the residual integral rather than recreate the original problem.
Study instruction:
- "Create a sequence showing the polynomial degree after each differentiation and identify when the repeated process terminates."
Key point:
- Repeated integration by parts is useful when successive derivatives simplify one factor.
- Reuse a previously evaluated residual integral carefully, including its multiplier.
Source links:

22. Q: Can integration by parts be combined with substitution in the same integral?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter's example involving x e^x divided by (x+1)^2 illustrates a choice in which one factor is x e^x and the other is 1/(x+1)^2. The first factor is differentiated using the product rule, while the second is integrated using substitution to obtain an antiderivative of -1/(x+1). Integration by parts then produces a simpler expression whose remaining integral can be evaluated directly. The final antiderivative is e^x/(x+1)+C. This illustrates that integration methods are not mutually exclusive. A problem may be solved efficiently by selecting a method for each sub-expression as it arises.
Study instruction:
- "Rework the example and label where the product rule, substitution, and integration-by-parts formula are each used."
Key point:
- A complex integral may require a sequence of techniques.
- Choose each technique for the specific expression it simplifies.
Source links:
