23. Q: How can an integration-by-parts answer be checked, and why is differentiation particularly useful?
A: Show answer
Differentiate the proposed antiderivative using the product rule wherever a product appears. The derivative should simplify exactly to the original integrand. This check is important because integration by parts contains a subtraction, and errors often occur in the sign of the residual integral or in a coefficient carried through repeated applications. For definite integrals, also verify the endpoint substitution and the order of subtraction. If a result represents cumulative depreciation, revenue, or production, check that differentiating the cumulative function returns the stated rate. This links the mathematical verification to the economic meaning of the model.
Study instruction:
- "Select an integration-by-parts result from the chapter and verify it term by term by differentiation, then check its interpretation as a rate or total where applicable."
Key point:
- The product rule is the main verification tool for integration by parts.
- Check signs, coefficients, endpoints, and units separately.
Source links:
-
24. Q: What common errors should be avoided when applying integration by parts?
A: Show answer
Frequent errors include choosing a factor whose derivative is not simpler, choosing a g' whose antiderivative is difficult, reversing the subtraction in fg minus the residual integral, forgetting a coefficient in f' or g, and stopping before the remaining integral has been evaluated. Repeated applications create additional opportunities to lose a multiplier. Another mistake is omitting +C in an indefinite integral or applying the definite formula without evaluating the boundary term. The best protection is to write f, f', g', and g explicitly before substituting into the formula, then simplify the residual integral carefully and differentiate the final result.
Study instruction:
- "Review a worked solution and annotate every sign, coefficient, antiderivative, and remaining integral before checking it by differentiation."
Key point:
- Most errors arise from factor selection or incomplete bookkeeping.
- A final derivative check can expose sign and coefficient mistakes.
Source links:
-
25. Q: What is the purpose of partial fractions in integration?
A: Show answer
Partial fractions are used to rewrite a rational function, a quotient of polynomials P(x)/Q(x), as a sum of simpler rational expressions. The simpler terms are selected so that their antiderivatives can be found using familiar rules, often substitution and logarithmic integration. The denominator is factored first, and the form of the decomposition depends on the factors present. For example, a denominator made of distinct linear factors leads to separate terms with linear denominators. Repeated linear factors require a term for each power. An unfactorable quadratic factor requires a linear numerator over that quadratic. The method turns an algebraically complicated fraction into several manageable integrals.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the numerator and denominator polynomials, factor the denominator, and write the correct decomposition form before solving for coefficients."
Key point:
- Partial fractions simplify rational functions before integration.
- The denominator's factorization determines the required form of the decomposition.
Source links:
-
26. Q: What should be done when the degree of the numerator is greater than or equal to the degree of the denominator?
A: Show answer
Perform polynomial long division before applying partial fractions. The division expresses the original rational function as a polynomial quotient plus a proper rational remainder whose numerator has lower degree than its denominator. Integrate the polynomial quotient directly using the power rule, then decompose the remainder into partial fractions. Attempting to use the standard proper-fraction forms before division can lead to an incomplete or incorrect decomposition. The chapter explicitly treats long division as the first step when the numerator degree is at least the denominator degree. The degree comparison is therefore a necessary preliminary check.
Study instruction:
- "Compare polynomial degrees before setting up partial fractions and practice separating a rational function into quotient and remainder."
Key point:
- Improper rational functions require long division first.
- Apply partial fractions to the proper remainder, not the undivided expression.
Source links:
-
27. Q: Why must the denominator be factored before a partial-fraction decomposition is written?
A: Show answer
The factorization reveals the structure of the denominator and therefore determines which partial-fraction terms are needed. Distinct linear factors require separate constants over each factor. A repeated linear factor requires a sequence of terms for every power from one through its multiplicity. An irreducible quadratic factor requires a linear numerator because a constant numerator is not general enough to represent every possible remainder over that factor. If the denominator is not factored correctly, the decomposition may omit necessary terms or use an inappropriate form. Factoring also helps identify whether the rational function is proper and whether long division is required first.
Study instruction:
- "Factor each denominator completely over the real numbers and classify every factor as distinct linear, repeated linear, or irreducible quadratic."
Key point:
- Factorization controls the structure of the decomposition.
- Include every required factor and power before solving coefficients.
Source links:
-
28. Q: What partial-fraction form is used when the denominator contains distinct linear factors?
A: Show answer
If the denominator factors into distinct linear terms such as (x+3)(x+5), use one constant numerator over each factor: A/(x+3)+B/(x+5). The constants A and B are unknown until the identity is solved. Multiply both sides by the common denominator to clear fractions, expand, and equate the coefficients of matching powers of x and the constant terms. Once the constants are known, integrate each term separately. The antiderivatives of reciprocal linear expressions are logarithms, with absolute values in the real-valued indefinite integral.
Study instruction:
- "Write a decomposition for a denominator with two and then three distinct linear factors, clear denominators, and solve for all unknown constants."
Key point:
- Each distinct linear factor receives its own constant numerator.
- The resulting reciprocal terms integrate to logarithms.
Source links:
-
29. Q: How are the unknown coefficients in a partial-fraction decomposition determined?
A: Show answer
Multiply the proposed decomposition by the original denominator so that the equation contains no fractions. Expand the right-hand side and collect like powers of x. Then equate the coefficients of corresponding powers on both sides. This creates a system of simultaneous equations in the unknown constants. Solve that system and substitute the values back into the decomposition. A useful verification is to recombine the partial fractions over a common denominator and confirm that the original numerator is recovered. This last step catches algebraic mistakes before integration. The chapter uses coefficient comparison in examples with two and three unknown constants.
Study instruction:
- "Solve one two-unknown and one three-unknown coefficient system, then recombine the fractions to verify the identity."
Key point:
- Clearing denominators converts the decomposition into a polynomial identity.
- Verify the coefficients by reconstructing the original rational function.
Source links:
-
30. Q: How does partial fractions evaluate the integral of (2x+1) divided by x squared minus 1?
A: Show answer
Factor the denominator as (x+1)(x-1) and write (2x+1)/(x^2-1) as A/(x+1)+B/(x-1). Clearing denominators gives 2x+1 = A(x-1)+B(x+1). Comparing coefficients produces A+B=2 and -A+B=1, so A=1/2 and B=3/2. Integrating the decomposed terms gives one-half ln(abs(x+1)) plus three-halves ln(abs(x-1))+C. The example demonstrates the full workflow: factor, decompose, solve coefficients, integrate each reciprocal term, and retain absolute values in logarithms.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the coefficient equations and verify the decomposition by combining the two fractions before integrating."
Key point:
- A rational expression with distinct linear factors becomes a sum of reciprocal linear terms.
- Coefficient comparison determines the constants needed for integration.
Source links:
-
31. Q: How does a repeated linear factor change the form of a partial-fraction decomposition?
A: Show answer
If the denominator contains a repeated factor (x-c)^n, include a separate term for every power from one through n: A1/(x-c)+A2/(x-c)^2 and so on up to An/(x-c)^n. For example, a squared factor requires both a first-power and a second-power term. Including only the highest power is incomplete because the rational function may contain contributions from each lower power. Other distinct factors in the denominator receive their own terms as well. After clearing the denominator, solve the resulting coefficient system and integrate each component. Some terms produce logarithms, while higher reciprocal powers produce power-rule antiderivatives.
Study instruction:
- "For a denominator containing a squared linear factor and another linear factor, write every required term before expanding or solving for coefficients."
Key point:
- A repeated factor requires a ladder of terms for all its powers.
- The decomposition must represent the full rational function, not only its highest repeated power.
Source links:
-
32. Q: How is the example with denominator (x-2) squared times (x+3) decomposed and integrated?
A: Show answer
For the integrand (2x^2-x+4)/((x-2)^2(x+3)), the denominator contains a repeated linear factor and a distinct linear factor. Use A/(x-2)+B/(x-2)^2+C/(x+3). Clearing denominators and comparing coefficients gives A=1, B=2, and C=1. The integral therefore separates into the integrals of 1/(x-2), 2/(x-2)^2, and 1/(x+3). The result is ln(abs(x-2)) - 2/(x-2) + ln(abs(x+3))+C. The repeated-power term is integrated with the power rule after a simple substitution.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the three coefficients by expanding the cleared identity, then integrate each term separately and check by differentiation."
Key point:
- Repeated linear factors generate both logarithmic and power-rule terms.
- Do not omit the lower power term associated with a repeated factor.
Source links:
-
33. Q: What form is used when the denominator contains an unfactorable quadratic factor?
A: Show answer
For an irreducible quadratic factor q(x), use a linear numerator of the form Ax+B over q(x). The numerator must be general enough to represent a first-degree remainder after polynomial division by the quadratic. In the chapter's example, x^2+2 is irreducible over the real numbers, so the decomposition uses (Ax+B)/(x^2+2), together with a separate constant-over-linear term for the other factor. Clearing denominators produces a polynomial identity whose coefficients determine A, B, and any other constants. Once decomposed, the terms can be integrated using substitution when the numerator matches the derivative of the denominator.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a linear numerator is required over an irreducible quadratic and solve the coefficient identity for a simple example."
Key point:
- An irreducible quadratic receives a general linear numerator.
- The decomposition form is determined by the factor type.
Source links:
-
34. Q: How does the partial-fraction example with x squared plus 2 lead to logarithmic antiderivatives?
A: Show answer
The chapter considers (x^2+2x-2)/((x^2+2)(x+1)). The denominator has an irreducible quadratic x^2+2 and a linear factor x+1. Use (Ax+B)/(x^2+2)+C/(x+1). After clearing denominators and comparing coefficients, the constants are A=2, B=0, and C=-1. The integral becomes the integral of 2x/(x^2+2) minus the integral of 1/(x+1). In the first term, the numerator is the derivative of x^2+2, up to a constant; both terms therefore integrate to logarithms. The result is ln(x^2+2)-ln(abs(x+1))+C.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the derivative of each denominator after decomposition and explain why substitution produces a logarithm in both terms."
Key point:
- Partial fractions can reduce an irreducible-quadratic problem to a substitution pattern.
- The numerator's relationship to the denominator derivative determines the antiderivative form.
Source links:

35. Q: How should a learner decide among substitution, integration by parts, and partial fractions?
A: Show answer
Examine the structure of the integrand before calculating. If it is a composite expression accompanied by the derivative of its inner function, test substitution. If it is a product of functions and differentiating one factor while integrating the other is likely to simplify the expression, test integration by parts. If it is a rational function, factor the denominator, compare polynomial degrees, and consider long division and partial fractions. These are recognition guidelines, not rigid rules. Some problems combine methods, as the chapter's logistic-growth and advertising examples do. A method should be selected because it reduces the problem to simpler integrals, not merely because the expression resembles a familiar example.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a mixed set of integrals by structure and write one sentence explaining why the selected technique is appropriate for each."
Key point:
- Choose a method based on the algebraic structure of the integrand.
- Complex problems may require several techniques in sequence.
Source links:
-
36. Q: What should be done when substitution leaves an expression that still contains the original variable?
A: Show answer
Stop and assess whether the substitution is genuinely complete. A valid change of variable must express the entire integrand and differential in terms of the new variable. If an x term remains that cannot be rewritten using u, the transformed integral is not ready for integration. The chapter's example with numerator 2x+6 and denominator x^2+4x illustrates this problem: choosing u as the denominator leaves a numerator that does not match du. Do not silently replace the unmatched term or proceed as if it were constant. Consider algebraic rearrangement or another integration technique, and verify that the alternative is valid.
Study instruction:
- "Take an incomplete substitution and mark every factor that remains in x; explain what additional identity or method would be required to proceed."
Key point:
- A substitution is complete only when no unconverted variable remains.
- Do not force an invalid change of variable to fit a memorized pattern.
Source links:
-
37. Q: How does integration convert a business or economic rate into a cumulative total?
A: Show answer
If a rate R(t) describes the amount of a quantity generated, sold, depleted, depreciated, or accumulated per unit of time, the cumulative quantity is obtained by integrating that rate with respect to time. In symbols, if Q'(t)=R(t), then Q(t) is an antiderivative of R(t). For a total accumulated between times a and b, integrate the rate over that interval. The units change accordingly: units per month integrated over months gives units, and dollars per year integrated over years gives dollars. The model must distinguish a rate from a total; adding or reporting a rate as though it were a cumulative amount is a category error.
Study instruction:
- "For each business example, label the rate units, time units, and cumulative units before writing the integral."
Key point:
- Integration accumulates a rate over time.
- The units of the total must be consistent with the rate and time interval.
Source links:
-
38. Q: How is the constant of integration determined when a cumulative business function is required?
A: Show answer
First integrate the rate to obtain a general antiderivative containing C. Then use a known starting value or other initial condition to solve for C. For example, if a total-sales function is zero at time zero, substitute t=0 into the general expression and set the result equal to zero. This determines the particular cumulative function that matches the business situation. Without an initial condition, the rate determines changes in the cumulative quantity but not its absolute starting level. The chapter repeatedly uses conditions such as no sales or no subscribers at the beginning of a period to identify the constant.
Study instruction:
- "Integrate a rate, apply a stated initial total, and show how the initial condition selects one function from the family of antiderivatives."
Key point:
- A rate determines the change in a total, not its initial level.
- Initial conditions identify the correct constant for the model.
Source links:
-
39. Q: How is a total-sales function derived when monthly sales are modeled by 1000 minus 750 e to the power of negative 0.1t?
A: Show answer
Treat the expression as the rate of sales, S'(t)=1000-750e^(-0.1t). Integrate each term separately. The constant term integrates to 1000t. For the exponential term, use u=-0.1t, so its antiderivative contributes 7500e^(-0.1t). Thus S(t)=1000t+7500e^(-0.1t)+C. If sales are assumed to be zero at t=0, then 0=7500+C and C=-7500. The resulting total-sales function is 1000t+7500e^(-0.1t)-7500. The example shows how substitution and an initial condition combine to produce a cumulative business model.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the cumulative sales function to recover the stated sales rate and verify that the total is zero at time zero."
Key point:
- Integrate the rate term by term and use the starting total to find C.
- A cumulative model should satisfy both its rate equation and initial condition.
Source links:
-
40. Q: How can the total number of subscribers be estimated from a monthly subscriber-growth rate?
A: Show answer
The chapter models new subscribers with a rate G(t)=1500(1-e^(-0.3t)), where t is measured in months. Let N(t) be the cumulative number of subscribers, so N'(t)=G(t). Integrating gives N(t)=1500t+5000e^(-0.3t)+C. Assuming there were no subscribers at the start, N(0)=0, so C=-5000. Evaluating at 24 months gives approximately 31,004 subscribers gained over the first two years. The distinction between G(t) and N(t) is important: G is a monthly rate, while N is a cumulative count. The initial condition fixes the baseline.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the integration and evaluate N at 24 months, keeping the time unit in months throughout the calculation."
Key point:
- A growth rate is not the same as the accumulated number of subscribers.
- The initial value determines the constant in the cumulative function.
Source links:
-
41. Q: How is total resource depletion calculated when the depletion rate follows an inverse-square pattern?
A: Show answer
The chapter's resource example uses a rate that decreases with time in the form 500/(1+t)^2 units per year. The cumulative depletion over the first ten years is the definite integral from 0 to 10 of that rate. Set u=1+t, so du=dt and the integral becomes 500 times the integral of u^-2. Its antiderivative is -500/u. Evaluating from u=1 to u=11 gives 500(1-1/11), or 5000/11 units, approximately 454.55. The decreasing rate means each later year contributes less depletion than an earlier year. The calculation is an accumulation over the specified period, not the rate at year ten.
Study instruction:
- "Convert the time limits after substitution and explain why the total is found by integrating the rate rather than evaluating it at the final year."
Key point:
- A decreasing rate can still produce an increasing cumulative total.
- Use the definite integral to accumulate the rate over the whole period.

42. Q: How can a rational depreciation rate be integrated to find total depreciation over time?
A: Show answer
The chapter considers a depreciation accumulation involving 10000 divided by t^2+10t+25. Factor the denominator as (t+5)^2. A direct substitution u=t+5 converts the integral to 10000 times the integral of u^-2 du. The antiderivative is -10000/u. Evaluating from zero to T gives total depreciation D(T)=2000-10000/(T+5). The factorization is important because it reveals a simple repeated linear expression; in this particular example, substitution is sufficient and a full partial-fraction decomposition is unnecessary. The result starts at zero when T=0 and increases toward a limiting amount as T grows.
Study instruction:
- "Factor the quadratic denominator, integrate using substitution, and check both D(0) and the direction of change as T increases."
Key point:
- Factorization may reveal that substitution is simpler than partial fractions.
- A cumulative depreciation function should agree with the initial asset condition and the rate model.

43. Q: How does integration by parts calculate depreciation when the depreciation rate is t times an exponential function?
A: Show answer
The machine example uses a depreciation rate r(t)=t e^(0.1t), measured in thousands of dollars per year. To accumulate depreciation from year zero to year three, integrate t e^(0.1t) over that interval. Choose f=t and g'=e^(0.1t), giving f'=1 and g=10e^(0.1t). Integration by parts produces 10t e^(0.1t)-100e^(0.1t). Evaluating from zero to three gives 100-70e^(0.3), approximately 5.50988 thousand dollars, or about $5,509.88. The result is an accumulated amount over three years, not the instantaneous depreciation rate at year three.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the definite integral and convert the result from thousands of dollars to dollars only after completing the integration."
Key point:
- Integration by parts handles a time factor multiplied by an exponential rate.
- Check the scale of the answer when the rate is stated in thousands.

44. Q: How is cumulative production modeled when the production rate is 50t times e to the power of negative 0.2t?
A: Show answer
Let T'(t)=50t e^(-0.2t), where the rate is measured in thousands of units per year. Use integration by parts with f=50t and g'=e^(-0.2t). Then f'=50 and g=-5e^(-0.2t). Applying the formula gives an antiderivative of -250t e^(-0.2t)-1250e^(-0.2t)+C. If production is zero at rollout, T(0)=0 and C=1250. The cumulative production function is therefore 1250-250t e^(-0.2t)-1250e^(-0.2t), in thousands of units. Differentiating it recovers the stated production rate.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the cumulative production function by differentiation and explain why the initial condition determines C=1250."
Key point:
- Integrate the rate to obtain total production and use the rollout baseline.
- Retain the stated scale of thousands of units in the interpretation.

45. Q: How can the remaining value of equipment be derived from an exponential depreciation rate?
A: Show answer
The equipment example starts with an asset value of $120,000 and a depreciation rate of 10,000t e^(-0.1t) dollars per year. The remaining value equals the initial value minus cumulative depreciation. Integrate the rate using parts with f=t and g'=e^(-0.1t), then apply the initial-value condition. The chapter's resulting value function is V(t)=100000t e^(-0.1t)+1000000e^(-0.1t)-880000. At t=0, this equals $120,000, as required. Its derivative is the negative of the depreciation rate, so the value declines at the modeled rate. This distinction between a decreasing value function and a positive depreciation rate is essential.
Study instruction:
- "Check the initial value and differentiate V(t), confirming that V'(t) is the negative of the stated depreciation rate."
Key point:
- Remaining value equals initial value less accumulated depreciation.
- A positive depreciation rate produces a negative rate of change in asset value.

46. Q: How is a cumulative revenue function developed when both selling price and sales volume change over time?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's example, price increases linearly from $20 to $50 over ten years, so p(t)=20+3t. The sales rate is 1000e^(-0.05t) units per year. Revenue generated per year is the product of price and sales rate, so the cumulative revenue function is obtained by integrating 1000(20+3t)e^(-0.05t). This product calls for integration by parts. The chapter derives an antiderivative containing an arbitrary constant and notes that the constant cannot be determined without additional information. If the model explicitly states cumulative revenue is zero at time zero, that condition can determine C; without it, the general family is the correct result.
Study instruction:
- "Write the price, unit-sales rate, and revenue rate separately before integrating, and identify what additional condition would determine the starting total."
Key point:
- Revenue rate is price multiplied by units sold per unit of time.

47. Q: How can a logistic-style technology-adoption rate require substitution followed by partial fractions?
A: Show answer
The chapter models adoption with R(t)=2000/(1+25e^(-0.5t)). Let u=1+25e^(-0.5t). Differentiation gives du/dt=-12.5e^(-0.5t). Since 25e^(-0.5t)=u-1, the time differential can be rewritten as -2du/(u-1). Substituting transforms the integral into -4000 times the integral of 1/[u(u-1)] du. Decompose this rational expression as -1/u+1/(u-1), integrate the logarithms, and substitute back. The initial condition A(0)=0 determines the constant. This example demonstrates why a single integral may need substitution to expose a rational expression and partial fractions to finish it.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the change of variable, derive the partial-fraction coefficients, and apply the zero-at-start condition only after returning to the cumulative function."

- Substitution can reveal a rational integral that is then solved by partial fractions.

48. Q: How is total advertising impact calculated when the impact rate is logarithmic?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a rate r(t)=100 ln(1+0.5t), measured in thousands of units per month. To obtain cumulative impact, integrate this rate. Use integration by parts with f=ln(1+0.5t) and g'=100. The result contains 100t ln(1+0.5t) minus an integral involving 50t/(1+0.5t). That rational expression is simplified by expressing it as a constant plus a reciprocal linear term, after which substitution integrates the logarithmic part. The resulting cumulative function is 100t ln(1+0.5t)-100t+200 ln(1+0.5t)+C. The increase over the first 12 months is about 1,524.27 thousand units.

49. Q: How are accumulated loan-interest amounts found when the interest rate is a rational function?
A: Show answer
The chapter models the interest rate as 3000/(t^2+13t+40), with t measured in months. Factor the denominator as (t+5)(t+8), then write the integrand as A/(t+5)+B/(t+8). Clearing denominators and solving gives A=1000 and B=-1000. Integrating the two reciprocal terms produces logarithms. Applying the lower limit zero and upper limit T gives I(T)=1000 ln(8(T+5)/(5(T+8))). This is the cumulative interest under the stated rate model. The calculation assumes the given rate function and time unit; actual loan interest must also be checked against the loan contract, compounding convention, payment schedule, and applicable fees.

- "Factor the quadratic, verify the partial-fraction identity, and distinguish the mathematical model from contractual interest terms in a real loan."
Key point:

50. Q: How does a cumulative distribution function relate to an income density function?
A: Show answer
If f(x) is a probability density for income and A is the minimum possible income, the cumulative distribution function is F(x)=integral from A to x of f(t) dt. It represents the proportion or probability of individuals whose income is no greater than x, assuming the density is valid and the distribution is normalized. The derivative of F, where it exists, is f. The chapter uses this relationship to express the proportion of a population earning less than a specified amount. The variable t is used inside the integral to distinguish the integration variable from the endpoint x.
Study instruction:
- "Given a density function and its lower support limit, write the corresponding cumulative function and explain its interpretation in words."
Key point:
- Integrating a density from its lower support to x gives the cumulative probability up to x.
- The derivative of the cumulative distribution is the density where differentiable.

51. Q: How is the probability that income lies between two amounts calculated using integration?
A: Show answer
For a continuous income variable X with density f, the probability that a randomly selected individual has income between a and b is the integral of f(t) from a to b. Equivalently, it is F(b)-F(a), the difference between the cumulative probabilities at the upper and lower endpoints. This is the area under the density curve over the interval. The interval must lie within the support of the distribution, and the density must be correctly normalized. In the chapter's examples, substitution simplifies the integrals for particular income-density functions. The method is general even when the density has a different functional form.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate an interval probability both by direct integration and by subtracting cumulative probabilities, then compare the results."
Key point:
- Interval probability is the area under the density between the two income values.
- The CDF difference provides an equivalent calculation.
Source links:
-
52. Q: How is the probability of earning more than a specified income calculated?
A: Show answer
For a valid continuous distribution, the probability that income X is at least c is the integral of f(t) from c to infinity. If F is the cumulative distribution function and total probability is one, this upper-tail probability equals 1-F(c). The chapter presents this relationship as a way to calculate the proportion of individuals earning above a threshold. The complement method can be easier when F(c) is already known or simpler to calculate. Ensure that the upper limit represents the full remaining support and that the density integrates to one over its complete domain.
Study instruction:
- "Solve a threshold-probability problem using both the upper-tail integral and the complement 1-F(c)."
Key point:
- Upper-tail probability is the probability remaining above the threshold.
- The complement formula depends on a properly normalized distribution.
Source links:

53. Q: How is expected income calculated from a probability density function?
A: Show answer
The expected value of a continuous income variable is the integral of x f(x) over the full support of the distribution. It is the probability-weighted average income, not the income of a typical or guaranteed individual. The chapter gives the expression E(X)=integral from the minimum income A to infinity of x f(x) dx. Its example with a shifted exponential density uses integration by parts and substitution to obtain an expected income of $2,500 when the minimum is $500 and the exponential scale is $2,000. The calculation weights each possible income by its density and integrates across all possible values.
Study instruction:
- "Set up the expected-value integral from the full support and identify which factor is x and which is the density before integrating."
Key point:
- Expected value is a weighted average across the distribution.
- It requires the density and its support to be correctly specified.
Source links:
-
54. Q: What checks are necessary before treating a function as a probability density?
A: Show answer
A probability density must be nonnegative over its support and integrate to one over the entire support. The support must be stated correctly, and any piecewise definition must be applied on the appropriate intervals. A formula that produces a plausible interval area is not automatically a valid density if its total integral is not one. The chapter's income examples illustrate the use of integration for interval probabilities, upper-tail probabilities, and expected values; in practice, the normalizing constant and limits must be checked before interpreting the results as probabilities. If the density is truncated at a maximum income, the upper limit is finite; if support is unbounded, the improper integral must converge to one.
Study instruction:
- "Before calculating a probability, verify nonnegativity, support, and total integral, then check that the resulting probability lies between zero and one."
Key point:
- A valid density must be nonnegative and have total area one.
- Incorrect support or normalization makes probability conclusions unreliable.

Table 8
Multivariable Functions and Economic Applications
1. Q: What is a multivariable function and why is it useful in economics?
A: Show answer
A multivariable function has two or more independent inputs and produces an output that depends on their combined values. Economic outcomes commonly depend on several factors at once: demand may depend on price and income, cost may depend on quantities and input prices, and production may depend on labor and capital. A two-variable function z = f(x,y) assigns an output to each admissible pair (x,y). This framework allows analysts to study how one input affects the output while the other inputs are held constant, and how several inputs together influence a business or economic result.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the inputs and output in three economic models and explain why a single-variable model would omit relevant influences."
Key point:
- A multivariable function represents outcomes influenced by several inputs.
- The variables must be interpreted in the context of the model.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
2. Q: How is a multivariable function evaluated at a specified point?
A: Show answer
Substitute the supplied value of each independent variable into the function, preserving brackets and the order of operations. For example, if f(x,y) = 3x - 2y, then f(4,-2) = 12 + 4 = 16. Each input must be assigned to the correct variable. With three variables, all three values must be substituted. The result is a single output value associated with that point, not a derivative or a rate of change.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate several two- and three-variable functions and show each substitution before simplifying."
Key point:
- Every independent variable requires its own supplied value.
- Function evaluation produces an output at a point.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
3. Q: What is the domain of a function of two variables?
A: Show answer
The domain is the set of all ordered pairs (x,y) for which the formula produces a valid real output under the mathematical conventions being used. Restrictions may arise from denominators that cannot be zero, logarithms whose arguments must be positive, and even roots whose radicands must be nonnegative. For example, a square root of x - y requires x - y to be at least zero, so the domain is the region x ≥ y. Identifying the domain is important before evaluating, differentiating, or optimizing the function.
Study instruction:
- "List every algebraic restriction in a multivariable formula and express the resulting domain as conditions on x and y."
Key point:
- The domain is a region of admissible input pairs.
- Denominators, logarithms, and roots create important restrictions.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
4. Q: How can a function of two variables be represented graphically?
A: Show answer
The graph of z = f(x,y) is a surface in three-dimensional coordinate space. Each admissible pair (x,y) determines a height z. Unlike a one-variable function, whose graph is typically a curve in a plane, a two-variable function generally produces a surface. The surface helps visualize how the output changes as both inputs vary. A fixed value of one input produces a cross-section of the surface, while a fixed output produces a level curve in the x-y plane.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a simple surface and explain how changing x and y moves the point across it."
Key point:
- A two-input function is generally represented by a three-dimensional surface.
- Cross-sections and level curves reveal behavior in selected directions.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
5. Q: What does a first-order partial derivative measure?
A: Show answer
A first-order partial derivative measures the instantaneous rate of change of a multivariable function with respect to one input while the other inputs are held constant. For z = f(x,y), the derivative with respect to x measures how z changes as x changes at a fixed y; the derivative with respect to y does the corresponding job at a fixed x. In economics, these derivatives can represent marginal cost, marginal revenue, or marginal product, depending on what the function and inputs represent.
Study instruction:
- "For a cost or production function, state what each partial derivative measures while naming the input held fixed."
Key point:
- A partial derivative isolates the effect of one input.
- All other independent variables are held constant in that derivative.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
6. Q: What do the symbols fx, fy, and the partial-derivative notation mean?
A: Show answer
The symbols fx and ∂f/∂x both denote the first-order partial derivative of f with respect to x. Similarly, fy and ∂f/∂y denote the partial derivative with respect to y. The notation identifies which input is being varied. This is essential because a function of two variables has more than one first-order derivative. A result without a clear variable of differentiation may be ambiguous and can lead to an incorrect economic interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Translate between subscript notation and partial-derivative notation for several functions."
Key point:
- The subscript identifies the variable of differentiation.
- A multivariable function can have several distinct first-order partial derivatives.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
7. Q: How is a partial derivative calculated when the function is a sum of separate terms?
A: Show answer
Differentiate with respect to the selected variable and treat every other independent variable as a constant. For f(x,y) = 2x^3 + 5y^4, the x-partial is 6x^2 because the y term is constant with respect to x. The y-partial is 20y^3 because the x term is constant with respect to y. The ordinary power rule still applies; the key difference is that only one variable is treated as changing at a time.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate both partial derivatives of a sum and explicitly state why the unrelated term differentiates to zero."
Key point:
- Use ordinary differentiation rules on the selected variable.
- Terms containing only held-constant variables differentiate to zero.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
8. Q: How are partial derivatives calculated for a product such as 5x²y³?
A: Show answer
When differentiating 5x²y³ with respect to x, treat y³ as a constant multiplier and obtain 10xy³. When differentiating with respect to y, treat 5x² as constant and obtain 15x²y². The same principle applies to any monomial: retain the factors that do not depend on the selected variable, and apply the power rule to the factor that does. This method is a foundation for more complex product-rule problems.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a monomial with respect to each variable and compare the two results."
Key point:
- The other variable's factor remains in the derivative.
- Apply the power rule only to the variable being differentiated.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
9. Q: How does the multivariable chain rule work?
A: Show answer
If f depends on an intermediate expression u(x,y), then the partial derivative with respect to x is df/du multiplied by ∂u/∂x, and the partial derivative with respect to y is df/du multiplied by ∂u/∂y. First differentiate the outer function with respect to u, then multiply by the derivative of the inside expression with respect to the chosen variable. For example, for f(x,y) = (x³ - y²)^4, the outer derivative is 4u³ and the inside derivatives are 3x² and -2y.
Study instruction:
- "Use a temporary variable u for a composite function and calculate both partial derivatives separately."
Key point:
- The chain rule multiplies the outer derivative by the relevant inner partial.
- The inner derivative differs according to the selected variable.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
10. Q: How can the chain rule be applied to an exponential function of two variables?
A: Show answer
For a function such as f(x,y) = e^(x² - y²), identify u = x² - y². The derivative of e^u with respect to u is e^u. Multiply it by ∂u/∂x = 2x to obtain fx = 2x e^(x² - y²), and by ∂u/∂y = -2y to obtain fy = -2y e^(x² - y²). The sign in the y derivative is important because the inner expression contains -y².
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a two-variable exponential and check the sign of each inner partial derivative."
Key point:
- The exponential remains unchanged when differentiated with respect to its exponent.
- The inner partial derivative supplies the variable-specific factor.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
11. Q: How does the chain rule apply to a logarithm of a multivariable expression?
A: Show answer
For f(x,y) = 3 ln(2x^5y^4), set u = 2x^5y^4. The outer derivative is 3/u. Multiplying by ∂u/∂x = 10x^4y^4 gives fx = 15/x, while multiplying by ∂u/∂y = 8x^5y^3 gives fy = 12/y, within the domain where the logarithm is defined. This illustrates how the chain rule can simplify after common factors cancel.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a logarithm of a product and state the domain required for its argument."
Key point:
- Differentiate the logarithm's argument and divide by that argument.
- Domain restrictions remain relevant after simplification.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
12. Q: How is the multivariable product rule applied?
A: Show answer
If f(x,y) = u(x,y)v(x,y), then fx = ux v + u vx and fy = uy v + u vy. Differentiate each factor with respect to the selected variable while treating the other independent variable as fixed. The structure is the familiar one-variable product rule, applied separately in each direction. It is often clearer to label the two factors first, calculate all four partial derivatives, and then assemble the x and y results.
Study instruction:
- "Label the two factors and calculate ux, vx, uy, and vy before applying the product rule."
Key point:
- Differentiate both factors in turn for each partial derivative.
- Do not omit the cross-products in the product rule.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
13. Q: When is the multivariable quotient rule needed?
A: Show answer
When a function is the quotient u(x,y)/v(x,y), differentiate it with respect to the selected variable using (v times the partial of u minus u times the partial of v) divided by v squared. Apply the formula once for x and again for y. The denominator must be nonzero, and the numerator and denominator partial derivatives must be calculated with the other variable held constant. Factoring common terms after applying the rule can make a complicated expression easier to interpret.
Study instruction:
- "Identify numerator and denominator functions and apply the quotient rule separately for x and y."
Key point:
- The denominator is squared in the quotient rule.
- The order of the two numerator terms matters.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
14. Q: How should domain restrictions be handled when differentiating multivariable functions?
A: Show answer
Differentiation does not remove restrictions in the original function. A quotient remains undefined where its denominator is zero, and a logarithm remains restricted to points where its argument is positive. A simplified derivative may appear to be defined at additional points, but those points are not automatically restored to the domain of the original function. When interpreting derivatives, state the domain inherited from the original model and avoid treating algebraic cancellation as permission to evaluate where the original function was undefined.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the domain of an original quotient or logarithm with the apparent domain of its simplified derivative."
Key point:
- The derivative inherits restrictions from the original function.
- Algebraic simplification does not automatically extend the original domain.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
15. Q: What are the four second-order partial derivatives of a function of two variables?
A: Show answer
For f(x,y), differentiating fx again with respect to x gives fxx, and differentiating fx with respect to y gives fyx. Differentiating fy with respect to x gives fxy, while differentiating fy with respect to y gives fyy. These describe how the first-order marginal effects themselves change as the inputs change. They are useful in curvature analysis and in classifying local maxima, minima, and saddle points.
Study instruction:
- "Construct all four second partial derivatives from the two first partial derivatives of a polynomial."
Key point:
- There are two choices of second differentiation for each first partial.
- Second partials describe changes in marginal effects.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
16. Q: What is the equality of mixed partial derivatives?
A: Show answer
Under suitable smoothness conditions, particularly when the relevant mixed second partial derivatives are continuous near the point, the order of differentiation does not matter: fxy = fyx. This result is commonly called equality of mixed partials or Young's theorem. It allows a mixed derivative calculated by differentiating first with respect to x and then y to be checked against the reverse order. The continuity condition matters; equality should not be asserted without the theorem's regularity assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate fxy and fyx for a smooth polynomial and verify that they agree."
Key point:
- Continuous mixed second partials are equal in either order.
- The equality relies on appropriate regularity conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
17. Q: How are second partial derivatives calculated for a polynomial?
A: Show answer
First calculate fx and fy. Then differentiate each of those results with respect to x and y to obtain fxx, fyx, fxy, and fyy. For example, if f(x,y) = 4x^7y^5, then fx = 28x^6y^5 and fy = 20x^7y^4. Differentiating again gives fxx = 168x^5y^5, fyx = 140x^6y^4, fxy = 140x^6y^4, and fyy = 80x^7y^3.
Study instruction:
- "Work from the first partial derivatives rather than trying to take second derivatives directly from the original expression."
Key point:
- Second partials are derivatives of first partials.
- The mixed derivatives provide a useful calculation check.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
18. Q: Why can second-order partial differentiation require the chain or quotient rule?
A: Show answer
The first partial derivative may itself be a composite expression or a quotient. Differentiating it again therefore requires the appropriate one-variable rule while the other variable remains fixed. For example, differentiating a logarithm of a two-variable expression may first produce a quotient; a second derivative then requires the quotient rule. The correct process is sequential: calculate the first partial carefully, preserve its algebraic structure, and then differentiate that result with respect to the requested variable.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a composite function from the chapter and show which rule is needed at each differentiation stage."
Key point:
- Second differentiation can require the same rules as first differentiation.
- Keep the held-constant variable consistent at each stage.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
19. Q: What is a total differential for a function of one variable?
A: Show answer
For y = f(x), the total differential is dy = f'(x) dx. It links a small change in the input to the corresponding differential change in the output. When dx is approximated by a small finite change Δx, the resulting output change can be estimated by Δy ≈ f'(x)Δx. The derivative is evaluated at the starting point. This approximation is local, so it is most reliable when the change in x is small and the function is sufficiently smooth near that point.
Study instruction:
- "Use a derivative at a starting value to estimate the output change caused by a small input change."
Key point:
- The differential is derivative multiplied by the input differential.
- The approximation is local and first-order.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
20. Q: How is a two-variable total differential constructed?
A: Show answer
For z = f(x,y), the total differential is dz = fx dx + fy dy. Each partial derivative measures the local sensitivity to one input, and its product with that input's small change gives that input's first-order contribution to the output change. Replacing dx and dy with small finite changes gives Δz ≈ fx Δx + fy Δy. The partial derivatives are evaluated at the starting point. The approximation combines the effects of both changing inputs.
Study instruction:
- "Write the total differential for a two-input function and identify the contribution from each input."
Key point:
- The total differential adds the first-order effects of all changing inputs.
- Evaluate both partial derivatives at the same starting point.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
21. Q: Why is a total differential an approximation rather than an exact finite-change formula?
A: Show answer
The total differential uses the tangent-plane, first-order part of the function's local change. For sufficiently small input changes, the omitted higher-order terms are usually small. For larger changes, curvature and interactions between variables may make the actual change differ materially from the approximation. The differential is therefore useful for quick sensitivity analysis, but an exact finite change should be calculated by evaluating f at the new point and subtracting f at the original point when the full function is available.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a total-differential estimate with the exact change for a chosen finite input movement."
Key point:
- A total differential omits higher-order effects.
- Larger changes may require direct function evaluation.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
22. Q: How can a total differential estimate the effect of percentage changes in two inputs?
A: Show answer
Convert each percentage change into an absolute change using the current input value. If x increases by 4 percent, use Δx = 0.04x; if y decreases by 2 percent, use Δy = -0.02y. Substitute these changes into Δz ≈ fxΔx + fyΔy. Simplify to see how the percentage movements combine. The result depends on the function's sensitivities at the starting point, so the same percentage changes can produce different output effects at different operating levels.
Study instruction:
- "Translate percentage changes into absolute changes before applying the total differential."
Key point:
- A percentage change must be multiplied by the current input.
- Sensitivity depends on the starting point.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
23. Q: How does a one-variable revenue derivative help assess a price change?
A: Show answer
If revenue is modeled as R(x) = 100x - 0.5x², where x is the unit price, then R'(x) = 100 - x measures the local change in revenue per unit change in price. At a price of 50, a one-dollar increase produces an estimated revenue increase of about 50, using ΔR ≈ R'(50)Δx. The derivative is a marginal sensitivity, not a statement that every price increase always raises revenue. Its sign changes with the operating point.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the revenue derivative at several prices and interpret its positive, zero, or negative sign."
Key point:
- The derivative measures local revenue sensitivity to price.
- The sign depends on the current price.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
24. Q: What does a zero first derivative imply for a small price adjustment in a revenue model?
A: Show answer
If R'(x) = 0 at the current price, the first-order total-differential estimate of a small revenue change is zero. In the chapter's smartphone example, R(x) = 150x - x² has derivative 150 - 2x, which is zero at x = 75. A small price change therefore has no first-order estimated revenue effect at that point. This does not mean every finite price change leaves revenue exactly unchanged; curvature and second-order effects may still change the actual result.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a zero marginal estimate from the exact revenue difference after a finite price change."
Key point:
- A zero derivative means zero first-order change at that point.
- Finite changes can still have second-order effects.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
25. Q: How can revenue depending on demand and price be modeled?
A: Show answer
If demand is D units and price is P per unit, revenue can be modeled as R(D,P) = DP. Its partial derivatives are R_D = P and R_P = D. The first measures the revenue contribution of an additional unit of demand while price is held fixed; the second measures the local revenue contribution of a one-unit price increase while demand is held fixed. A total differential combines both effects when demand and price change together.
Study instruction:
- "Derive both partial derivatives of R = DP and explain their units and economic meanings."
Key point:
- Revenue is the product of unit price and quantity sold.
- Each partial derivative holds the other revenue driver fixed.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
26. Q: How is the approximate revenue change calculated when both demand and price increase?
A: Show answer
For R(D,P) = DP, the total differential is ΔR ≈ PΔD + DΔP. If demand is 10,000 units at $50 and demand rises by 5 percent while price rises by 2 percent, then ΔD = 500 and ΔP = $1. The estimated change is 50(500) + 10,000(1) = $35,000, producing an estimated revenue of $535,000 from the $500,000 starting level. This is a first-order estimate; the exact new revenue also includes the interaction term ΔDΔP.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the numerical example and compare the differential estimate with the exact product at the new demand and price."
Key point:
- The differential adds the separate demand and price effects.
- The exact product includes an interaction effect omitted by the first-order estimate.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
27. Q: How can a multivariable cost function estimate the effect of production and material-price changes?
A: Show answer
For a cost function C(Q,M) = aQ + bMQ + c, the partial derivatives are C_Q = a + bM and C_M = bQ. The first measures the marginal cost of an additional unit of output at a fixed material price. The second measures cost sensitivity to a change in material cost at a fixed production quantity. The total differential combines them: ΔC ≈ C_QΔQ + C_MΔM. In the chapter example, this method estimates the cost impact of producing 100 more units while material cost rises by one dollar.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate both partial derivatives, substitute the current operating values, and separate the production and input-price effects."
Key point:
- Production and material-price changes contribute separately to cost.
- The total differential combines the two local effects.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
28. Q: How can a subscription business assess a price increase that may reduce subscribers?
A: Show answer
If subscription revenue is R(P,S) = PS, then R_P = S and R_S = P. A price increase contributes approximately SΔP, while a subscriber loss contributes PΔS. The total estimate is ΔR ≈ SΔP + PΔS. In the chapter example, a $5 increase at 2,000 subscribers combined with a loss of 100 subscribers gives 2,000(5) + 30(-100) = $7,000 estimated additional monthly revenue. The result is a local estimate, and the business should also consider retention, costs, and longer-term demand responses.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the two partial effects separately and explain why the subscriber change is negative."
Key point:
- Price gains and subscriber losses can offset one another.
- Revenue sensitivity does not by itself determine long-term profitability.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
29. Q: What is marginal cost in a two-product business?
A: Show answer
For a cost function C(x,y), the partial derivative Cx measures the marginal cost of producing one additional unit of product X while holding the quantity of product Y constant. Cy measures the corresponding marginal cost for product Y while X is fixed. In the chapter's linear example, C(x,y) = 7x + 9y + 18,000, so Cx = 7 and Cy = 9. At the stated production levels, these values estimate the cost of the next unit of each product under the model.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a two-product cost function and interpret each partial derivative while naming the fixed product quantity."
Key point:
- Each marginal cost is a partial derivative of total cost.
- The other product's quantity is held constant.

30. Q: How is marginal revenue calculated for two products?
A: Show answer
If revenue is R(x,y), the marginal revenue of product X is Rx and the marginal revenue of product Y is Ry. These derivatives measure the local change in total revenue from a small increase in one product's sales while the other product's sales are held fixed. In the chapter's example, Rx = 50 + 0.07y - 0.08x and Ry = 70 + 0.07x - 0.08y. Evaluating them at x = 800 and y = 1,000 gives estimated marginal revenues of 56 and 46 respectively.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the revenue function with respect to each product and evaluate both at the same sales combination."
Key point:
- Marginal revenue is product-specific in a multi-product model.
- Cross-product terms can make one product's marginal revenue depend on the other's sales.

31. Q: How are separate price-demand functions combined into total revenue?
A: Show answer
For two products, total revenue is the sum of each product's price multiplied by its own quantity: R(x,y) = pX(x,y)x + pY(x,y)y. The price-demand functions may depend on both quantities, so each product's price can be influenced by the sales mix. Expand and simplify the expression before differentiating. The resulting revenue function captures own-quantity effects and cross-product interactions, which then influence marginal revenue and profit calculations.
Study instruction:
- "Substitute both price-demand functions into the revenue identity and expand every product term."
Key point:
- Total revenue sums price times quantity for each product.
- Cross-demand effects can make prices depend on both quantities.

32. Q: How is a profit function constructed from multivariable revenue and cost?
A: Show answer
Profit is revenue minus cost, so P(x,y) = R(x,y) - C(x,y). First derive or identify the total revenue function, then subtract the complete cost function using brackets to preserve signs. Simplify like terms before calculating marginal profit. The resulting function expresses profit for each combination of product quantities. It is the appropriate starting point for estimating the profit effect of an additional unit or for finding an unconstrained profit maximum.
Study instruction:
- "Write revenue minus cost with brackets and simplify the result term by term."
Key point:
- Profit equals total revenue less total cost.
- A sign error in subtracting cost changes every later result.

33. Q: What does marginal profit mean for the next unit of a product?
A: Show answer
The partial derivative Px measures the local change in profit from a small increase in product X while product Y is held constant. Evaluating Px at a current production point estimates the contribution of the next unit of X. In the chapter's example, the marginal profit of X at x = 500 and y = 700 is $350. This is a marginal estimate based on the model, not a guarantee that every individual unit will earn exactly that amount or that demand and costs remain unchanged beyond the local point.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the marginal-profit function at a specified sales mix and interpret the next-unit estimate."
Key point:
- Marginal profit is a partial derivative of the profit function.
- It is a local estimate at the stated production combination.

34. Q: What is marginal productivity in a two-input production function?
A: Show answer
If output is z = f(x,y), the partial derivative fx is the marginal productivity of input x, while fy is the marginal productivity of input y. Each measures the local change in output associated with a small increase in one production factor while the other factor is held constant. The inputs might represent labor, capital, land, materials, or machine time. These derivatives help managers analyze the contribution of an additional input at the current operating point.
Study instruction:
- "Label the production inputs and calculate the marginal productivity of each one separately."
Key point:
- Marginal productivity is the partial derivative of output with respect to an input.
- The other production factor is held constant.

35. Q: How are marginal products derived from a Cobb-Douglas production function?
A: Show answer
For the chapter's example f(K,L) = K³L², marginal product of capital is MPK = 3K²L² and marginal product of labor is MPL = 2K³L. Each derivative treats the other input as constant. The formulas show how the marginal contribution of one factor depends on the current levels of both capital and labor. The chapter introduces this function as a Cobb-Douglas example and indicates that it is developed further later; the immediate task is to calculate and interpret the partial derivatives.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate the production function once with respect to capital and once with respect to labor."
Key point:
- Each marginal product is a first-order partial derivative.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
36. Q: What is a local maximum of a function of two variables?
A: Show answer
A function has a local maximum at (a,b) when its value there is at least as large as the values at nearby points in the domain. The term local matters: the point need not be the greatest value over the entire domain. Geometrically, the surface is locally higher at the point than around it. For a differentiable interior maximum, the tangent plane is horizontal, so both first-order partial derivatives must be zero. The condition identifies candidates but does not by itself prove a maximum.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish local from global maximum and identify the nearby region used in the definition."
Key point:
- A local maximum is greatest relative to nearby points.
- A stationary point is only a candidate for a maximum.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
37. Q: What is a local minimum of a function of two variables?
A: Show answer
A function has a local minimum at (a,b) when its value there is no greater than the values at nearby points in the domain. The surface forms a local basin around the point. For a differentiable interior minimum, the tangent plane is horizontal and both first-order partial derivatives are zero. As with a maximum, this is a necessary condition for an interior optimum, not a sufficient classification. The second-derivative test is used to distinguish a minimum from other types of critical point.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a surface with a local minimum and explain why both first partial derivatives vanish at an interior smooth minimum."
Key point:
- A local minimum is lowest relative to nearby points.
- Use second-order information to classify the critical point.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
38. Q: Why are both first-order partial derivatives set to zero when finding interior critical points?
A: Show answer
At a smooth interior local maximum or minimum, the surface has no first-order slope in either coordinate direction. Therefore fx = 0 and fy = 0 are necessary conditions for an interior stationary point. Solving the two equations simultaneously gives candidate coordinates. A point satisfying both equations may still be a saddle point, so the second-order test is needed. Boundary optima and constrained problems require separate attention because an optimum can occur where the interior stationary conditions do not apply.
Study instruction:
- "Solve the two first-order equations together and keep the resulting points as candidates until they are classified."
Key point:
- Interior stationary points satisfy both first partials equal to zero.
- The equations generate candidates, not final classifications.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
39. Q: How is the second-derivative determinant used to classify a critical point?
A: Show answer
At a critical point, calculate D = fxx fyy - (fxy)². If D is positive and fxx is positive, the point is a local minimum. If D is positive and fxx is negative, it is a local maximum. If D is negative, the point is a saddle point. If D equals zero, the test is inconclusive. All derivatives must be evaluated at the same critical point, and the test assumes the required differentiability conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate fxx, fyy, fxy, and D at a candidate point before applying the classification rules."
Key point:
- The determinant combines the second-order curvature information.
- The sign of D and fxx determines the standard classification.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
40. Q: What is a saddle point in a function of two variables?
A: Show answer
A saddle point is a critical point that is not a local maximum or minimum. The surface rises in some directions and falls in others, resembling a saddle. In the second-derivative test, a negative determinant D = fxx fyy - (fxy)² identifies a saddle point. The chapter's function 3x² - 4y² - 6x + 1 has a critical point at (1,0) with D negative, demonstrating that setting both first partials to zero does not guarantee an optimum.
Study instruction:
- "Use a surface sketch and the sign of D to distinguish a saddle from a local maximum or minimum."
Key point:
- A saddle point is neither a local maximum nor a local minimum.
- A negative second-derivative determinant identifies a saddle under the test.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
41. Q: What does an inconclusive second-derivative test mean?
A: Show answer
If D = 0 at a critical point, the standard second-derivative test does not determine whether the point is a maximum, minimum, or saddle. It does not mean that no extremum exists. Further analysis is needed, such as examining the function along different paths near the point or using higher-order information. The correct conclusion is that this particular test is inconclusive, not that the point should be assigned an arbitrary classification.
Study instruction:
- "Find a critical point with D equal to zero and describe what additional analysis would be needed."
Key point:
- D = 0 provides no classification from this test.
- An inconclusive result is not proof of the absence of an extremum.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
42. Q: How can a critical point be classified as a minimum in a quadratic example?
A: Show answer
For f(x,y) = 2x² + 3y², the first partial derivatives are fx = 4x and fy = 6y, so the only critical point is (0,0). The second partials are fxx = 4, fyy = 6, and fxy = 0. Therefore D = 4(6) - 0² = 24, which is positive, and fxx is positive. The test classifies the point as a local minimum. Because the quadratic terms are positive, the surface also visibly curves upward in both coordinate directions.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the critical point and determinant, then explain the role of the positive fxx value."
Key point:
- Positive D with positive fxx indicates a local minimum.
- The signs of the second derivatives describe local curvature.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
43. Q: How can a function have more than one critical point?
A: Show answer
The equations fx = 0 and fy = 0 form a system that may have several simultaneous solutions. Each solution is a candidate critical point and must be classified separately because different points can have different local behavior. In the chapter's cubic example, solving the two equations produces two critical points; one is a saddle and the other a local maximum. Do not stop after finding the first solution, and do not assume all critical points share the same classification.
Study instruction:
- "Solve a two-equation critical-point system completely and evaluate the determinant at every solution."
Key point:
- A function may have multiple critical points.
- Classify each point separately using local second-order values.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
44. Q: Why must all second derivatives be evaluated at the same critical point?
A: Show answer
The determinant D uses fxx, fyy, and fxy at one specific coordinate pair. Combining derivatives evaluated at different points would mix unrelated local curvature information and produce a meaningless classification. After solving for critical coordinates, substitute each pair into every required second partial. If there are multiple critical points, repeat the calculation for each one and keep the results clearly separated.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a small evaluation table with one row per critical point and columns for fxx, fyy, fxy, and D."
Key point:
- The second-derivative test is local to one coordinate pair.
- Never mix derivative values from different critical points.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
45. Q: What is the limitation of the chapter's extrema test for constrained economic problems?
A: Show answer
The chapter's test identifies interior critical points of an unconstrained function of two variables. Economic decisions often involve constraints such as limited budgets, capacity, labor availability, or nonnegative production. A constrained optimum may occur on a boundary or along a restriction and therefore may not satisfy the unconstrained equations fx = 0 and fy = 0. Such problems require methods that explicitly incorporate the constraints. The chapter introduces the relevance of constrained optimization but does not develop a full constrained-optimization procedure in this section.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the feasible region in a business optimization problem and decide whether an optimum could occur on its boundary."
Key point:
- The unconstrained test does not solve every constrained problem.
- Boundary and constraint conditions require additional methods.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
46. Q: How do partial derivatives support economic sensitivity analysis?
A: Show answer
Economic variables often respond to several drivers at once. A partial derivative measures the local sensitivity of an outcome to one driver while other modeled drivers are fixed. For example, revenue may depend on price and demand, while cost may depend on output and material prices. Comparing the size and sign of partial derivatives helps identify which inputs have the strongest local effects and whether an increase in an input raises or lowers the modeled outcome. The interpretation must remain tied to the units and assumptions of the function.
Study instruction:
- "For a selected economic model, rank the input sensitivities and explain the meaning of each derivative's sign and units."
Key point:
- Partial derivatives quantify input-specific sensitivity.
- Economic interpretation depends on units and held-constant assumptions.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
47. Q: How should cross-product terms be interpreted in a multivariable business function?
A: Show answer
A term such as 0.07xy means that the combined effect of x and y contributes to the outcome. Its x-partial is 0.07y, so the marginal effect of x depends on the current value of y. Its y-partial is 0.07x, so the marginal effect of y depends on x. This is why marginal revenue in a multi-product model may change when the sales volume of the other product changes. Cross terms capture interaction between inputs rather than treating their effects as entirely independent.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a cross-product term with respect to both variables and explain how one input changes the other's marginal effect."
Key point:
- Cross terms make marginal effects depend on other inputs.
- The product mix can change the marginal economics of each product.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
48. Q: How can the marginal revenue of a product be interpreted as the next-unit estimate?
A: Show answer
The partial derivative of revenue with respect to a product quantity estimates the change in total revenue from a very small increase in that quantity while other product quantities are fixed. At integer production levels, it is commonly interpreted as an approximation to the revenue associated with the next unit. The estimate is local and depends on the current mix. If the marginal revenue function changes with quantity, the next unit at a different sales level may have a different estimated contribution.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a marginal-revenue function at the current sales point and describe the next-unit interpretation with the other product fixed."
Key point:
- Marginal revenue is a local next-unit estimate.
- The estimate depends on the current product mix.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
49. Q: How should marginal productivity be used in a production decision?
A: Show answer
Marginal productivity estimates the additional output associated with a small increase in one input while other inputs are held fixed. Managers can use it to compare the local contribution of labor, capital, or another production factor. The derivative does not by itself determine whether an input should be purchased: the value of additional output, input cost, capacity, quality, and operational constraints also matter. A positive marginal product indicates that more of the input raises output locally under the model, but the economic decision requires comparing benefits and costs.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate a marginal product and identify the additional economic information needed before recommending more input."
Key point:
- Marginal product measures the local output contribution of an input.
- A production decision also requires input costs and business constraints.

- Chapter 8 source file in Box
50. Q: What is the difference between a marginal effect and a total effect?
A: Show answer
A marginal effect is a local derivative that describes how an output changes for a small movement in one input at a fixed level of the others. A total effect may involve several inputs changing together, or a finite movement large enough that local sensitivities change along the path. The total differential approximates a combined small change by adding the partial-derivative contributions. For larger movements, evaluate the function at both endpoints or use a more complete path analysis rather than treating the initial marginal value as constant.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a partial derivative, a total differential estimate, and an exact finite change for one model."
Key point:
- Marginal effects are local and input-specific.
- A total change can include multiple inputs and nonlinear interactions.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
51. Q: How can the product and quotient rules be organized to reduce errors?
A: Show answer
Before differentiating, rewrite the function as clearly identified factors or as a numerator and denominator. Calculate each factor's partial derivatives separately, keeping the held-constant variable explicit. Apply the product or quotient formula only after those components are correct. Simplify at the end rather than combining many algebraic operations prematurely. This approach makes sign errors, missing terms, and incorrect powers easier to detect and allows each component to be checked independently.
Study instruction:
- "Use a structured worksheet for a product and a quotient, listing the component derivatives before assembling the result."
Key point:
- Organizing components first reduces algebraic mistakes.
- Simplification should follow, not replace, correct rule application.
Source links:
- Chapter 8 source file in Box
52. Q: What is a practical workflow for analyzing a two-variable economic function?
A: Show answer
Define the output and both inputs, including their units and economic meaning. Check the domain, evaluate the function at the current operating point, and calculate the first partial derivatives. Interpret each derivative as a marginal effect while holding the other input fixed. If a small simultaneous change is proposed, calculate the total differential. If an optimum is sought, solve the first-order equations and classify each interior critical point with second derivatives. Finally, check whether the model's constraints, assumptions, and domain permit the proposed decision.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this workflow to a cost, revenue, or production function and explain each stage in business terms."
Key point:
- A consistent workflow links calculus to model interpretation.
- Domain, constraints, units, and assumptions remain part of the analysis.
